In the 1-categorical case one can even restrict the
\coprod_{i_{0}\to i_{1}}
to a generating set of morphisms.

2.2
Commutative diagrams and correspondences

We are often in need to show commutativity of a diagram of 1-morphisms and 2-morphisms in a (2,1)-category
12
12

12

The section has an analogue for
(\infty,2)
-categories, that we will not mention because it is not needed in these lectures.

\mathcal{C}
(in the examples:
\Cat
or
\Cat^{\PF}
)
in which the 1-morphisms are compositions of a 1-morphism and the adjoint of another.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
X
and
Y
be objects. We define the 2-category

\Cor(X,Y)

with objects diagrams

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}

in which
f
has a right adjoint
{}^{t}\!f
. 1-Morphisms
h:(f,g)\to(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})
and 2-Morphisms
h\Rightarrow h^{\prime}
are 2-commutative diagrams

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Rightarrow}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Rightarrow}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\Rightarrow}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Rightarrow}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Rightarrow}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}

Lemma 2.13
.

There is a 2-functor

\displaystyle\can:\Cor(X,Y)

\displaystyle\to\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y),

\displaystyle(f,g)

\displaystyle\mapsto{}^{t}\!f\circ g,

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\mapsto\left({}^{t}\!f\circ g\to{}^{t}\!f\circ{}^{t}\!h\circ h\circ g\to{}^{t}\!f^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime}\right)\qquad\text{(unit)}.

In particular, since the target category is a 1-category, any two 1-morphisms that are connected by a chain of 2-morphisms are mapped to the same morphism
13
13

13

In the analogous (
\infty
,2)-categorical construction,
2-morphisms become isomorphisms.
.

2.14.

A diagram in the image of “
\can
” is called of
standard form
and to
check its commutativity is thus a matter of checking the commutativity of two diagrams involving only the left adjoints.

Of course, there is a dual construction, where
f
is assumed to have a left adjoint, which we leave to the reader to state. If the
f
and
g
are of the form
\phi^{*},\gamma^{*}
and hence
{}^{t}f=\phi_{*}
(or their corresponding 1-morphisms in
\Cat^{\PF}
)
a composition
\Cor(X,Y)\times\Cor(Y,Z)\to\Cor(X,Z)
can be defined, such that “
\can
” is compatible. And there is an operadic version of this, see e.g.
[
35
]
.

2.3
Preliminaries on pre-sheaves

This section contains some basic facts about pre-sheaves. It should be consulted only when needed, except for the following definition:

Definition/Lemma 2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a 1-category and
I
a 1-category.
The category
\mathcal{C}^{I^{\op}}
is enriched in
(\Set^{I^{\op}},\times)
by the formula

\underline{\Hom}(C,D)_{k}:=\int_{i\in I}\Hom(\Hom_{I}(i,k),\Hom(C(i),D(i)).

If
\mathcal{C}
has coproducts then it is left-tensored via

(C\times D)(k)=C(k)\times D(k).

This is called the
canonical enrichment
in pre-sheaves.

2.16.

Consider an adjunction in 2 variables

F:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{E}\qquad G:\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{D}\qquad H:\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{D}^{\op}\to\mathcal{C}.

It yields functors

\mathcal{C}^{I}\times\mathcal{D}^{J}\to\mathcal{E}^{I\times J}\qquad(\mathcal{C}^{I})^{\op}\times\mathcal{E}^{J}\to\mathcal{D}^{I^{\op}\times J}\qquad\mathcal{E}^{I}\times(\mathcal{D}^{J})^{\op}\to\mathcal{C}^{I\times J^{\op}}\

by applying
F,G,H
point-wise.
Assuming that suitable Kan extensions exist, we also have again an adjunction in 2 variables

\mathcal{C}^{I}\times\mathcal{D}^{J}\to\mathcal{E}^{I\times J}\qquad(\mathcal{C}^{I})^{\op}\times\mathcal{E}^{I\times J}\to\mathcal{D}^{J}\qquad\mathcal{E}^{I\times J}\times(\mathcal{D}^{J})^{\op}\to\mathcal{C}^{I}

where, for instance, the second functor is the composition

(\mathcal{C}^{I})^{\op}\times\mathcal{E}^{I\times J}\to\mathcal{D}^{I^{\op}\times I\times J}\to\mathcal{D}^{J}

in which the second functor is
R_{\mathcal{C}}
applied to the product of
\id_{J}
with the pro-functor
I^{\op}\times I\to\cdot
given by
\Hom_{I}(-,-)
which is equivalently
r\,{}^{t}\!l
for

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{I^{\op}\times I}
\textstyle{\cdot}

In fact,
R_{\mathcal{C}}(r\,{}^{t}\!l)=\int_{I}
(the end). (Pre-)composing with the diagonal and its adjoint, we get in particular an adjunction in two variables:

F:\mathcal{C}^{I}\times\mathcal{D}^{I}\to\mathcal{E}^{I}\quad G:(\mathcal{C}^{I})^{\op}\times\mathcal{E}^{I}\to\mathcal{D}^{I}\quad H:\mathcal{E}^{I}\times(\mathcal{D}^{I})^{\op}\to\mathcal{C}^{I}.

All this is nicely explained by the fact that the functor
\op:I\to I^{\op}
(
2.4
) can be seen as the internal Hom
\Hom_{\Cat^{\PF}}(-,\cdot)
(resp.
\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\PF}}(-,\cdot)
) in pro-functors, the
“evaluation morphism” being
r\,{}^{t}\!l
, but we will not discuss this connection here.

Lemma 2.17
.

1.

Let
\alpha:I\to J
be functor and
F,G,H
an adjunction as above. We have

\alpha^{*}F(-,-)\cong F(\alpha^{*}-,\alpha^{*}-)

(13)

or equivalently

G(-,\alpha_{*})\cong\alpha_{*}G(\alpha^{*}-,-)\qquad H(\alpha_{*}-,-)\cong\alpha_{*}H(-,\alpha^{*}-)

2.

If
\alpha
is fully-faithful, we also have:

\alpha^{*}G(\alpha_{!},\alpha_{*})\cong G(-,-)\qquad\alpha^{*}H(\alpha_{*}-,\alpha_{!}-)\cong H(-,-)

3.

For cofibrations
\alpha:I\to J
the mates of (
13
)

\alpha_{!}F(-,\alpha^{*}-)=F(\alpha_{!},-)\quad\alpha_{!}F(\alpha^{*}-,-)=F(-,\alpha_{!}-)

are isomorphisms as well, or equivalently:

G(\alpha_{!},-)\cong\alpha_{*}G(-,\alpha^{*}-)\qquad\alpha^{*}G(-,-)\cong G(\alpha^{*}-,\alpha^{*})

H(-,\alpha_{!})\cong\alpha_{*}H(\alpha^{*}-,-)\qquad\alpha^{*}H(-,-)\cong H(\alpha^{*}-,\alpha^{*})

Proof.

1. is clear, 2. follows by observing that the assumption implies that
\alpha_{*}
and
\alpha_{!}
are fully-faithful, and 3. follows because the left Kan extension along cofibrations is computed fiber-wise and
F
, being a left adjoint when regarded as a functor of any of the two variables, commutes with colimits.
The other morphisms are the formal adjoints of the morphisms involving
F
.
∎

2.18.

For example:
For a complete and cocomplete 1-category
\mathcal{C}
, we have the (co)tensoring adjunction

\times:\Set\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\qquad\Hom_{l}:\Set^{\op}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\qquad\Hom_{r}=\Hom:\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C}\to\Set

which induces as in
2.16
an adjunction on pre-sheaves

\times:\Set^{I^{\op}}\times\mathcal{C}^{I^{\op}}\to\mathcal{C}^{I^{\op}}\quad\Hom_{l}:(\Set^{I^{\op}})^{\op}\times\mathcal{C}^{I^{\op}}\to\mathcal{C}^{I^{\op}}\quad\Hom_{r}:(\mathcal{C}^{I^{\op}})^{\op}\times\mathcal{C}^{I^{\op}}\to\Set^{I^{\op}}

with formula (which can be extracted from the discussion in
2.16
):

\displaystyle(C\times D)(k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C(k)\times D(k)

\displaystyle\Hom_{r}(C,D)(k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{i\in I}\Hom(\Hom_{I}(i,k),\Hom(C(i),D(i))

The formula for
\Hom_{r}
is valid even without any assumption on
\mathcal{C}
and turns
\mathcal{C}^{I^{\op}}
into a
(\Set^{I^{\op}},\times)
-enriched category. This gives
2.15
.

2.4
(Co)operads

2.19.

In these notes we adopt a very flexible notion of
\infty
-operad. However, the purpose is clearly to discuss either
\infty
-(co)operads
or
planar
\infty
-(co)operads in the sense of Lurie.
Planar (co)operads (Definition
2.21
) are combinatorially very pleasant to discuss the classical bar and cobar constructions, whereas more general
\infty
-operads will be important, for instance, for generalizations to (co)algebras over the
\mathbb{E}_{k}
(little discs) operads.

For simplicity
, we understand in the sequel for the moment

(
\infty
-)(co)operad = planar (
\infty
-)(co)operad.

This may change in a future version of these notes.

2.20.

Let
\Delta
be the simplex category. We adopt the convention that its objects are precisely the ordinals
[n]=\{0,\dots,n\}
. (One can include all finite non-empty ordinals, obtaining an equivalent category, of course).
Define
\Delta_{\emptyset}
be the
category of the
[n]
, including
[-1]:=\emptyset
.
Call a morphism in
\Delta
of the form
i<i+1<\dots<i+k\hookrightarrow 0<\dots<n

inert
and a morphism
a:[n]\to[m]

active
, if

a(0)=0
and
a(n)=m
. Every morphism factors uniquely as an active morphism followed by an inert one. A morphism also factors
uniquely into a surjective map (called a
degeneracy
) followed by an injective map (called
face
map).
Call the subcategory of active morphism
\Delta_{\act}
. This is sometimes called the category of
finite intervals
. Both
\Delta_{\emptyset}
and
\Delta_{\act}
are symmetric monoidal
with products
\ast
and
\ast^{\prime}
, given by

[n]\ast[m]:=[n+m+1]

concatenation and

[n]\ast^{\prime}[m]:=[n+m]

concatenation with identification of the extremal points. The units being
\emptyset
, and
[0]
, respectively.
The restriction of
\ast
to
\Delta_{\act}
comes equipped with
a natural transformation

s_{\can}:\ast\Rightarrow\ast^{\prime}

(14)

the
canonical
degeneracy, identifying the extremal points.

Recall that

Definition 2.21
.

A
planar (
\infty
-)operad
is an (
\infty
-)category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a functor
p:\mathcal{C}\to\Delta^{\op}
such that

1.

coCartesian morphisms over inert morphisms exist,

2.

for the standard family
\alpha_{i}:[n]\hookleftarrow[1]
of inert morphisms
14
14

14

given by the inclusions
\alpha_{i}:\{i<i+1\}\hookrightarrow[n]
, the morphism (choosing push-forward functors along these inert ones)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{[n]}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\prod\mathcal{C}_{[1]}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})

is an isomorphism such that it induces (via composition with the corresponding coCartesian morphisms
X\to X_{i}
) an isomorphism

\Hom_{f}(Z,X)\cong\Hom_{f_{1}}(Z,X_{1})\times\cdots\times\Hom_{f_{n}}(Z,X_{n})

for all objects
X,Z\in\mathcal{C}
and
f:p(Z)\to p(X)
.

We denote by
\mathrm{(co)Op}
(resp.
\mathrm{(co)Op}_{\infty}
) the (1,2)-category (resp.
(\infty,2)
-category) of planar (co)operads.
We denote by
\mathcal{O}
the associative planar operad, i.e. the category
\Delta^{\op}
equipped with the identity, considered as operad.

A planar (
\infty
-)cooperad is an (
\infty
-)category equipped with a functor
p:\mathcal{C}\to\Delta
such that
\mathcal{C}^{\op},p^{\op}
is a planar (
\infty
-)operad.

2.22.

The morphisms over the unique active morphism
[n]\to[1]
may, via the identification
\mathcal{C}_{[n]}\cong\prod\mathcal{C}_{[1]}
, be seen as
as sets (resp.
\infty
-groupoids) of multi-morphims

\Hom(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y)

for all
X_{1},\dots,X_{n},Y\in\mathcal{C}_{[1]}
that are composed as in a multi-category. In fact a planar 1-operad is the same as a non-symmetric multi-category and a planar
\infty
-operad is a non-symmetric multi-category
which is “weakly enriched in
\infty
-groupoids” in the same way that an
\infty
-category is a usual category “weakly enriched in
\infty
-groupoids”.

2.23.

Already,
in general statements
, only the following facts about operads will be used, which are true for
\infty
-operads and planar
\infty
-operads alike, and even in generalizations of these concepts:

1.

There is an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{O}
(in both cases we are interested in, in fact, a 1-category) with a unique factorization system in the sense of
[
40
, 5.2.8.8]
into
inert
and
active
morphisms.

2.

An operad
X
is an
\infty
-category with a functor
X\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
satisfying certain conditions (as in Definition
2.21
, for instance) including the existence of coCartesian morphisms over inert morphisms.
Such morphisms are again called inert in
X
and arbitrary morphisms lying over active ones in
\mathcal{O}
are called active. They form thus again a unique factorization system in
X

[
41
, Proposition 2.1.2.5]
.

3.

A functor of operads
X\to Y
is a functor of
\infty
-categories over
\mathcal{O}
which maps inert morphisms to inert morphisms.
It is called a cofibration of operads if it is, in addition, a cofibration of
\infty
-categories.

4.

There is an operad
\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
with functor
\times:\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}\to\Cat_{\infty}
of
\infty
-categories such that a cofibration
X\to I
, where
X
is an arbitrary
\infty
-category and
I
an operad, is a cofibration of operads if and only if
the straighening
I\to\Cat_{\infty}
classifying
X\to I
factorizes into

I\to\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}\to\Cat_{\infty}.

and the functor
I\to\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}
is a functor of operads.
In that case, it factors in an essentially unique way. In other words,
\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}
(as operad) classifies cofibrations of operads.

5.

For two morphisms between cofibrations of operads classified by
F,G:I\to\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}
with compositions with
\times
denoted
F^{\times}
and
G^{\times}

we have
15
15

15

where the first equivalence is induced by the usual one for cofibrations of
\infty
-categories, cf. Proposition
2.54
below.

\Hom_{\Op_{\infty}/M}(\int F^{\times},\int G^{\times})\cong\Hom^{\lax,\inert-\pseudo}_{\Cat^{I}}(F^{\times},G^{\times})\cong\Hom^{\lax,\inert-\pseudo}_{(\Cat_{\infty}^{\times})^{I}}(F,G)

for
\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}
is considered as
(\infty,2)
-category in the obvious way.

6.

For an operad
I
the functor

i\mapsto I_{\act}\times_{/I_{\act}}i

(comma category) which, by the unique factorization, extends to a functor
I\to\Cat_{\infty}
factors through a morphism of operads
I\to\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}
.

2.5
(Co)fibrations, exponential fibrations, and Day convolutions

2.24.

Recall the definition of fibration and cofibration for a functor
F:J\to I
. The definition can be split up into two conditions of which one is shared by both of them.
Functors satisfying only the shared condition are a very convenient class. First observe that
any
functor
F:J\to I
defines for every morphism
\alpha:i\to j
a pro-functor
\alpha^{\bullet}:J_{j}\to J_{i}

between the fibers given by

\alpha^{\bullet}:J_{i}^{\op}\times J_{j}\to\Set\qquad(\text{resp.{}}\ \alpha^{\bullet}:J_{i}^{\op}\times J_{j}\to\Gpd_{\infty})

x\times y\mapsto\Hom_{J}(x,y)

where
J_{i}
is the fiber of
F
over
i
.
In fact, there is an equivalence of categories between pro-functors from
X
to
Y
and the category of functors
\mathcal{C}\to[1]
with fibers
Y
and
X
(this is a 1-category (resp.
(\infty,1)
-category) because the fibers are fixed).

For composable morphisms
\alpha
and
\beta
, composition in
J
yields a morphism:

\alpha^{\bullet}\ \beta^{\bullet}\Rightarrow(\beta\alpha)^{\bullet}.

(15)

(One could say that
F
yields a
lax functor

I^{\op}\to\Cat^{\PF}
(resp.
I^{\op}\to\Cat_{\infty}^{\PF}
) but we will not use this unless it is an actual functor).
We consider the following conditions:

Definition 2.25
.

1.

F
is called
locally coCartesian
(resp.
locally Cartesian
), if for all
\alpha
, the pro-functor
\alpha^{\bullet}
is of the form
{}^{t}\!\beta
(resp.
\beta
) for a functor
\beta:J_{i}\to J_{j}
(resp.
\beta:J_{j}\to J_{i}
).

2.

F
is called an
exponential fibration
(resp.
an
\infty
-exponential fibration
), if (
15
) is always an isomorphism.

3.

F
is called a
cofibration
(resp. a
fibration
) if it satisfies condition 2. and the corresponding version of 1.

One does not have to distinguish between fibrations and
\infty
-fibrations for a functor
I\to J
of 1-categories because here fibration already implies
\infty
-fibration. Similarly for cofibrations.

Exponential fibrations (resp.
\infty
-exponential fibrations) without necessarily satisfying a version of condition 1. are thus a natural generalization of both cofibrations and fibrations and they are classified by functors
I\to\Cat^{\PF}
(resp.
I\to\Cat_{\infty}^{\PF}
).
Many important constructions that work for cofibrations and fibrations alike have generalizations to exponential fibrations. The first is the internal Hom in categories over
I
. Its existence is in fact equivalent to being
“exponential fibration” (cf.
[
6
]
):

Proposition 2.26
.

F
is an exponential fibration (resp.
\infty
-exponential fibration) if and only if the functor

\mathcal{C}\mapsto J\times_{I}\mathcal{C}

has a right 2-adjoint in
\Cat_{/I}
(resp.
\Cat_{\infty}{/I}
)

\mathcal{C}\mapsto D_{I}(J,\mathcal{C}).

In particular, sections of
D_{I}(J,\mathcal{C})\to I
are equal to
\Hom_{I}(J,\mathcal{C})
.

The objects and morphisms in the category
D_{I}(J,\mathcal{C})
can be explicitly described: Objects are pairs of an object
i\in I
and a functor

X\in(\mathcal{C}_{i})^{J_{i}}
and morphisms
(X,i)\to(Y,i^{\prime})
are morphisms
\alpha:i\to i^{\prime}
in
I
together with an element in

\displaystyle\Hom_{\Cat^{\PF}}(\alpha^{\bullet}_{I},(X,Y)^{*}\alpha^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{j,j^{\prime}}\Hom(\alpha^{\bullet}_{I}(j,j^{\prime}),\Hom(X(j),Y(j^{\prime})))

where
\alpha^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C}_{i^{\prime}}
is the pro-functor extracted from
\mathcal{C}
as in
2.24
(
\mathcal{C}
does not have to be small to extract it).
One sees immediately that those can be composed, if the
\alpha^{\bullet}_{I}
are functorial (and not only laxly functorial). The
\alpha^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}
can be laxly functorial though. Taking this as a definition in the 1-categorical context, one can show that it satisfies the universal property above. For the
\infty
-categorical generalization, see
[
6
]
.

For (co)operads (resp.
\infty
-(co)operads) the same is true mutatis mutandis.
See section
2.4
for a discussion of the definition, and of generalizations and an axiomatization.

Definition 2.27
.

Let
J\to I
be a morphism of (co)operads.

1.

F
is called
locally Cartesian
(resp.
locally coCartesian
), if
\alpha^{\bullet}
is of the form
\beta
(resp.
{}^{t}\,\beta
) for a functor
\beta:J_{i^{\prime}}\to J_{i}
(resp.
\beta:J_{i}\to J_{i^{\prime}}
)
for all active morphisms

\alpha:i\to i^{\prime}
.

2.

F
is called an
exponential fibration
(resp.
\infty
-exponential fibration
), if (
15
) is always an isomorphism
for active
\alpha
and
\beta
.

3.

F
is called a
cofibration
(resp. a
fibration
) if it satisfies conditions 1. and 2.

Remark 2.28
.

Usually, we state the definition of coCartesian for operads (over
I=\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
this is equivalent to being a monoidal category considered as operad) and the definition of Cartesian for cooperads (over
I=\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
it is also equivalent to monoidal category, but considered as cooperad). In these cases, it is equivalent to claim that the whole functor of associated categories (of operators) is a (co)fibration in the usual sense, because the locally (co)Cartesianity for the inert morphisms holds by definition and
the exponential fibration condition follows for compositions involving inert morphisms from the other axioms.
A fibration of operads will also occasionally be considered. Be aware that, in the monoidal case, say, this is different from the existence of a right adjoint w.r.t. to one of the arguments, i.e. the existence of internal Homs.

Warning 2.29
.

For a general exponential fibration of operads according to the above definition, (
15
), in general, does not have to be an isomorphism for compositions involving inert morphisms. This is
related to the fact that
\times
is not a product in
\Cat^{\PF}
and
\cdot
is not a terminal object.

The proposition holds also true for (co)operads:

Proposition 2.30
.

F:J\to I
is an exponential fibration (resp.
\infty
-exponential fibration) if and only if the functor

\mathcal{C}\mapsto J\times_{I}\mathcal{C}

has a right 2-adjoint in
\mathrm{(co)Op}_{/I}
(resp.
\mathrm{(co)Op}_{\infty/I}
)

\mathcal{C}\mapsto D_{I}(J,\mathcal{C}).

In particular, sections of
D_{I}(J,\mathcal{C})\to I
(e.g.
I
-(co)algebras) are equal to
\Hom_{I}(J,\mathcal{C})
.

See e.g.
[
41
, 2.2.6]
.

D_{I}(J,\mathcal{C})
is called the
Day convolution
of the (co)operads.

The objects and morphisms in the (co)operad
D_{I}(J,\mathcal{C})
can be explicitly described in the same way as before.
E.g. in the cooperad case, objects (over
[1]
) are pairs of an object
i\in I_{[1]}
and a functor

X\in(\mathcal{C}_{i})^{J_{i}}
and morphism
(X,i)\to(Y_{1},i_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,(Y_{n},i_{n}^{\prime})
are morphisms
\alpha:i\to i_{1}^{\prime},\dots,i_{n}^{\prime}
in
I
together with an element in

\displaystyle\Hom_{\Cat^{\PF}}(\alpha^{\bullet}_{I},(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{j,j_{1}^{\prime},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime}}\Hom(\alpha^{\bullet}_{I}(j,j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j^{\prime}_{n}),\Hom(X(j);Y(j^{\prime}_{1}),\dots,Y(j^{\prime}_{n})))

where
\alpha^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C}_{i_{1}^{\prime}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{i_{n}^{\prime}}
is the pro-functor extracted from
\mathcal{C}
as in
2.24
(
\mathcal{C}
does not have to be small to extract it).

In the following case, we have more control over the morphism spaces in this (co)operad:

Definition 2.31
.

An arbitrary functor
\mathcal{C}\to I
of
\infty
-categories or
\infty
-cooperads is

1.

L
-admissible
, if the fibers are cocomplete and the canonical morphism

\Hom(\colim_{J}X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})\to\lim_{J^{\op}}\Hom(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})

via composition with the canonical element in
\lim_{J^{\op}}\Hom(X,\colim_{J}X)

is an isomorphism for all
i\in I,J,X\in\mathcal{C}_{i}^{J},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
.

2.

R
-admissible
, if the fibers are complete and for any small category
J

\Hom(X;Y_{1},\dots,\lim_{J}Y_{k},\dots,Y_{n})\to\lim_{J}\Hom(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})

via composition with the canonical element in
\lim_{J}\Hom(\lim_{J}Y_{k},Y_{k})

is an isomorphism for all
X,Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n},i\in I,k,J,Y_{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}^{J}
.

Similarly for
\infty
-operads.

The following is clear from the definition:

Lemma 2.32
.

If
\mathcal{C}\to I
is a functor between
\infty
-categories (not
\infty
-(co)operads) then the
L
-admissibility (resp.
R
-admissibility) amounts to

\mathcal{C}\to I
having (co)complete fibers such that the inclusion of the fibers preserves (co)limits.

2.33.

Definition
2.31
may be stated (for cooperads) as follows: Let
\alpha:i\to i_{1}^{\prime},\dots,i_{n}^{\prime}
be a morphism in
I
and
X\in\mathcal{C}_{i}^{K}
,
Y_{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{i_{k}^{\prime}}^{K_{k}}
.
We have for each
m\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and a pro-functor
\beta:K_{m}\to K_{m}^{\prime}
a morphisms of pro-functors

(X,Y_{1},\dots,R(\beta)Y_{m},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\to R(\beta)((X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet})

and for each pro-functor
\beta:K^{\prime}\to K

(L(\beta)X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\to R(\beta^{\op})((X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet})

which are isomorphisms if and only if
\mathcal{C}
is
R
, resp.
L
-admissible.

2.34.

We also have a canonical morphism

(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}\to R(\beta^{\op})(R(\beta)X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}

(16)

which is the composition with the counit
L(\beta)R(\beta)\to\id
, if
L(\beta)
exists. However, the morphism always exists.

We also have canonical morphisms

(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}\to R(\beta)(X;Y_{1},\dots,L(\beta)Y_{k},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}

(17)

which is the composition with the unit
\id\to R(\beta)L(\beta)
if
R(\beta)
exists. However, the morphism always exists.

2.35.

By (slight) abuse of notation we continue to write
\times
for the usual product of two categories also as objects in
\Cat^{\PF}_{\infty}
.
Notice, however, that
(\Cat^{\PF}_{\infty},\times)
is not Cartesian (as monoidal
(\infty,2)
-category, i.e.
\times
is not the product), yet there is a natural morphism

\Hom_{\Cat^{\PF}_{\infty}}(I_{1},\dots,I_{n};J)\to\Hom_{\Cat^{\PF}_{\infty}}(I_{1};J)\times\cdots\times\Hom_{\Cat^{\PF}_{\infty}}(I_{n};J)

given by composition with the pro-functor
\cdot\to I_{i}
given by the final object in
\Hom(I_{i}^{\op},\Set)
.
Similarly for
(\Cat^{\PF}_{\infty},\times)^{\vee}
, there is a natural morphism

\Hom_{\Cat^{\PF}_{\infty}}(J;I_{1},\dots,I_{n})\to\Hom_{\Cat^{\PF}_{\infty}}(J;I_{1})\times\cdots\times\Hom_{\Cat^{\PF}_{\infty}}(J;I_{n})

given by composition with the pro-functor
I_{i}\to\cdot
given by the final object in
\Hom(I_{i},\Set)
.

Lemma 2.36
.

1.

Let
\mathcal{C}\to I
be a cofibration of
\infty
-operads. Then it is automatically
R
-admissible if it has complete fibers and
L
-admissible if it has cocomplete fibers and the push-forward functors are cocontinous in the sense that for all
\alpha:i\to i_{1}^{\prime},\dots,i_{n}^{\prime}
, and all entries
\in\{1,\dots,n\}
the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}^{J}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{\bullet,\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\id\times\cdots\times\colim\times\cdots\times\id}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{J}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\colim}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{\bullet,\mathcal{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}}

is commutative (and no condition on the 0-ary morphisms, e.g. units).

2.

Let
\mathcal{C}\to I
be a fibration of
\infty
-operads. Then it is automatically
L
-admissible if it has cocomplete fibers and
R
-admissible if it has complete fibers and the pull-back functors are cocontinous in the sense that
for all
\alpha:i\to i_{1}^{\prime},\dots,i_{n}^{\prime}
, the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{J}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lim}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{J}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}^{J}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lim\times\cdots\times\lim}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}}

are commutative (and no condition on the 0-ary morphisms).

Similarly for cooperads.

In particular, for cofibrations of
\infty
-operads, the notion of
L
-admissible is the same as a “cofibration compatible with
K
-indexed colimits” (for all
K
) in the sense of
[
41
, Definition 3.1.1.18]
.
For example, a monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
is
L
-admissible, if and only if it is cocomplete and
\otimes
commutes with colimits in each variable separately.

Proposition 2.37
.

Let
J\to I
be an (
\infty
-)exponential fibration of (
\infty
-)(co)operads, and
\alpha:i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\to i
(resp.
\alpha:i\to i_{1}^{\prime},\dots,i_{n}^{\prime}
) be an active morphism in
I

with associated pro-functor
\alpha^{\bullet}_{J}:J_{i_{1}^{\prime}}^{\op}\times\cdots\times J_{i_{1}^{\prime}}^{\op}\times J_{i}\to\Gpd_{\infty}

(resp.
\alpha^{\bullet}_{J}:J_{i^{\prime}}^{\op}\times J_{i_{1}}\times\cdots\times J_{i_{n}}\to\Gpd_{\infty}
).

1.

If
\mathcal{C}\to I
is an
L
-admissible cofibration of (
\infty
-)(co)operads. Then also

D_{I}(J,\mathcal{C})\to I

is a cofibration with push-forward along
\alpha
given by

L(\alpha^{\bullet}_{J})\circ\alpha_{\bullet,\mathcal{C}}(-,\dots,-)\text{ resp.{} }(L(\alpha^{\bullet}_{J,1}),\dots,L(\alpha^{\bullet}_{J,n}))\circ\alpha_{\bullet,\mathcal{C}}(-)

2.

If
\mathcal{C}\to I
is an
R
-admissible fibration of (
\infty
-)(co)operads. Then also

D_{I}(J,\mathcal{C})\to I

is a fibration with pull-back along
\alpha
given by

(R(\alpha^{\bullet}_{J,1}),\dots,R(\alpha^{\bullet}_{J,n}))\circ\alpha^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}(-)\text{ resp.{} }R(\alpha^{\bullet}_{J})\circ\alpha^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}(-,\dots,-).

Here the
\alpha^{\bullet}_{J,k}
are the projections of the pro-functor
J_{i_{1}^{\prime}}\times\cdots\times J_{i_{n}^{\prime}}\to J_{i}
(resp.
J_{i}\to J_{i_{1}^{\prime}}\times\cdots\times J_{i_{n}^{\prime}}
) (
2.35
).

Proof.

We prove the statements for cooperads, the others are dual.
If
\mathcal{C}\to I
is an
L
-admissible cofibration then

\displaystyle\int_{j,j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime}}\Hom(\alpha^{\bullet}_{I}(j;j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime}),\Hom(X(j);Y_{1}(j_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,Y_{n}(j_{n}^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\int_{j,j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime}}\Hom(\alpha^{\bullet}_{I}(j;j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime}),\Hom((\alpha_{\bullet,\mathcal{C},1}X(j));Y_{1}(j_{1}^{\prime}))\times\cdots

\displaystyle\times\Hom((\alpha_{\bullet,\mathcal{C},n}X(j)),Y_{n}(j_{n}^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\int_{j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime}}\Hom(\int^{j}\alpha^{\bullet}_{I}(j;j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime})\times(\alpha_{\bullet,\mathcal{C},1}X(j));Y_{1}(j_{1}^{\prime}))\times\cdots

\displaystyle\times\Hom(\int^{j}\alpha^{\bullet}_{I}(j;j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime})\times(\alpha_{\bullet,\mathcal{C},n}X(j)),Y_{n}(j_{n}^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\int_{j_{1}^{\prime}}\Hom((L(\alpha^{\bullet}_{I,1})(\alpha_{\bullet,\mathcal{C},1}X(j^{\prime}_{1})));Y_{1}(j_{1}^{\prime}))\times\cdots

\displaystyle\times\int_{j_{n}^{\prime}}\Hom(L(\alpha^{\bullet}_{I,n})(\alpha_{\bullet,\mathcal{C},n}X(j_{n}^{\prime})),Y_{n}(j_{n}^{\prime}))).

(notice that the end is just a limit, once the argument depends on the variable only contra- or covariantly).
If
\mathcal{C}\to I
is an
R
-admissible fibration then

\displaystyle\int_{j,j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime}}\Hom(\alpha^{\bullet}_{I}(j;j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime}),\Hom(X(j);Y_{1}(j_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,Y_{n}(j_{n}^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\int_{j,j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime}}\Hom(\alpha^{\bullet}_{I}(j;j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime}),\Hom(X(j);\alpha^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}(Y_{1}(j_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,Y_{n}(j_{n}^{\prime}))))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\int_{j}\Hom(X(j);\int_{j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime}}\Hom(\alpha^{\bullet}_{I}(j;j^{\prime}_{1},\dots,j_{n}^{\prime}),\alpha^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}(Y_{1}(j_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,Y_{n}(j_{n}^{\prime}))))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\int_{j}\Hom(X(j);R(\alpha^{\bullet}_{I})(\alpha^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}))(j)).

In each case (
15
) is an isomorphism by a formal calculation using the
L-
, resp.
R
-admissibility.
∎

Of course, instead of the
L
- or
R
-admissibility one needs only to assume the existence of
certain
(co)limits and compatibility for them. We will not put this into an explicit statement.

Example 2.38
.

Let
J\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
be an exponential fibration of operads (sometimes
J
is then called a pro-monoidal category). For

(\mathcal{C},\otimes)
a monoidal category with
\mathcal{C}
having the relevant colimits
16
16

16

more precisely, the Kan extensions
L(\gamma)
, where
\gamma
is one of the pro-functors encoding
J
must exist
such that
\otimes
commutes with them entrywise,
D(J,(\mathcal{C},\otimes))
is again monoidal, with product

-\otimes-:=L(m^{\bullet})-\boxtimes-

where

\boxtimes:\mathcal{C}^{J_{[1]}}\times\mathcal{C}^{J_{[1]}}\to\underbrace{\mathcal{C}^{J_{[1]}\times J_{[1]}}}_{\cong\mathcal{C}^{J_{[2]}}}

is the point-wise application of
\otimes
.
If
J
is itself monoidal (i.e.
J\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
a cofibration) with product
\dec:J_{[1]}\times J_{[1]}\to J_{[1]}
then
-\otimes-:=\dec_{!}-\boxtimes-
.
If
\mathcal{C}
has the relevant colimits but
\otimes
does not commute with them then
D(J,(\mathcal{C},\otimes))\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
is locally coCartesian but not a cofibration.

Similarly, if
J\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
is an exponential fibration of cooperads. For

(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee}
a monoidal category (considered as cooperad) with
\mathcal{C}
having the relevant limits such that
\otimes
commutes with them,
D(J,(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee})
is the monoidal category (considered as cooperad) with product

-\otimes-:=R(m^{\bullet})-\boxtimes-

If
J
is itself monoidal (i.e.
J\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}
a fibration) with product
\dec:J_{[1]}\times J_{[1]}\to J_{[1]}
then
-\otimes-:=\dec_{*}-\boxtimes-
.

This example is central for the understanding of the differences between the two natural tensor products on non-negatively graded complexes in the Abelian case (cf.
4.16
).

Example 2.39
.

If
J\to I
is a fibration and
\mathcal{C}\to I
is a cofibration of operads or vice versa then
D_{I}(J,\mathcal{C})
is a cofibration (or fibration, respectively) and we have

(D_{I}(J,\mathcal{C}))^{\vee}\cong D_{I}(J^{\vee},\mathcal{C}^{\vee}).

Moreover, if they are classified by functors
\Xi_{J}:I^{\op}\to(\Cat_{\infty},\times)
and
\Xi_{\mathcal{C}}:I\to(\Cat_{\infty},\times)
then
D_{I}(J,\mathcal{C})
is classified by the functor

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle\to(\Cat_{\infty},\times)

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\mapsto\Hom(\Xi_{J}(i),\Xi_{\mathcal{C}}(i))\qquad\text{for $i\in I_{[1]}$}.

Dually, the same holds true, of course, for cooperads.

2.40.

A functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
over
I
between (
\infty
-)exponential fibrations of (
\infty
-)(co)operads gives rise to an oplax transformation

F:\Hom^{\oplax,\inert-\pseudo}_{(\Cat^{\PF,\times})^{I^{\op}}}(\Xi_{\mathcal{C}},\Xi_{\mathcal{D}})\qquad(\text{resp.{} }F:\Hom^{\oplax,\inert-\pseudo}_{(\Cat^{\PF,\times}_{\infty})^{I^{\op}}}(\Xi_{\mathcal{C}},\Xi_{\mathcal{D}}))

where
\Xi_{\mathcal{C}},\Xi_{\mathcal{D}}:I^{\op}\to(\Cat^{\PF},\times)
(resp.
(\Cat^{\PF}_{\infty},\times)
) are the classifying functors
and
\Cat^{\PF,\times}
(resp.
\Cat^{\PF,\times}_{\infty}
) is the underlying
(1,2)
- (resp.
(\infty,2)
-) category (of operators) of
(\Cat^{\PF},\times)
(resp.
(\Cat^{\PF}_{\infty},\times)
)
with components for active
\alpha:i\to i^{\prime}
:

F_{i}\,\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\to\alpha_{\mathcal{D}}^{\bullet}\,F_{i^{\prime}}

(18)

or equivalently, gives rise to a lax transformation (its mate):

F:\Hom^{\lax,\inert-\pseudo}_{(\Cat^{\PF,\times})^{I^{\op}}}(\Xi_{\mathcal{D}},\Xi_{\mathcal{C}})\qquad(\text{resp.{} }F:\Hom^{\lax,\inert-\pseudo}_{(\Cat^{\PF,\times}_{\infty})^{I^{\op}}}(\Xi_{\mathcal{D}},\Xi_{\mathcal{C}}))

with components:

\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\,{}^{t}\!F_{i^{\prime}}\to{}^{t}\!F_{i}\,\alpha_{\mathcal{D}}^{\bullet}.

(19)

Notice that the functors have values in
\Cat^{\PF,\times}_{(\infty)}
(not
\Cat^{\PF}_{(\infty)}
). Therefore the formation of mate lands in the full subcategory of
\inert-\pseudo
transformation again. Notice also
(\Cat^{\PF}_{(\infty)},\times)
is to be considered as cooperad, if
I
is an operad and vice versa.
\Cat^{\PF,\times}_{(\infty)}
depends on this!

Definition 2.41
.

We call
F

Cartesian
(resp.
\infty
-Cartesian
), if (
18
) is an isomorphism for active morphisms and
coCartesian
(resp.
\infty
-coCartesian), if (
19
) is an isomorphism for active morphisms.

Note that a functor is a morphism of fibrations in the usual sense (i.e. maps Cartesian morphisms to Cartesian ones) if and only if it is Cartesian according to the Definition above. Similarly,
a functor is a morphism of cofibrations (i.e. maps coCartesian morphisms to coCartesian ones) if and only if it is coCartesian according to the Definition above.

Proposition 2.42
.

If
\mathcal{C}\to I
is an
L
-admissible functor of cooperads then the functor Day convolution extends to a functor of 2-categories:

\textstyle{\Hom^{1-\oplax,1-\inert-\pseudo}(I^{\op},(\Cat^{\PF},\times))^{1-\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{\mathcal{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{coOp}_{/I}}

If
\mathcal{C}\to I
is an
R
-admissible functor of cooperads then the functor Day convolution extends to a functor of 2-categories:

\textstyle{\Hom^{1-\lax,1-\inert-\pseudo}(I^{\op},(\Cat^{\PF},\times))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{\mathcal{C}}}
\textstyle{(\mathrm{coOp}_{/{I}})^{2-\op}}

The same is true with the decoration
\infty
everywhere and for operads.

To be clear: The 2-category on the left hand side has as objects functors of operads
\Xi_{\mathcal{C}}:I^{\op}\to(\Cat^{\PF},\times)
and morphism categories are given by

\Hom^{\mathrm{oplax},\inert-\pseudo}_{(\Cat^{\PF,\times})^{I^{\op}}}(\Xi_{\mathcal{D}},\Xi_{\mathcal{C}})

and

\Hom^{\mathrm{lax},\inert-\pseudo}_{(\Cat^{\PF,\times})^{I^{\op}}}(\Xi_{\mathcal{C}},\Xi_{\mathcal{D}})

where
\Cat^{\PF,\times}
is as before.

Proof.

We will not rigorously prove this and be content with specifying the map on morphism spaces.
Let
J\to I,K\to I
be exponential fibrations of cooperads classified by functors
\Xi_{J},\Xi_{K}:I^{\op}\to(\Cat^{\PF},\times)
and assume given a
lax transformation
\mu:\Xi_{J}\Rightarrow\Xi_{K}
.
For a morphism
\alpha:i\to i^{\prime}_{1},\dots i_{n}^{\prime}
in
I
this yields a diagram

\textstyle{J_{i^{\prime}_{1}}\times\cdots\times J_{i^{\prime}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\bullet}_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{i^{\prime}_{1}},\dots,\beta_{i^{\prime}_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\Nearrow}
\textstyle{J_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{i}}
\textstyle{K_{i^{\prime}_{1}}\times\cdots\times K_{i^{\prime}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\bullet}_{K}}
\textstyle{K_{i}}

Let
\alpha:i\to i_{1}^{\prime},\dots,i_{n}^{\prime}
be a morphism in
I
. Then the map is given by:

\displaystyle\ [\alpha^{\bullet}_{J},(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\ [\alpha^{\bullet}_{J},R(\beta_{i}^{\op})\left((R(\beta_{i})X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\right)]

)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ [L(\beta_{i}^{\op})\alpha^{\bullet}_{J},(R(\beta_{i})X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}]

-adjunction

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\ [L(\beta_{i^{\prime}_{1}},\dots,\beta_{i^{\prime}_{n}})\alpha^{\bullet}_{K},(R(\beta_{i})X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}]

laxness constraint

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ [\alpha^{\bullet}_{K},R(\beta_{i^{\prime}_{1}},\dots,\beta_{i^{\prime}_{n}})\left((R(\beta_{i})X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\right)]

L

R
-admissibility

Similarly for the

-functoriality:

\displaystyle\ [\alpha^{\bullet}_{J},(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\ [\alpha^{\bullet}_{J},(R(\beta_{i_{1}^{\prime}}),\dots,R(\beta_{i_{n}^{\prime}}))\left((X;L(\beta_{i_{1}^{\prime}})Y_{1},\dots,L(\beta_{i_{n}^{\prime}})Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\right)]

canonical map (

)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ [(L(\beta_{i_{1}^{\prime}},\dots,\beta_{i_{1}^{\prime}})\alpha^{\bullet}_{J},(X;L(\beta_{i_{1}^{\prime}})Y_{1},\dots,L(\beta_{i_{n}^{\prime}})Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}]

\displaystyle\ [\alpha^{\bullet}_{K},R(\beta_{i}^{\op})\left((X;L(\beta_{i_{1}^{\prime}})Y_{1},\dots,L(\beta_{i_{n}^{\prime}})Y_{n})^{*}\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}\right)]

-admissibility

The operad case is dual and everything works the same with
\infty
-cooperads.
∎

2.43.

If
\mathcal{C}\to I
and
\mathcal{D}\to I
are exponential fibrations, an oplax transformation (and inert-pseudo) of the associated functors
\Xi_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow\Xi_{\mathcal{D}}
is called an
oplax pro-functor of (co)operads

\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
,
and a lax transformation (and inert-pseudo)
F_{\mathcal{D}}\Rightarrow F_{\mathcal{C}}
is called a
lax pro-functor of (co)operads

\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
. We call an oplax pro-functor
Cartesian
, if it a natural transformation (not only oplax) and a lax pro-functor
coCartesian
if it a natural transformation (not only lax).

As just seen, a usual functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
of (co)operads over
I
can be seen as a either of these morphisms. This mirrors the situation over a point discussed in
2.4
. In fact, if
\mathcal{C}\to I
is
L
-admissible, the whole diagram (
11
)
extends to fibrations of (co)operads over
I
:

\textstyle{\mathrm{(co)Op}_{/I}^{\exp,1-\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\op}
\textstyle{\Hom^{1-\oplax,1-\inert-\pseudo}(I^{\op},(\Cat^{\PF},\times))^{1-\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\op}
\textstyle{\mathrm{(co)Op}_{/I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\op}
\textstyle{\mathrm{(co)Op}_{/I^{\op}}^{\exp,1-\op,2-\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{t}\iota}
\textstyle{\Hom^{1-\lax,1-\inert-\pseudo}(I,(\Cat^{\PF},\times))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{\mathcal{C}^{\op}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{(co)Op}^{2-\op}_{/I^{\op}}}

where Op and coOp are interchanged in the two rows.
Here
L_{\mathcal{C}}
and also
R_{\mathcal{C}}
, for a category or (co)operad
\mathcal{C}\to I
, are given by the Day convolution on objects, and on morphisms by Proposition
2.42
.

Also
\iota
and
{}^{t}\iota
agree on objects. More generally, if
F,G:I^{\op}\to(\Cat^{\PF},\times)
are functors and

\rho\in\Hom^{\oplax,\inert-\pseudo}_{\Cat^{\PF,\times}}(F,G)

has a point-wise right adjoint, then they assemble to a mate

{}^{t}\!\rho\in\Hom^{\lax,\inert-\pseudo}_{\Cat^{\PF,\times}}(G,F)

(and vice versa) and for
\mathcal{C}\to I
we have

L_{\mathcal{C}}(\rho)=R_{\mathcal{C}}({}^{t}\!\rho).

In particular,
L_{\mathcal{C}}\,\iota=R_{\mathcal{C}}\,{}^{t}\!\iota
also on morphisms.
The same is true in the
\infty
-categorical context.

2.44.

Let
\mathcal{C}\to I
be an (
\infty
-)exponential fibration of (
\infty
-)(co)operads with associated pro-functors
\alpha^{\bullet}:\mathcal{C}_{i^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{C}_{i}
.
Let
\iota:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
be a full embedding. Then the pro-functors associated with
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to I
are obviously given by

(\alpha^{\prime})^{\bullet}={}^{t}\!\iota\,\alpha^{\bullet}\iota
and the transition morphism

\displaystyle\,{}^{t}\!\iota\,g^{\bullet}\iota\,{}^{t}\!\iota f^{\bullet}\iota\Rightarrow\,{}^{t}\!\iota(fg)^{\bullet}\iota

(20)

is induced by the counit
\iota\,{}^{t}\!\iota\to\id
. Thus
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is an (
\infty
-)exponential fibration again, if and only if (
20
) is an isomorphism.
This is obviously the case
if
\iota
is (
\infty
-)Cartesian (i.e. if
\iota\,{}^{t}\!\iota\,f^{\bullet}\,\iota\to f^{\bullet}\,\iota
is an isomorphism) or (
\infty
-)coCartesian (i.e. if
{}^{t}\!\iota\,f^{\bullet}\,\iota\,{}^{t}\!\iota\to{}^{t}\!\iota\,f^{\bullet}
is an isomorphism). We have proven:

Lemma 2.45
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\to I
be an (
\infty
-)exponential fibration of (
\infty
-)(co)operads and
\iota:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
be a full embedding.
If
\iota
is (
\infty
-)Cartesian or (
\infty
-)coCartesian
17
17

17

cf. Definition
2.41
, which is not literally applicable as stated, thus interpreted as in
2.44
then
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to I
is again an (
\infty
-)exponential fibration.

2.6
Twisted arrow categories for operads

2.46.

Let
\Xi\in\{\downarrow,\uparrow\}^{n}
be an ordered sequence of directions. For an
\infty
-category, or more generally, an
\infty
-operad
I
, we will define

\infty
-categories (resp.
\infty
-operads)
{}^{\Xi}I
such that for
\infty
-categories:

{}^{\downarrow}I=I,\ {}^{\uparrow}I=I^{\op},\ {}^{\downarrow\uparrow}I=\mathrm{tw}(I),\ {}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I=\mathrm{tw}^{\op}(I)

where
\mathrm{tw}(I)
is the twisted arrow category
[
41
, 5.2.1]

and such that, more generally, the
active
morphism spaces in
{}^{\Xi}I
are equivalent to the space of commutative diagrams of the form

\textstyle{x_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x_{1}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x_{2}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x_{n}^{\prime}}

(21)

in which all morphisms are active, and where the arrow directions are dictated by
\Xi
. For operads, the equation
{}^{\downarrow\uparrow}I=\mathrm{tw}(I)
does not hold true
when one understands by
\mathrm{tw}(I)
the usual twisted arrow category associated with the underlying
\infty
-category (of operators). The two are related, however, see Lemma
2.51
below.

Definition 2.47
.

Let
I
be an
\infty
-operad or
\infty
-category. Define inductively:

{}^{\Xi\downarrow}I:=\int{}^{\Xi}(I_{\act}\times_{/I_{\act}}i)

the unstraightening construction associated with the functor of
\infty
-categories (of operators):

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\Cat_{\infty}

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle I_{\act}\times_{/I_{\act}}i

the functoriality being obtained by unique factorization into inert and active.
Finally set:

{}^{\Xi\uparrow}I:=({}^{\Xi^{\prime}\downarrow}I)^{\op}

where in
\Xi^{\prime}
all arrow directions are reversed.

This defines recursively
{}^{\Xi}I
for all operads
I
. Notice that the
\infty
-operad structure only plays a role in the first step; the comma category
I_{\act}\times_{/I_{\act}}i
is just to be considered as a plain
\infty
-category and does not
carry any operad structure.

Lemma 2.48
.

{}^{\Xi}I
is an
\infty
-operad again, if the last arrow of
\Xi
is
\downarrow
, and otherwise an
\infty
-cooperad.
The last projection
\pi_{n}:{}^{\Xi}I\to I
is a cofibration of operads, resp. a fibration of cooperads
\pi_{n}:{}^{\Xi}I\to I^{\op}
.

Proof.

By basic facts on
\infty
-operads
2.19
, 6.
the association

i\mapsto I_{\act}\times_{/I_{\act}}i

factors

I\to\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}\to\Cat_{\infty}

where the first is a functor of
\infty
-operads, and thus the corresponding cofibration is a cofibration of
\infty
-operads (cf.
2.19
, 4.).
∎

Definition 2.49
.

An active morphism which corresponds to a diagram (
21
) in which all vertical morphisms, except for the
i
-th one, are isomorphisms, is called a
type-
i
morphism.
With respect to the recursive definition, those are precisely the morphisms that lie in the fiber for
n-i
steps and are coCartesian for the next unstraighening (resp. for
i=1
are just any morphism in the fibers for the last step).
In particular, all type-
i
morphisms for
i<n
lie over an isomorphism in
\mathcal{O}
.

Lemma 2.50
.

Let
I
be an
\infty
-category.
Let
\Xi^{\prime}\subset\Xi
be a non-empty subsequence of arrow directions. Then the projection

\pi:{}^{\Xi}I\rightarrow{}^{\Xi^{\prime}}I

is a localization, i.e. it exhibits the target as the localization of
{}^{\Xi}I
at those morphisms that are mapped to isomorphisms under
\pi
.

Proof.

By induction, it suffices to show the following.
Let
\Xi=L\!\!\downarrow\!\!R
be a sequence of arrow directions and let
\Xi^{\prime}=L\,R
. One of
R
or
L
may be empty, but not both. Then the projection

{}^{\Xi}I\rightarrow{}^{\Xi^{\prime}}I

is a localization.
We first discuss the case in which the right-most arrow of
L
is
\downarrow
or the leftmost arrow of
R
is
\downarrow
.
Then there is even a section

{}^{\Xi^{\prime}}I\rightarrow{}^{\Xi}I

which turns
{}^{\Xi^{\prime}}I
a (co)reflective subcategory.
Now assume that the condition is not satisfied. If
L
or
R
is empty then this projection is actually a (co)fibration with fibers of the form

i\times_{/I}I
, resp.
I\times_{/I}i
, hence contractible. The morphism from a (co)fibration with contractible fibers to its base is a localization.
If
R
and
L
are not empty consider the projection

\textstyle{{}^{L\downarrow R}I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}^{LR}I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}^{R}I}

This is a morphism of (co)fibrations (i.e. mapping (co)Cartesian morphisms to (co)Cartesian ones) and the morphism between the fibers is of the form:

{}^{L\downarrow}I\times_{/I}i\to{}^{L}I\times_{/I}i

hence of a form discussed already. A morphism of (co)fibrations which is fiber-wise a localization is a localization itself
[
6
, 4.1.11]
.
∎

Lemma 2.51
.

Let
I
be an
\infty
-operad. There is a functor

\Pi:\mathrm{tw}^{\op}(I)\to{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I

which is a localization. Here
\mathrm{tw}^{\op}(I)
denotes the usual (dual) twisted arrow category of
I
considered as
\infty
-category (of operators).

Proof.

The functor is the unstraightening (in the sense of
\infty
-categories) of the fiber-wise (over
I
) functor

I\times_{/I}i\to I_{\act}\times_{/I_{\act}}i

given by unique factorization.
This is even a
reflective
localization.
One checks that for a morphism
\alpha:i\to i^{\prime}
in
I
one has a commutative diagram

\textstyle{I\times_{/I}i\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I_{\act}\times_{/I_{\act}}i\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I\times_{/I}i^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I_{\act}\times_{/I_{\act}}i^{\prime}}

where the left vertical functor is composition and the right hand side is the functoriality used to define
{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I
which also stems from the unique factorization.
They assemble to a natural transformation of functors
I\to\Cat_{\infty}
.
∎

2.7
(Co)fibrations of (co)operads and (op)lax limits

This section contains a brief discussion of the relation between (co)fibrations of (co)operads and the corresponding notions of
(op)lax morphisms of their classifying functors (more background, explicit constructions in a particular model of
(\infty,2)
-categories, and proofs can be found in
[
30
,
31
,
2
,
14
,
1
,
27
,
26
,
6
]
).

2.52.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
(\infty,2)
-category and
I
a small
\infty
-category. We denote by

\Hom^{\lax}_{\mathcal{C}^{I}}(F,G)

the
(\infty,1)
-category of lax natural transformations (cf. e.g.
[
14
,
2
,
31
]
). We adopt the convention that for a lax transformation and
for each morphism
i\to i^{\prime}
in
I
there is a diagram in
C
of shape

\textstyle{F(i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Uparrow}
\textstyle{F(i^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(i^{\prime})}

(as opposed to the other direction of the 2-morphism).
For
F\in C^{I^{\op}}
and
G\in C^{I}
we also denote by

\Dinat^{\lax}(F,G)

a similar construction in which for each morphism
i\to i^{\prime}
in
I
there is a diagram in
C

\textstyle{F(i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Rightarrow}
\textstyle{F(i^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(i^{\prime})}

We have the notions of (op)lax limit and colimit which are the 2-adjoint to the constant functor. They exist with decoration for a set
C
of morphisms in
I
, i.e.

\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}^{I}}^{\mathrm{(op)lax},C-\pseudo}(F,\alpha^{*}G)\cong\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}}(\mathrm{(op)laxcolim}^{C-\pseudo}F,G)

where
\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}^{I}}^{\mathrm{(op)lax},C-\pseudo}\subseteq\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}^{I}}^{\lax}
means the full subcategory of lax transformation in which the constraint is an isomorphism for all morphisms in
C
and

\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}^{I}}^{\mathrm{(op)lax},C-\pseudo}(\alpha^{*}F,G)\cong\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}}(F,\mathrm{(op)laxlim}^{C-\pseudo}G).

They are called
partially (op)lax limits
in
[
14
,
1
]
.

This allows to write also

\mathrm{(op)laxlim}^{C-\pseudo}G=\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}^{I}}^{\mathrm{(op)lax},C-\pseudo}(\cdot,G).

Furthermore, we have

\laxcolim F=\int F\qquad\oplaxcolim F=\mathop{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int$}}}}F.

the unstraightening construction, and its dual, respectively. These are a cofibration, resp. a fibration over
I
.
Then

\displaystyle\laxlim_{I}^{C-\pseudo}G=\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}/I}(I,\laxcolim_{I}G)^{C-\cocart}

\displaystyle\oplaxlim_{I}G=\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}/I}(I,\oplaxcolim_{I}G)^{C-\cart}

Here
C-\mathrm{(co)cart}
means the full subcategory of functors that map morphisms in
C
to (co)Cartesian morphisms.

We have also

\laxcolim_{I}^{C-\pseudo}YG=\laxcolim_{I}G[\{C-\cart\}^{-1}]\qquad\oplaxcolim_{I}^{C-\pseudo}G=\oplaxcolim_{I}G[\{C-\cocart\}^{-1}]

(localization at the Cartesian morphism over morphisms in
C
) which will no be needed in the sequel, though.

Lemma 2.53
.

If
F,G:I\to(\Cat_{\infty},\times)
are functors of
\infty
-operads then the association
18
18

18

Here
\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}
denotes the underlying
(\infty,2)
-category (of operators) of the
(\infty,2)
-operad
(\Cat_{\infty},\times)
, i.e. with
(\Cat_{\infty}^{\times})_{[n]}=\Cat_{\infty}^{n}
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{tw}^{\op}(I)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\Cat_{\infty}

\displaystyle\alpha:i\to i^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\Hom^{p(\alpha)}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(F(i),G(i^{\prime}))

where
p:I\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
is the structural morphism,
factors through
{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I
and induces an isomorphism of
(\infty,1)
-categories:

\displaystyle\Hom_{(\Cat^{\times}_{\infty})^{I}}^{\lax,\inert-\pseudo}(F,G)\cong\laxlim_{\mu:i\to i^{\prime}\in{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I}^{1-\pseudo,\inert-\pseudo}\Hom^{p(\mu)}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(F(i),G(i^{\prime})).

Proof.

Recall from Lemma
2.51
that

\Pi:\mathrm{tw}^{\op}(I)\to{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I

is a localization. Thus, we have to show that the functor maps morphisms
\beta
, which are mapped to isomorphisms under
\Pi
, to isomorphisms.
Such a
\beta
induces a diagram

\textstyle{i_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{i^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{i}
\textstyle{i_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{2}}

where
\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{2}
are inert and
\alpha
is active, and which in turn induces a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Hom^{p(\alpha\iota_{2})}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(F(i_{2}),G(i))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{F(\beta)\circ-}
\textstyle{\Hom^{p(\alpha\iota_{1})}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(F(i_{1}),G(i))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Hom^{p(\alpha)}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(\iota_{2,\bullet}F(i_{2}),G(i))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Hom^{p(\alpha)}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(\iota_{1,\bullet}F(i_{1}),G(i))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Hom^{p(\alpha)}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(F(i^{\prime}),G(i))}

Here
\iota_{1,\bullet}
and
\iota_{2,\bullet}
are the (2-categorical) push-forward along the inert morphisms
\iota_{1}
,
\iota_{2}
.
Since
F
is a functor of
(\infty,2)
-operads the diagonal morphisms are isomorphisms as well.

The last assertion boils down to that statement that this following morphism induces by the localization functor

\Pi:\mathrm{tw}^{\op}I\to{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I
is an equivalence:

\laxlim_{\alpha:i\to i^{\prime}\in\mathrm{tw}^{\op}(I)}^{1-\pseudo,\inert-\pseudo}\Hom^{p(\alpha)}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(F(i),G(i^{\prime}))\cong\laxlim_{\alpha:i\to i^{\prime}\in{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I}^{1-\pseudo,\inert-\pseudo}\Hom^{p(\alpha)}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(F(i),G(i^{\prime}))

From the first part follows that

\displaystyle\laxlim_{\alpha:i\to i^{\prime}\in\mathrm{tw}^{\op}(I)}^{1-\pseudo,\inert-\pseudo}\Hom^{p(\alpha)}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(F(i),G(i^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{tw}^{\op}(I)}(\mathrm{tw}^{\op}(I),\Pi^{*}\laxcolim_{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I}(\Hom^{p(\alpha)}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(F(i),G(i^{\prime})))^{1-\cocart,\inert-\cocart}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Hom_{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I}(\mathrm{tw}^{\op}(I),\laxcolim_{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I}(\Hom^{p(\alpha)}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(F(i),G(i^{\prime})))^{1-\cocart,\inert-\cocart}.

\Pi
is a localization at inert type-1 morphisms. Those are mapped to isomorphisms by functors that map inert morphisms to coCartesian (here iso-) morphisms.
Hence this is the same as:

=\Hom_{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I}({}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I,\laxcolim_{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I}(\Hom^{p(\alpha)}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}}(F(i),G(i^{\prime})))^{1-\cocart,\inert-\cocart}.\qed

Proposition 2.54
.

Let
F:I\to\Cat_{\infty}
be a functor.

1.

Let
I
be an
\infty
-category and
F,G:I\to\Cat_{\infty}
be functors.
Denote by
\Cat_{\infty}/I
the slice
(\infty,2)
-category (not lax slice) over
I
.
Then we have a natural isomorphism of
(\infty,1)
-categories

\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}/I}(\int F,\int G)\cong\Hom^{\lax}_{\Cat^{I}_{\infty}}(F,G).

2.

Let
I
be an
\infty
-category and
F:I^{\op}\to\Cat_{\infty}
and
G:I\to\Cat_{\infty}
be functors. Then

\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}/I}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int$}}}}F,\int G)\cong\Dinat^{\lax}(F,G).

3.

Let
I
be an
\infty
-category and
F,G:I^{\op}\to\Cat_{\infty}
be functors.
Then

\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}/I}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int$}}}}F,\mathop{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int$}}}}G)\cong\Hom^{\oplax}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{I^{\op}}}(F,G).

4.

Let
I
be an
\infty
-category and
F:I\to\Cat_{\infty}
and
G:I^{\op}\to\Cat_{\infty}
be functors. Then

\Hom_{\Cat/I}(\int F,\mathop{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int$}}}}G)\cong\Dinat^{\oplax}(F,G).

2.55.

If
C
is a class of morphisms in
I
then more generally

\Hom_{\Cat_{\infty}/I}^{C-\cocart}(\int F,\int G)\cong\Hom^{\lax,C-\pseudo}_{\Cat^{I}_{\infty}}(F,G)

where the LHS is the full subcategory of
of those functors which map coCartesian morphisms lying over morphisms in
C
to coCartesian morphisms
and the RHS is the full subcategory of those lax natural transformations where the corresponding 2-morphism is an isomorphism for every morphism in
C
. The same is true dually.

This can be used to establish analogues of the preceding equivalences for operads:

Let
I
be an operad and
F:I\to(\Cat_{\infty},\times)
a functor of
\infty
-operads. We write
\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
for the category of operators of the target, i.e. with
(\Cat_{\infty}^{\times})_{[n]}=\Cat_{\infty}^{n}
.
The composition
I\to\Cat_{\infty}^{\times}\to\Cat_{\infty}
with
\times
is denoted by
F^{\times}
.
Then
F
classifies a cofibration of
\infty
-operads
\int F\to I
whose underlying
\infty
-category (of operators) is given by
\int F^{\times}=\laxcolim_{I}F^{\times}
.

Proposition 2.56
.

1.

Let
I
be an
\infty
-operad and
F,G:I\to(\Cat_{\infty},\times)
be functors of
\infty
-operads.
Then

\Hom_{\Op_{\infty}/I}(\int F,\int G)\cong\Hom^{\lax,\inert-\pseudo}_{(\Cat_{\infty}^{\times})^{I}}(F,G)\cong\Hom^{\lax,\inert-\pseudo}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{I}}(F^{\times},G^{\times})

2.

Let
I
be an
\infty
-cooperad and
F,G:I^{\op}\to(\Cat_{\infty},\times)
be functors of
\infty
-operads.
Then

\Hom_{\Coop_{\infty}/I}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int$}}}}F,\mathop{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\int$}}}{\reflectbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\int$}}}}G)\cong\Hom^{\oplax,\inert-\pseudo}_{(\Cat_{\infty}^{\times})^{I^{\op}}}(F,G)\cong\Hom^{\oplax,\inert-\pseudo}_{\Cat_{\infty}^{I^{\op}}}(F^{\times},G^{\times})

Proof.

Follows immediately from Proposition
2.54
, the discussion in
2.55
and basic facts on operads
2.19
.
∎

See
[
14
, Proposition 5.2.]
for a similar statement.

2.57.

Let
I
be a small
\infty
-category. The
twisted arrow categories
{}^{\downarrow\uparrow}I
, and
{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I
, discussed in Section
2.6
may be used to transfer between lax and oplax natural transformations.
This construction is at the heart of the (co)bar constructions discussed in the next chapter.
For
{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I
, or more generally for categories of the form
{}^{\Xi}I
, for
\Xi\in\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}^{n}
, we write
i-\pseudo
instead of
C-\pseudo
(
2.55
) for
C
being the class of type
i
-morphisms (
2.49
).

\Hom^{\oplax,2-\pseudo}_{\Cat^{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I}_{\infty}}:\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern
