(Lemma
3.30
) or in other words:

\cobarlurie_{R}(X\to Y)=X\times_{Y}X

and a bit more refined argument shows that the induced algebra structure is as claimed.
∎

3.31.

Let
A\in\Alg(\mathrm{Bimod}_{X}(\mathcal{C})_{X/},\times_{X})
. Then
\barconst(A)
is a coalgebra
B
of shape
\Delta^{\op}_{\act}
of the form

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\times_{X}A\times_{X}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\times_{X}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces X}

(34)

where the morphisms are the structure morphisms of the algebra
with canonical coalgebra structure

B_{[i+j]}\cong B_{[i]}\times_{X}B_{[j]}.

Then apply the forgetful functor
Q
and then
(\rho^{*})^{-1}
(in
\mathcal{C}_{X/}
the unit becomes final!) to get a simplicial diagram (with redundant coalgebra structure by the trivial Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem
4.70
)

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\times_{X}A\times_{X}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\times_{X}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces X.}

This is a simplicial diagram in
\mathcal{C}_{X/}
, not in
\Alg(\mathrm{Bimod}_{X}(\mathcal{C}))
!
Examining the definitions, one can easily determine the morphisms. For instance, the two morphisms
A\to X
are given by the left and right comodule structure, i.e. by the given morphism
A\to X\times X
(which are not bimodule morphisms themselves, for instance).

\barlurie_{Q}(A)
is then the colimit over this diagram.

Remark 3.32
.

If
\mathcal{C}=\Gpd_{\infty}
and for
X=\cdot
, we have
1\times_{Y}1=\Hom_{Y}(1,1)
, where
1\in Y
denotes the distinguished object given by the coaugmentation with
the product given by composition.

Corollary 3.33

(Connected case, cf. also
[
41
, Corollary 5.2.2.13]
)
.

There are adjunctions

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{Mon}(\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\barlurie}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cobarlurie}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}}

where
\Sigma:X\mapsto 1\amalg_{X}1
is the suspension, i.e. the left adjoint of
\mathcal{L}:X\mapsto 1\times_{X}1
, and
F
is the free monoid functor.

Of course dual statements are true for a coCartesian monoidal
\infty
-category
(\mathcal{C},\amalg)
, i.e. where
\amalg
is the coproduct.

3.34.

Consider the diagram

\textstyle{((\mathrm{Bimod}_{X}(\mathcal{C})_{X/},\times_{X})^{\vee})^{{}^{\downarrow\uparrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Q}
\textstyle{ ((\mathcal{C}_{/X},\times)^{\vee})^{{}^{\downarrow\uparrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces((\mathcal{C}_{/X},\times)^{\vee})^{(\Delta,*)^{\op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Coalg(\mathcal{C}_{/X}^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{/X}^{\Delta^{\op}}}

where the middle equivalence is given by the trivial Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem
4.70
.
Unraveling the definitions, we see that the composition induces an isomorphism:

((\mathrm{Bimod}_{X}(\mathcal{C})_{X/},\times_{X})^{\vee})^{{}^{\downarrow\uparrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$},2-\cart}\cong(\mathcal{C}_{X/}^{\Delta^{\op}})_{\mathrm{Segal},0}

where the index “0” means the full subcategory in which
X\to Y_{[0]}
is an isomorphism
and where “
\mathrm{Segal}
” means the full subcategory of the
Y
satisfying the
Segal condition
, that is, the induced morphism

Y_{[n+m]}\to Y_{[n]}\times_{Y_{[0]}}Y_{[m]}

is an isomorphism for all morphisms
[n+m]=[n]\ast^{\prime}[m]\to[n]\ast[m]
given by the canonical degeneracy. In particular
Y_{[n]}\cong Y_{[1]}\times_{Y_{[0]}}\cdots\times_{Y_{[0]}}Y_{[1]}
. Notice that
((\mathrm{Bimod}(\mathcal{C})_{X/},\times_{X})^{\vee})^{{}^{\downarrow\uparrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$},2-\cart}
is precisely the image of the (fully faithful) classical bar construction
in this case and thus isomorphic to
\Alg(\mathrm{Bimod}(\mathcal{C})_{X/},\times_{X})
(category objects).
In the connected case, this implies:

Proposition 3.35
.

The connected (i.e. for
X=\cdot
) classical (co)bar adjunction has the following form in the Cartesian case:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Mon}(\mathcal{C}_{\cdot/})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\barconst}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{C}_{\cdot/}^{\Delta^{\op}})_{\mathrm{Segal},0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{\cdot/}^{\Delta^{\op}}}
\scriptstyle{\cobarconst}

Note that the left adjoint “
\cobarconst
” exists by Theorem
3.15
, if
\mathcal{C}
has geometric realizations and
\times
commutes with them in each variable, which is true in any
\infty
-topos, for example.
The Proposition is a special case of results of
[
7
]
(see also
[
32
]
).

Proposition 3.36
.

Via the identification
Q\circ(\rho^{*})^{-1}\circ\barconst
of category objects with Segal objects, we have for
X\to Y\in\mathcal{C}_{X/}

\boxed{\cobarlurie_{R}(X\to Y)\cong\text{\v{C}ech nerve of $X\to Y$}}

and if
\mathcal{C}
is an
\infty
-topos (e.g.
\mathcal{C}=\Gpd_{\infty}
), the restriction of the derived (co)bar adjunction is an isomorphism

\textstyle{(\mathcal{C}_{X/}^{\Delta^{\op}})_{\mathrm{Gpd},0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\barlurie_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces(\mathcal{C}_{X/})_{(-1)-\mathrm{conn}}}
\scriptstyle{\cobarlurie_{R}}

where
\mathrm{Gpd}
indicates the full subcategory of
(\mathcal{C}_{X/}^{\Delta^{\op}})_{\mathrm{Segal},0}
of groupoid objects
24
24

24

meaning, in the picture above, that, in addition to the Segal condition,
Y_{[n+m]}\to Y_{[n]}\times_{Y_{[0]}}Y_{[m]}
is an isomorphism induced by
any
partition of
[n+m]
sharing one vertex.

and where
(\mathcal{C}_{X/})_{(-1)-\mathrm{conn}}
is the full subcategory of morphisms
X\to Y
which are
(-1)
-connected.

Proof.

The first part is clear from Proposition
3.29
and the definitions. The fully-faithfulness of
\barlurie_{Q}
on groupoid objects is thus actually part of the axioms of an
\infty
-topos. The characterization of
the essential image is in
[
40
, Proposiiton 6.2.3.15]
).
∎

3.7
Using cobar to construct Kan extensions — e.g. the cofree coalgebra

In Section
2.11
an idea to construct relative (operadic) Kan extensions has been discussed, even in situations which are not
L
- resp.
R
-admissible.
This leads precisely to the (dual) classical cobar construction discussed in this chapter and is included as an illustration. It is otherwise not related to the main aims of these lectures.

We will prove the following classical theorem using (the dual) cobar, noting that this method to construct problematic relative Kan extensions can be applied in far greater generality.

Theorem 3.37
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)
be an Abelian tensor category. Assume

1.

\otimes
is exact;

2.

the mate

(\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}X_{i})\otimes Y\to\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}(X_{i}\otimes Y)

is a monomorphism;

3.

\otimes
commutes with countable intersections.

Then the forgetful functor

\Coalg(\mathcal{C},\otimes)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}

has a right adjoint (cofree coalgebra functor).

Proof.

Denote
c:\cdot\hookrightarrow\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}
.
As in the proof of Proposition
2.76
we have
an adjunction in the
2
-category
\Dia^{\op}(\mathcal{\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}^{\op})

\textstyle{(\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}\times_{/\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}}\cdot,\pi_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\pi_{2}}=(\pi_{2},S(\mu))}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces(\cdot,c)}
\scriptstyle{(\iota,\id)}

and an isomorphism

c_{*}\cong\pi_{1,*}\,\iota_{*}^{(\pi_{1})}

in the strong sense that the existence of the RHS adjoints implies the existence of the LHS. By the adjunction, we see that

\iota_{*}^{(\pi_{1})}=S(\mu)^{\bullet}\pi_{2}^{*}.

We have
\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}\times_{/\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}}\cdot\cong(\mathbb{N}_{0},+)^{\vee}
as discrete cooperad and
X^{\prime}:=S(\mu)^{\bullet}\pi_{2}^{*}X
is the object

X^{\prime}_{n}=X^{\otimes n}

with the coalgebra structure given by the isomorphisms

X^{\prime}_{i+j}\cong X^{\prime}_{i}\otimes X^{\prime}_{j}\qquad X^{\prime}_{0}\cong 1.

A right relative (or operadic) Kan extension
\pi_{1,*}
exists in general only if
\otimes
commutes with infinite products.
However,
in
2.11
we saw that
it suffices to construct a (dual) cobar functor for the object
X^{\prime}
pulled back to
({}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$})\times_{\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}}(\mathbb{N}_{0},+)^{\vee}

and then right Kan extended along the fibration (!) of operads

({}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$})\times_{\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}}(\mathbb{N}_{0},+)^{\vee}\to{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}.

By the dual of Theorem
3.15
, we have
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}=((\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee})^{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow\uparrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$},3-\cart}
, and the dual cobar is given by the Kan extension
\pi_{3,*}
which is computed fiber-wise, i.e. as limit over
((\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee})^{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow\uparrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}_{[1]}\cong\mathcal{C}^{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\Delta^{\op}_{\act}}
,
if
\otimes
commutes with the relevant limits
. We will argue that the same colimit works in this case as well without that hypothesis.
The corresponding object
X^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\Delta^{\op}_{\act}}
is the following:

\left([n_{1}]\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}[n_{m}]\leftarrow[m]\right)\ \mapsto\ X^{\prime\prime}_{[n_{1}]}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{\prime\prime}_{[n_{m}]}

(the
[n_{1}]\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}[n_{m}]
is the induced decomposition, cf.
4.10
) with

X^{\prime\prime}_{[n]}=\prod_{k_{1},\cdots,k_{n}}X^{\otimes k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{\otimes k_{n}}.

Thus the dual of the cobar formula (
3.19
) would give

\displaystyle\boxed{\cobarconst^{\vee}X^{\prime}=\lim\left(\coprod_{n=0}^{\infty}(X^{\prime\prime}_{[1]})^{\otimes n}\rightrightarrows\begin{subarray}{c}\prod X^{\prime\prime}_{[1]}\otimes\cdots\times X^{\prime\prime}_{[2]}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{\prime\prime}_{[1]}\\
\amalg\\
\coprod X^{\prime\prime}_{[1]}\otimes\cdots\times X^{\prime\prime}_{[0]}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{\prime\prime}_{[1]}\end{subarray}\right)}

(35)

From 2. follows that the object
X^{\prime\prime\prime}
has the property that type-2 morphisms go to monomorphisms.
Hence (
35
) is essentially a countable intersection.

The general existence Theorem
3.15
(better: its dual) cannot be applied as it stands because
\otimes
does not commute with limits (here countable products would be sufficient, which we do not want to assume either).
Examining the proof, we see that this is used at two places:

1.

to construct the Kan extension along
\pi_{3}:{}^{\uparrow\downarrow\uparrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}
.
However the relevant object
X^{\prime\prime\prime}
has the property that type-2 morphisms go to monomorphisms. To have a fiber-wise Kan extension the limit over the fiber must commute with
\otimes
. This is Lemma
3.38
, 2.

2.

to see that the (fiber-wise for
\pi_{3}
) Kan extension
\pi_{1,*}:{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\Delta^{\op}_{\act}\to\Delta_{\act}
commutes with
\otimes
. This is Lemma
3.38
, 1. ∎

Lemma 3.38
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)
be an Abelian tensor category with
\otimes
exact and commuting with countable intersections.
If
\mathcal{E}\in\Ab^{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\Delta^{\op}_{\act}}
maps type-2 morphisms to monomorphisms
then

1.

the Kan extension
\mathcal{E}\mapsto\pi_{1,*}\mathcal{E}\in\Ab^{\Delta_{\act}}
commutes with
-\otimes X
for any
X\in\mathcal{C}
,

2.

the limit
\mathcal{E}\mapsto\lim\mathcal{E}
commutes with
-\otimes X
for any
X\in\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Exercise.
∎

Remark 3.39
.

In
[
4
]
, for instance, it is shown for a large class of Abelian tensor categories that, in fact,
already

\cobarconst^{\vee}X^{\prime\prime\prime}=\lim\left(\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 26.08334pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 50.08334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{\prime\prime}_{[1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.12502pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-26.08334pt\raise-31.96228pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{\prime\prime}_{[1]}\otimes X^{\prime\prime}_{[1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.08334pt\raise-31.96228pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.08334pt\raise-31.96228pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.08334pt\raise-31.96228pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{\prime\prime}_{[2]}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\right).

4
Non-Abelian foundations

This chapter discusses basic properties of simplicial sets and simplicial objects in categories culminating in the non-Abelian Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem
4.65
.
It deals almost exclusively with 1-categories. Some facts from Sections
4.1
–
4.2
have already been used in Chapter
3
.

4.1
The simplex category

4.1.

Recall from
2.20
the definition of the simplex category, the notions of active and inert morphism, and the definition of the concatenation products
\ast
and
\ast^{\prime}
.

4.2.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Barr-exact category (
[
8
]
, we will here only be interested in
\Set_{\bullet}
and Abelian categories), and let
X\in\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
.
Then there is a filtration by subobjects

F^{0}X_{[n]}\subset\cdots\subset F^{n}X_{[n]}=X_{[n]}

such that each
F^{k}X_{[n]}
is the smallest subobject over which all
X(\nu)
for degeneracies
\nu:[m]\to[n]
with
m\leq k
factor. We define

X_{[n]}^{\nd}:=X_{[n]}/F^{n-1}X_{[n]}.

the quotient of
non-degenerate elements
. In fact, the
F^{k}X_{[n]}
assemble to simplicial objects
F^{k}X
themselves and are called the
skeletal filtration
.

For any
n
and
k
, consider a subset
S\subset\{s:[k]\twoheadleftarrow[n]\}
of the degeneracies. Since each such degeneracy can be written
uniquely as
s=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{n-k}}
with
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{n-k}
, the lexicographic ordering of the
i_{j}
defines a total ordering on
S
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Fix integers
m
and
n
.
For each subset
S\subset\{s:[m]\twoheadleftarrow[n]\}
of degeneracies, there is a face morphism
D:[n]\hookleftarrow[m]
such that
D\,s=\id
for
s\in S
precisely if
s
is the smallest
element of
S
.

Proof.

Let
s_{i}
be the smallest degeneracy occurring as a left-most factor of
s\in S
and decompose

S=s_{m}S^{\prime}\cup S^{\prime\prime}

where
S^{\prime}
is a set of degeneracies
[m+1]\twoheadleftarrow[n]
and
S^{\prime\prime}
is a set of degeneracies in which only
s_{j}
for
j>i
occur.
By induction, there is a face
D^{\prime}:[n]\hookleftarrow[m+1]
which is a section of the first element of
S^{\prime}
and not of any other.
Then
D:=D^{\prime}d_{i}
does the job, because
D^{\prime}d_{i}s_{i}s=D^{\prime}s=\id
precisely for the first element of
s\in S^{\prime}
and not for any other, while
D^{\prime}d_{i}s_{j_{1}}\cdots s_{j_{m}}s=D^{\prime}s_{j_{1}-1}\cdots s_{j_{m}-1}d_{i}
for
j_{k}>i
cannot be the identity.
∎

4.4.

The Lemma is usually stated in a weaker form saying “the set of sections of a degeneracy
s:[m]\twoheadleftarrow[n]
is non-empty and determines
s
”.
This follows obviously from the previous Lemma, because it shows non-emptyness for
\#S=1
, and, for
\#S=2
, that for each pair of degeneracies there is a section of the first which is not a section of the second.

Lemma 4.5

(Eilenberg-Zilber)
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is Barr-exact and has a zero object (we will be interested in
\Set_{\bullet}
and Abelian Categories only), then

\gr^{i}X_{n}\cong\coprod_{s:[m]\twoheadleftarrow[n]}X_{m}^{\nd}

(36)

where the coproduct runs over all degeneracies.

Proof.

We will show this for
\mathcal{C}
Abelian (by suitably embedding a suitable small subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
, this actually suffices, but we won’t discuss the technical details).
Consider the morphism

\bigoplus_{s:[m]\twoheadleftarrow[n]}X_{m}^{\nd}\to F^{m}X_{n}/F^{m-1}X_{n}.

It is an epimorphism by definition of the filtration, so we have to see that it is a monomorphism. Let
K
be the kernel. If it is non-trivial, consider a minimal subset
S\subset\{s:[m]\twoheadleftarrow[n]\}
with the property that

K\subset\bigoplus_{s\in S}X_{m}^{\nd}.

By the Lemma, there is
D
such that
Ds=\id
precisely for the smallest
s\in S
. All other
Ds
factor thus through a degeneracy and hence the composition with
D

\bigoplus_{s\in S}X_{m}^{\nd}\to F^{m}X_{n}/F^{m-1}X_{n}\to X_{m}^{\nd}

maps the first (w.r.t. the lexicographic ordering)
X_{m}^{\nd}
isomorphically to
X_{m}^{\nd}
and all others to zero
25
25

25

Note that
D
must map
F^{i-1}X_{n}
to
F^{i-1}X_{m}
and hence the morphism is well-defined.
. Thus

K\subset\bigoplus_{s^{\prime}\in S\setminus\{s\}}X_{m}^{\nd}

in contradiction to the minimality. This shows that
K
is trivial.
∎

Let
X\in\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}
be a simplicial set.
Applied to
\mathcal{C}:=\Set_{\bullet}
, the category of pointed sets, and
X_{\bullet}
, the statement of Lemma
4.5
translates to

X_{n}=\coprod_{m}F^{m}X_{n}\setminus F^{m-1}X_{n}\cong\coprod_{[m]\to[n]}\underbrace{X_{m}\setminus F^{m-1}X_{m}}_{=:X_{m}^{\nd}},

i.e. that every element in

X_{n}
is in the image of the non-degenerate elements of
X_{m}
for a unique degeneracy
[m]\twoheadleftarrow[n]
. (This follows even more directly from the weaker form
4.4
of Lemma
4.3
.)

The properties discussed here are shared by other diagrams than
\Delta^{\op}
as well. These are condensed, for example, in the notions of
elegant Reedy category
and
Eilenberg-Zilber category

[
12
,
13
]
.

4.2
Some basic (co)operads

This section discusses the following zoo of very basic (
\infty
-exponential) operads over
\mathcal{O}
:

\textstyle{(\FinSet_{\emptyset},\coprod)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{(\Delta_{\emptyset},\coprod)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{(\Delta_{\emptyset},\ast)={}^{\downarrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\dec}}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{forget}}
\textstyle{(\Delta_{\emptyset}^{\op},\ast)={}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\op}}
\textstyle{(\FinSet,\coprod)}
\textstyle{(\Delta,\coprod)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\delta}}
\textstyle{(\Delta,\ast)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\dec}}
\scriptstyle{\AW^{\op}=\mathrm{forget}}

(37)

These operads (or their opposites) will occur in various related and unrelated places, most notably:

1.

The cooperad
(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}
describes the canonical simplicial enrichment on simplicial objects in a category
\mathcal{C}
in a different way, which is very convenient to
construct homotopies. See, in particular, Proposition
4.25
.

2.

The cooperads
(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}
and
(\Delta,\coprod)^{\op}
give, via Day convolution, rise to the two different tensor products
\otimes
(point-wise for simplicial objects) and
\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}
(usual tensor product of complexes)
on simplicial objects = non-negatively graded complexes in Abelian categories (cf.
4.16
). The forgetful map gives rise to the Alexander-Whitney map. In fact, the diagram above extends (cf. Theorem
5.15
) in the case of
\Ab
-enriched operads so as to include a model
of the Eilenberg-Zilber map as well.

3.

The morphism
\rho^{\op}
can be described by generators and relations (cf. Lemma
4.13
) and plays an important role in the (co)bar construction (cf. Theorem
3.12
), more precisely, it takes care of the augmentation.

4.

The product in
(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}
, also denoted
\dec
(decalage), connects (via pullback
\dec^{*}
) the classical cobar construction (Definition
3.5
) with the geometric cobar construction (or Kan’s loop group), and Adams cobar construction, respectively. Dually,
its adjoint
\dec_{*}
(Artin-Mazur codiagonal, or total complex in the Abelian case) connects the classical bar construction with the classifying space, and Eilenberg-MacLane bar construction, respectively.

4.6.

In the diagram (
37
) the
i
’s are
\infty
-Cartesian (for the right hand side
i
this is Lemma
4.8
, for the others it is trivial), the
\iota_{\delta}
’s are
\infty
-Cartesian, and the
\iota_{\dec}
’s are 1-coCartesian (for the upper trivial, it is even
\infty
-coCartesian, while for the lower, it boils down to the 1-finality of
\iota^{\op}
, Lemma
A.6
.)

4.7.

Consider the category
\FinSet_{\emptyset}
of finite sets. It is monoidal w.r.t. to the coproduct
\coprod
giving rise to an operad

(\FinSet_{\emptyset},\coprod).

Since
\coprod:\FinSet_{\emptyset}\times\FinSet_{\emptyset}\to\FinSet_{\emptyset}
has the right adjoint
\delta:\FinSet_{\emptyset}\to\FinSet_{\emptyset}\times\FinSet_{\emptyset}

the morphism

(\FinSet_{\emptyset},\coprod)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}

is thus a
fibration and cofibration
of operads.
On
\Delta_{\emptyset}
(totally ordered finite sets) there is the concatenation product
\ast
(obtained from
\coprod
by choosing one of the two canonical total orderings) which is not symmetric anymore.

\Delta_{\emptyset}
can also be turned into an operad
(\Delta_{\emptyset},\coprod)
setting
\Hom(x,y;z)=\Hom(x,z)\times\Hom(y,z)
. This operad is not monoidal, of course, because
\Delta_{\emptyset}
does not have a coproduct, but

(\Delta_{\emptyset},\coprod)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}

is also a
fibration
of operads.
There are two morphisms of operads:

\iota_{\delta}:(\Delta_{\emptyset},\coprod)\to(\FinSet_{\emptyset},\coprod)

which is a morphism of fibrations (i.e. maps Cartesian (active) morphisms to Cartesian morphisms) and

\iota_{\dec}:(\Delta_{\emptyset},\ast)\to(\FinSet_{\emptyset},\coprod)

which is a morphism of cofibrations (i.e. maps coCartesian (active) morphisms to coCartesian morphisms).

The concatenation product
\ast
restricts from
\Delta_{\emptyset}
to
\Delta
, but the unit doesn’t.
However,
(\Delta,\ast)
is still pro-monoidal (even in the
\infty
-categorical sense), or in other words:

Lemma 4.8
.

The restriction

i:(\Delta,\ast)\hookrightarrow(\Delta_{\emptyset},\ast)

is
\infty
-Cartesian and thus (cf. Lemma
2.45
)
(\Delta,\ast)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
is an
\infty
-exponential fibration of operads.
Furthermore, the morphism
(\Delta,\ast)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
is
\infty
-Cartesian.

Proof.

Let
\alpha:[n]\leftarrow[m]\in\Delta^{\op}_{\act}
be an active morphism.
For the pull-back pro-functors
\alpha^{\bullet}
in
(\Delta,\ast)
, and
\alpha^{\bullet}_{\emptyset}
in
(\Delta_{\emptyset},\ast)
, we have

\alpha^{\bullet}={}^{t}i_{m}\alpha^{\bullet}_{\emptyset}i_{n}.

Since the latter is a cofibration, the
\alpha^{\bullet}_{\emptyset}
are of the form

{}^{t}\beta
. They are, in particular, determined by
(\delta_{1})^{\bullet}_{\emptyset}={}^{t}\dec_{\emptyset}
and
(s_{0})^{\bullet}_{\emptyset}={}^{t}[\emptyset]
where
\dec_{\emptyset}=\ast:\Delta_{\emptyset}\times\Delta_{\emptyset}\to\Delta_{\emptyset}
is
the monoidal product and
[\emptyset]:[\emptyset]\hookrightarrow\Delta_{\emptyset}
is the embedding.

\dec_{\emptyset}
restricts to a morphism
\dec:\Delta\times\Delta\to\Delta
. Thus
(\delta_{1})^{\bullet}={}^{t}\!\dec
.
Furthermore
[\emptyset]:[\emptyset]\hookrightarrow\Delta_{\emptyset}
has the right adjoint
\pi_{\emptyset}:\Delta_{\emptyset}\to\cdot
(because
[\emptyset]
is initial). Therefore
{}^{t}[\emptyset]=\pi_{\emptyset}
.
This restricts to
\pi:\Delta\to\cdot
, i.e.
(s_{0})^{\bullet}=\pi
.
i
being
\infty
-Cartesian boils down to

i\,{}^{t}\!\dec_{\infty}\cong{}^{t}\!\dec\,i\qquad\pi\cong\pi_{\emptyset}\,i

The second is trivial, while the first is shown (dually) in Lemma
A.5
.

The
\infty
-Cartesianity of
(\Delta,\ast)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
is trivial over degeneracies (because locally Cartesian morphisms exist) while for
\ast
, it is the
\infty
-finality (cf. (dually) Lemma
A.3
).
∎

Dually, we obtain, that

(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}

is an
\infty
-exponential fibration of cooperads.
Notice that
(\Delta_{\emptyset},\ast)^{\op}=(\Delta_{\emptyset}^{\op},\ast)^{\vee}
is again the cooperad associated with a monoidal category, namely
\Delta_{\emptyset}^{\op}
with the dual structure.
Restricting the
operad

(\Delta_{\emptyset}^{\op},\ast)
to
\Delta^{\op}
though gives an operad, which is somewhat unnatural, and is never considered.

Lemma 4.9

(Duality between (finite) ordered sets and intervals)
.

There is an equivalence of operads (or, equivalently, monoidal categories):

(\Delta_{\act}^{\op},\ast^{\prime})\cong(\Delta_{\emptyset},\ast)

given by
26
26

26

The total ordering on
\Hom_{\Delta_{\emptyset}}([n],[1])
is the natural one with minimal element the constant morphism
0
and with maximal element the constant morphism
1
. The one on
\Hom_{\Delta_{\act}}([n],[1])
is its restriction.

[n]\mapsto\Hom_{\Delta_{\act}}([n],[1])\cong[n-1]

[m]\mapsto\Hom_{\Delta_{\emptyset}}([m],[1])\cong[m+1]

This equivalence exchanges face and degeneracy maps.

Proof.

A combinatorial exercise.
∎

4.10.

Recall from Section
2.6
the notions of (twisted) arrow operads
{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I
and
{}^{\downarrow\downarrow}I
for an operad
I
.
For
I=\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
those are, being cofibered over
I
, in particular monoidal categories, and
we have isomorphisms

{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}\cong(\Delta_{\act},*^{\prime})\cong(\Delta_{\emptyset}^{\op},*)

(38)

and also

{}^{\downarrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}\cong(\Delta_{\act}^{\op},*^{\prime})\cong(\Delta_{\emptyset},*)

in such a way that (for the first isomorphism) a multi-morphism
[n_{0}],\dots,[n_{k}]\to[m]
in
(\Delta_{\act},*^{\prime})
corresponding to

[n_{0}]\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}[n_{k}]\rightarrow[m]

(using the arrow direction in
\Delta^{\op}
) is mapped to

\textstyle{[n_{1}]\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}[n_{k}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces[m]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[k]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[1]}

with the obvious maps.
In the other direction an active morphism in
{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
respresented by a diagram

\textstyle{[x]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces[m]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[k]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[1]}

determines a decomposition of
[x]
into
[x_{1}]\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}[x_{k}]
, and is mapped to
the corresponding multi-morphism
[x_{1}],\dots,[x_{k}]\to[m]
in
(\Delta_{\act},*^{\prime})
.

The category (of operators)
({}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$})_{\act}\cong({}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\Delta_{\act})\cong{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\Delta_{\emptyset}
is essentially
the category of necklaces
, cf.
[
20
,
50
,
7
]
). It is itself
monoidal, which can be expressed easiest by saying that

{}^{\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}

(or dually
{}^{\downarrow\uparrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
) is cofibered, and hence the left hand side is monoidal and its fiber over
[1]
is the category
({}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$})_{\act}
.
The associated monoidal product is just
\ast^{\prime}
applied to source and destination of the morphism.
All that was said can be translated replacing (via Lemma
4.9
)
(\Delta_{\act}^{\op},*^{\prime})
by the perhaps more tractable
(\Delta_{\emptyset},*)
everywhere.
For instance, the process of associating to

[x-1]\leftarrow[k-1]

a decomposition
[x_{1}-1],\dots,[x_{k}-1]
is then just
collecting preimages
of the morphism in
\Delta_{\emptyset}
.

The functor

\displaystyle\Xi_{\emptyset}:\Delta_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle\to\Cat

\displaystyle[n]

\displaystyle\mapsto(\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset})^{n+1}

classifying the operad (
38
) (as covariant functor) but composing into
\Cat^{\PF}
, it is rather associated with the cooperad functor:

(\Delta_{\emptyset},\ast)^{\op}\cong{}^{\uparrow\uparrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}

There is also the functor

\displaystyle\Xi:\Delta_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle\to\Cat^{\PF}

\displaystyle[n]

\displaystyle\mapsto(\Delta^{\op})^{n+1}

where the transitions are the
\dec
and the
{}^{t}\!\pr
. It is associated with the cooperad
(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

All functors in the image of
\Xi_{\emptyset}
are cofibrations with discrete fibers.

Proof.

They are of the form

(\Delta_{\emptyset}\times_{/\Delta_{\emptyset}}[n])^{\op}\to(\Delta_{\emptyset}\times_{/\Delta_{\emptyset}}[m])^{\op}

given by composition with
[n]\to[m]
.
∎

4.12.

Denote by
(\Delta_{\act}^{\op},\ast^{\prime})^{\vee}
the associated cooperad, i.e.

\Hom([n];[k_{0}],\dots,[k_{m}])=\Hom_{\Delta_{\act}^{\op}}([n],[k_{0}]\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}[k_{m}]).

The sets of 0-ary morphisms are
\Hom([n];)=\emptyset
for
n>0
and
\Hom([0];)=\{\rho_{0}\}
with
\rho_{0}
corresponding to
\Delta_{0}=\Delta_{0}
.
The transformation (
14
) induces (by composition) a functor of cooperads

\rho:(\Delta_{\emptyset},\ast)^{\vee}\cong(\Delta_{\act}^{\op},\ast^{\prime})^{\vee}\rightarrow(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}.

Lemma 4.13
.

(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}
is obtained from
(\Delta_{\act}^{\op},\ast^{\prime})^{\vee}
by adjoining a
0-ary morphism

\rho_{1}\in\Hom([1];)

subject to the relation that there be only one 0-ary morphism from any object:
\Hom([n];)=\{\rho_{n}\}
(including
n=0
).

Notice that there are new 1-ary morphisms introduced, for example the unique morphism in
\Hom([1];[1],[1])
composed with
\rho_{1}
at one of the slots.

Proof.

In the category obtained from
(\Delta_{\act}^{\op},\ast^{\prime})^{\vee}
, as described in the Lemma, we have
\rho_{n}=\rho_{1}p_{n}
where
p_{n}:[n]\leftarrow[1]
is the
morphism to the final object in
\Delta_{\act}^{\op}
(also for
n=0
).
The category
thus has multi-morphisms

\Hom([m];[k_{0}],\dots,[k_{n}])=\{[m]\leftarrow[l_{0}]\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}[l_{s}]\}

where the
k_{i}
are an ordered subset of the
l_{i}
, such that no two consecutive elements are missing, and such that all missing
l_{i}
are equal to
1
.
We can map it to the unique morphism in
\Hom_{(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}}([m];[k_{0}],\dots,[k_{n}])
corresponding to

[m]\leftarrow[l_{0}]\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}[l_{s}]\leftarrow[k_{0}]\ast\cdots\ast[k_{n}]

mapping the
[k_{i}]
to the corresponding factor. If there is no
k_{i}
then we map it to the unique 0-ary morphism in
\Hom_{(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}}([m];)
.

On the other hand, consider an
n
-ary morphism in
\Hom_{(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}}([m];[k_{0}],\dots,[k_{n}])
given by

\alpha:[m]\leftarrow[k_{0}]\ast\cdots\ast[k_{n}].

Now add factors
[1]
everywhere, where the condition that the extremal points go to the same point, resp. to the inital and final points, is violated:

[m]\leftarrow([1])\ast[k_{0}]\ast([1])\ast\cdots\ast([1])\ast[k_{n}]\ast([1]).

This morphism factors

\displaystyle[m]\leftarrow([1])\ast^{\prime}[k_{0}]\ast^{\prime}([1])\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}([1])\ast^{\prime}[n_{k}]\ast^{\prime}([1])

\displaystyle\leftarrow([1])\ast[k_{0}]\ast([1])\ast\cdots\ast([1])\ast[k_{n}]\ast([1])

and thus corresponds to a multi-morphism in

\Hom_{(\Delta_{\act}^{\op},\ast^{\prime})^{\vee}}([m];([1]),[k_{0}],([1]),\dots,([1]),[k_{n}],([1]))

Compose this with at the various
[1]
with the
\rho_{1}:[1]\rightarrow\emptyset
adjoint, to yield a morphism

[n]\to[k_{0}],\dots,[k_{n}].

Finally, the unique 0-ary morphism in
\Hom([n];)
in
(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}
is mapped to
\rho_{n}
.
The two constructions obviously determine an isomorphism of cooperads.
∎

Corollary 4.14
.

For any monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
in which the unit is a final object,
\rho^{*}
induces an isomorphism

(\mathcal{C}^{\vee})^{(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}}\cong(\mathcal{C}^{\vee})^{(\Delta_{\act}^{\op},\ast^{\prime})^{\vee}}.

Proof.

That is an obvious consequence of Lemma
4.13
in the 1-categorical case but needs some careful argument for
\infty
-categories that is omitted for the moment.
∎

4.15.

(\FinSet_{\emptyset},\coprod)
restricts to
(\FinSet,\coprod)
, not as cofibration over
\mathcal{O}
(the unit is lacking) but as fibration, in particular still
\infty
-exponential.
Similarly
(\Delta_{\emptyset},\coprod)
restricts to
(\Delta,\coprod)
as a fibration and
(\Delta_{\emptyset},\ast)
restricts to
(\Delta,\ast)
, which is neither a cofibration
nor a fibration, but
\infty
-exponential by Lemma
4.8
. Furthermore there are obvious forgetful maps

(\Delta_{\emptyset},\ast)\to(\Delta_{\emptyset},\coprod)
etc.

4.16.

Cf. also Example
2.38
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a finitely complete category with monoidal product
\otimes
commuting with finite limits. It defines a cooperad

(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee}.

We will be interested in the Day convolution cooperads

D((\Delta,\ast)^{\op},(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee})\qquad\text{and}\qquad D((\Delta,\coprod)^{\op},(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee}).

By Proposition
2.30
, those are again cooperads on
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
associated with monoidal products

-\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}-:=\dec_{*}-\boxtimes-\qquad\text{and}\qquad-\otimes-:=\delta^{*}-\boxtimes-,

while in both cases the unit is given by
\pi^{*}1
, where
1
is the unit in
\mathcal{C}
. Note that finitely complete is sufficient for the monoidality, because
\dec_{*}
is effectively computed by finite limits (see Proposition
4.89
and also
5.39
).
The product
-\otimes-
is just the point-wise extension of
\otimes
on
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
.
If
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)
is an Abelian tensor category, then
-\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}-
translates via Dold-Kan into the usual tensor product of complexes (and commutation with finite products is sufficient). This will be discussed at length in Section
5.2
.

4.17.

The cooperad
(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}
is always fibered over
\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}
(i.e. comes from a monoidal product), regardless of any properties of
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)
. In fact, this is the situation of
4.16
.
We can thus equally well describe the
operad

(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)=D((\Delta^{\op},\coprod),(\mathcal{C},\otimes))

as a Day convolution.
Notice that
((\Delta,\coprod)^{\op})^{\vee}=(\Delta^{\op},\coprod)
.
The
operad

(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})
, however, cannot be described as a Day convolution
27
27

27

Notice that it involves morphisms departing from a
right
Kan extension.
.

4.3
Canonical enrichments in pre-sheaves

Let
I
be a small category and
let
\mathcal{C}
a category. We have recalled in

2.15
that
\mathcal{C}^{I^{\op}}
has a canonical enrichment in pre-sheaves

\Set^{I^{\op}}
(with particularly easy left tensors — if
\mathcal{C}
is cocomplete — namely just given by the point-wise tensoring of
\mathcal{C}
with
\Set
).
There is a second description of the same enrichment, which will be very convenient in Section
4.5
to construct homotopies, as, for example, the homotopy between
\Ez\Aw
and
\id
.
In these applications, we will always have
I=\Delta
and the second description amounts to the fact that

\boxed{\underline{\Hom}(X,Y)_{[n]}\cong\Hom_{\mathcal{C}^{(\Delta^{\op})^{n+1}}}(\dec_{n+1}^{*}X,\dec_{n+1}^{*}Y)}

and thus (under suitable (co)completeness assumptions)

\Delta_{n}\otimes X
can also be described as
\dec_{n+1,!}\dec_{n+1}^{*}X
with
the functoriality in
\Delta_{n}
(essentially) given by certain adjunction units, and
\mathcal{HOM}(\Delta_{n},X)
as
\dec_{n+1,*}\dec_{n+1}^{*}X
.
This second description is well-known (cf.
[
48
]
) but seems not to be widely used in the literature.

Definition 4.18
.

Let
I
be a small category with final object
i
.
Consider a functor

F:I^{\op}\to\Cat

and define

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}_{F}:I^{\op}\times F(i)^{\op}\times F(i)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\Set

\displaystyle(j,X,Y)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\Hom_{F(j)}(F(p_{j})X,F(p_{j})Y)

where
p_{j}:j\to i
denotes the unique morphism to
i
.

Proposition 4.19
.

The
\underline{\Hom}_{F}
of Definition
4.18
, defines an enrichment on
F(i)
in
\Set^{I^{\op}}
w.r.t. the point-wise
product

\Set^{I^{\op}}\times\Set^{I^{\op}}\to\Set^{I^{\op}}.

If the functors w.r.t. all
p_{j}
for all objects in
I
have a left (resp. right adjoint), and
F(i)
is cocomplete (resp. complete) then this is left, resp. right tensored.

More generally a functor

F:I^{\op}\to\Cat_{/J}\qquad\text{or}\qquad F:I^{\op}\to\mathrm{(co)Op}_{/J}

gives rise to a
\Set^{I^{\op}}
enriched category or (co)operad
F(i)\to J
.
If the
F(i)\to J
are (co)fibrations, then this only yields again a (co)fibration of simplicially enriched categories (i.e. induces a structure of simplicially enriched functor on the pull-backs, resp. push-forwards) if
F
has
values in (co)fibrations and (co)Cartesian functors.

Proof.

For fixed
j
the morphisms in
\underline{\Hom}_{F}(-,-)
can obviously be composed and the composition is functorial in
j
.
If all
F(p_{j})
have left adjoints
G(p_{j})
, then we can define

\displaystyle I\times F(i)

\displaystyle\to F(i)

\displaystyle j,X

\displaystyle\mapsto j\ltimes X:=G(p_{j})F(p_{j})X

This gives rise to a left tensoring defined by

\displaystyle\Set^{I^{\op}}\times F(i)

\displaystyle\to F(i)

\displaystyle C,X

\displaystyle\mapsto\int^{i}C(i)\times(i\ltimes X)

4.20.

If the
F(p_{j})
have left adjoints
G(p_{j})
, then the isomorphisms

F(\alpha)F(p_{k})\Rightarrow F(p_{j})
for
\alpha:j\to k
yield morphisms
j\ltimes X\to k\ltimes X
by means of applying Yoneda to the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Hom(F(p_{k})X,F(p_{k})Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Hom(F(\alpha)F(p_{k})X,F(\alpha)F(p_{k})Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Hom(k\ltimes X,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Hom(j\ltimes X,Y)}

Note that the upper horizontal arrow is the functoriality of the construction in the last Proposition.
These morphisms can be described equivalently as follows:

1.

G(p_{k})F(p_{k})\cong G(p_{k})F(\alpha)F(p_{j})\Rightarrow G(p_{j})F(p_{j})

where
G(p_{k})F(\alpha)\Rightarrow G(p_{j})
is the mate of
F(\alpha)F(p_{k})\cong F(p_{j})
.

2.

If the
F(\alpha)
also have left adjoints then this may be described even more easily as

G(p_{k})F(p_{k})\cong G(p_{j})G(\alpha)F(\alpha)F(p_{j})\Rightarrow G(p_{j})F(p_{j})

given by the counit
G(\alpha)F(\alpha)\to\id
.

We leave the proof as exercise.

4.21.

Let

F,F^{\prime}:I^{\op}\to\Cat

be functors and assume that the
F(p_{j})
(resp.
F^{\prime}(p_{j})
) have left adjoints
G(p_{j})
(resp.
G^{\prime}(p_{j})
).
A natural transformation

\mu:F^{\prime}\Rightarrow F

yields a natural transformation

k\ltimes\mu X\rightarrow\mu(k\ltimes X)

defined as the composition

\textstyle{G(p_{k})F(p_{k})\mu(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{G(p_{k})\mu(i)F^{\prime}(p_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mu(k)G^{\prime}(p_{k})F^{\prime}(p_{k})}

where the right hand side morphism is the mate of the isomorphism featuring in the natural transformation.

Lemma 4.22
.

The transformation
k\ltimes(\mu-)\Rightarrow\mu(k\ltimes-)
defined in
4.21
is natural in
k
, even if not all
F(\alpha)
have left adjoints.

Proof.

We have to show that the outer square in

\textstyle{G(p_{k})F(p_{k})\mu\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{G(p_{k})\mu F^{\prime}(p_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mu G^{\prime}(p_{k})F^{\prime}(p_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{G(p_{k})F(\alpha)F(p_{l})\mu\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(p_{k})F(\alpha)iF^{\prime}(p_{l})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(p_{k})\mu F^{\prime}(\alpha)F^{\prime}(p_{l})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mu G^{\prime}(p_{k})F^{\prime}(\alpha)F^{\prime}(p_{l})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(p_{l})F(p_{l})\mu\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(p_{l})\mu F^{\prime}(p_{l})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mu G^{\prime}(p_{l})F^{\prime}(p_{l})}

commutes. Here obviously everything commutes, except

\textstyle{G(p_{k})F(\alpha)\mu\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(p_{k})\mu F^{\prime}(\alpha)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mu G^{\prime}(p_{k})F^{\prime}(\alpha)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(p_{l})\mu\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mu G^{\prime}(p_{l}).}

This is an exercise with mates, that is left to the reader.
∎

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
I
be a small category with a final object
i
and let
\mathcal{C}
be a category.
Consider the functor

\displaystyle f:I

\displaystyle\to\Cat

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle\mapsto(I\times_{/I}j)^{\op}

classifying the fibration
{}^{\uparrow\uparrow}I\to I^{\op}
(or, equivalently, the cofibration
{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I\to I
). It yields via composition with
L_{\mathcal{C}}
(see
2.3
) a functor

F:=L_{\mathcal{C}}\circ f^{\op}:I^{\op}\to\Cat.

Notice that
\mathcal{C}
does not have to be cocomplete, because the composition consists entirely of pull-backs.
The enrichment in
\Set^{I^{\op}}
obtained by this functor
F
in Definition
4.18
is naturally isomorphic to the canonical enrichment in pre-sheaves (
2.15
) (i.e. to the one right adjoint to the
point-wise tensoring, if the latter exists), given by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}^{I^{\op}})^{\op}\times(\mathcal{C}^{I^{\op}})

\displaystyle\to\Set^{I^{\op}}

\displaystyle C,D

\displaystyle\mapsto j\mapsto\int_{k}\Hom(\Hom_{I}(k,j),\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(C(k),D(k)).

Proof.

We have

\Hom(p_{j}^{*}C,p_{j}^{*}D)\cong\lim_{k^{\prime}\to k\to j\in{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}(I\times_{/I}j)}\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(C(k),D(k^{\prime}))

and there is a canonical isomorphism, functorial in
j
,
F
and
G
:

\lim_{k^{\prime}\to k\to j\in{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}(I\times_{/I}j)}\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(C(k),D(k^{\prime}))\cong\lim_{k^{\prime}\to k\in{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I}\Hom(\Hom_{I}(k,j),\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(C(k),D(k^{\prime}))

because
{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}(I\times_{/I}j)\to{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I
is a fibration with discrete fibers
\Hom_{I}(k,j)
.
∎

4.24.

Applying Proposition
4.23
to the functor (cf.
4.10
)

\Xi_{\emptyset}:\Delta_{\emptyset}\to\Cat

with
\Xi_{\emptyset}([0])=\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}
, classifying the monoidal cooperad
(\Delta_{\emptyset},\ast)^{\op}
,
we see that the enrichment on
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}}
defined by

[n]\mapsto\Hom(\dec_{\emptyset,n+1}^{*}X,\dec_{\emptyset,n+1}^{*}Y)

(i.e. obtained by letting
F:=L_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\Xi_{\emptyset}^{\op}
in Definition
4.18
)
is the same as the canonical enrichment in
\Set^{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}}
.

Now assume that
\mathcal{C}
is cocomplete.
We have seen (cf.
4.10
), that there is a functor

\Xi:\Delta_{\emptyset}\to\Cat^{\PF}

with
\Xi([0])=\Delta^{\op}
, classifying the
\infty
-exponential cooperad
(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}
.
We have a transformation

{}^{t}\!i:\Xi_{\emptyset}\Rightarrow\Xi

which is natural (and not only oplax) by Lemma
4.8
.
By applying
L_{\mathcal{C}}
it gives a functor

L_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\Xi^{\op}\circ i:\Delta^{\op}\to\Cat

and a natural transformation

L_{\mathcal{C}}(i):L_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\Xi^{\op}\circ i\Rightarrow L_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\Xi^{\op}_{\emptyset}\circ i

and the the enrichment on
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
defined by
28
28

28

Note that, although this seems similar to before, the face maps now act as partial colimits!

[n]\mapsto\Hom(\dec_{n+1}^{*}X,\dec_{n+1}^{*}Y)

(i.e. obtained by letting
F:=L_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\Xi^{\op}\circ i
in Definition
4.18
) is
again
the same
as the canonical enrichment in
\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}
:

Proposition 4.25
.

The structure on
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
obtained by letting
F:=L_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\Xi^{\op}\circ i
in Definition
4.18
is again naturally isomorphic to the canonical enrichment (
2.15
) in pre-sheaves (i.e., here, simplicial sets).

Proof.

Cf.
[
48
]
for a similar proof (for
n=1
).
From Lemma
4.22
applied to the natural transformation
L_{\mathcal{C}}(i)
(
4.24
), we get a morphism
functorial in
n

[n]\ltimes(i_{!}-)=\dec_{\emptyset,n+1,!}\dec_{\emptyset,n+1}^{*}i_{!}\cong\dec_{\emptyset,n+1,!}i_{!}\dec_{n+1}^{*}\cong i_{!}\dec_{n+1,!}\dec_{n+1}^{*}=i_{!}([n]\ltimes-)

which is an
isomorphism
(because the mate is just the adjoint of the equality
\dec_{n+1}^{*}i^{*}=i^{*}\dec_{\emptyset,n+1}^{*}
).
We have thus functorially in
[n]\in\Delta^{\op}
:

\begin{array}[b]{llll}\underline{\Hom}_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}}(X,Y)_{[n]}&\cong&\underline{\Hom}_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}}}(i_{!}X,i_{*}Y)_{[n]}&\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{LEMMAHOM}, 2.}\\
&\cong&\Hom_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}}}([n]\ltimes(i_{!}X),i_{*}Y)&\text{Proposition\penalty\ \ref{PROPENRICHTWARROW}}\\
&\cong&\Hom_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}}}(i_{!}([n]\ltimes X),i_{*}Y)&\text{as seen above}\\
&\cong&\Hom_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}}([n]\ltimes X,Y)&\text{$i$ is fully-faithful.}\end{array}\qed

Remark 4.26
.

The enrichment of
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
in
\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}
given by procedure
4.18

(i.e. applied to
L_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\Xi^{\op}
instead of
L_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\Xi^{\op}\circ i
)
is in fact an enrichment in
\Set^{\Delta_{\emptyset}^{\op}}
. It follows from
the definition, that we have

\underline{\Hom}(X,Y)_{[-1]}=\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\pi_{!}X,\pi_{!}Y).

Corollary 4.27
.

If a left tensoring exists,
the diagram of functors

\textstyle{\id\cong\pr_{i,!}\dec^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dec_{!}\dec^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\id}

is canonically equivalent to the diagram

4.28.

The isomorphism (cf. Corollary
4.27
)

\Hom(\dec^{*}X,\dec^{*}Y)\cong\Hom(\Delta_{1}\otimes X,Y)

can be made very explicit:
A morphism

F:\dec^{*}X\to\dec^{*}Y

which is in components given by

F_{[i],[j]}:X_{[i]\ast[j]}\to Y_{[i]\ast[j]}

also determines morphisms
F_{[-1],[j]}
and
F_{[i],[-1]}
via the colimit.

In turn, an element

G:\Delta_{1}\times X\to Y

is given in degree
n
by

(G_{[n],i})_{i}:\Hom_{\Delta^{\op}}([1],[n])\times X_{[n]}\to Y_{[n]}

where
i=-1,\dots,n
index the elements of
\Hom_{\Delta^{\op}}([1],[n])
.
If
F
and
G
correspond under the isomorphism above, we have explicitly:

\boxed{G_{[n],i}=F_{[i],[n-i-1]}}.

This can be seen, for instance, from the formula

(\dec_{!}K)_{[n]}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=n-1\\
i,j\geq-1\end{subarray}}K_{[i],[j]}

established in Proposition
4.89
below.

There is a monoidal version of the above:

Proposition 4.29
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)
be a cocomplete monoidal category such that
\otimes
commutes with colimits.
The category of simplicial objects is equipped with the point-wise monodial structure. The corresponding operad
(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)
can also be seen as the
Day convolution
D((\Delta^{\op},\coprod),(\mathcal{C},\otimes))
, and the corresponding cooperad
(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}
as the Day convolution
D((\Delta,\coprod)^{\op},(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee})
(cf.
4.16
).
The simplicially enriched structure on
\otimes
given by

K\otimes(X\otimes Y)\to(K\otimes X)\otimes(K\otimes Y)

(39)

induced by the diagonal
K\to K\times K
(all
\otimes
are computed point-wise) can be equally given (using the procedure in Definition
4.18
above) by a
functor:

\Xi:\Delta_{\emptyset}\to\Hom^{1-\oplax,1-\inert-\pseudo}(\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op},(\Cat^{\PF},\times)^{\vee})

with
\Xi([0])
classifying
(\Delta^{\op},\coprod)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
(
\Xi
consists actually of natural transformation — not only oplax)
and applying
L_{(\mathcal{C},\otimes)}
(Proposition
2.42
). Likewise, it can be given by a functor

\Xi^{\vee}:\Delta_{\emptyset}\to\Hom^{1-\oplax,1-\inert-\pseudo}(\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$},(\Cat^{\PF},\times))

with
\Xi^{\vee}([0])
classifying
(\Delta,\coprod)^{\op}\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}
(
\Xi^{\vee}
does not consist of natural transformations) and applying
L_{(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee}}
(Proposition
2.42
).

Proof.

We need to observe that the exponential operad
(\Delta^{\op},\coprod)
extends to a functor

\Xi:\Delta_{\emptyset}\to\Hom^{1-\oplax,1-\inert-\pseudo}(\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op},(\Cat^{\PF},\times)^{\vee})

or, in other words, that there is a functor
29
29

29

Shifting slightly the perspective and considering
\Delta_{\emptyset}
instead of the usual
\Delta_{\act}^{\op}
via Lemma
4.9
.

\Xi:\Delta_{\emptyset}\times\Delta_{\emptyset}^{\op}\to\Cat^{\PF}

sending

to products in both variables, and roughly speaking, encoding the operad
\mathbb{Z}[(\Delta^{\op},\coprod)]
vertically and the cooperad

For this one one has to construct the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{(\Delta^{\op})^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\delta_{12,34}}
\textstyle{(\Delta^{\op})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\dec_{13,24}}
\textstyle{(\Delta^{\op})^{2}}

which is obvious, and

\textstyle{(\Delta^{\op})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{(\Delta^{\op})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The fact that this yields the structure defined by (

For this functor all diagrams commute (not only oplaxly), i.e. it is morphism-wise Cartesian, reflecting the fact that this yields a simplicially enriched structure on the push-forward
\otimes
.
The functor

\Xi^{\vee}:\Delta_{\emptyset}\to\Hom^{1-\oplax,1-\inert-\pseudo}(\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$},(\Cat^{\PF},\times))

is formed by applying

in the

∎

4.4
Naive homotopy

4.30.

Recall the notions of
simplicially enriched category, functor, and natural transformation
(see, for instance
[
37
]
).
Let
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{D}
be simplicially enriched categories, and
\mu:A\Rightarrow B
a natural transformation between simplicially enriched functors.

\mu
is simplicially enriched, if

\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}(i,j)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{A}
\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}(A(i),A(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu(j)\circ}
\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}(B(i),B(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ\mu(i)}
\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}(A(i),B(j))}

commutes. The set of simplicially enriched natural transformations can be enhanced to a simplicial set turning the category
\Fun(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})

into a simplicially enriched category, as follows:

4.31.

Given a simplicially enriched category
\mathcal{C}
,
for each simplicial set
K
, we may define a simplicially enriched category
\mathcal{C}_{K}
with the same objects and morphisms

\underline{\Hom}(K,\underline{\Hom}(X,Y))=\underline{\Hom}(K\times X,Y)

with composition induced by the diagonal
K\to K\times K
.
There is a simplicially enriched inclusion functor

\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{K}

given by composition with
K\to\Delta_{0}
and a
simplicially enriched functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
induces a functor

F_{K}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{K}.

For a second such functor
G
there is a simplicial set
\underline{\Nat}(F,G)
characterized by

\Hom(K,\underline{\Nat}(F,G))=\Hom(F_{K},G_{K})

which can also be expressed using the
enriched
end:

\underline{\Nat}(F,G)\cong\int_{c}\underline{\Hom}(F(c),G(c)).

This can also be seen as the natural enriched
\Hom
-object in the enriched functor category of functors from
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
.

4.32.

Let
I
be a small category or (co)operad and let
\mathcal{C}\to I
and
\mathcal{D}\to I
be simplicially enriched cofibrations (of categories or (co)operads) classified by functors

\Xi_{\mathcal{C}},\Xi_{\mathcal{D}}:I\to\SCat
(resp.
I\to(\SCat,\times))
where
\SCat
denotes the 2-category of simplicially enriched categories.
We have

\Hom_{I}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})=\Hom^{\lax}(\Xi_{\mathcal{C}},\Xi_{\mathcal{D}}).

The compatibility with the simplicially enriched structure is as follows:

\Hom_{I}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
consists of simplicially enriched functors.
For
\mu\in\Hom^{\lax}(\Xi_{\mathcal{C}},\Xi_{\mathcal{D}})
the
\mu(i)
have to be simplicially enriched and

\textstyle{\Xi_{\mathcal{C}}(i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu(i)}
\scriptstyle{\Xi_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\Leftarrow^{\mu(\alpha)}}
\textstyle{\Xi_{\mathcal{D}}(i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi_{\mathcal{D}}(\alpha)}
\textstyle{\Xi_{\mathcal{C}}(j)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu(j)}
\textstyle{\Xi_{\mathcal{D}}(j)}

the natural transformations
\mu(\alpha)
have to be simplicially enriched, in the sense of
4.30
.

Let
I^{\flat}
the underlying set of objects (only those in
I_{[1]}
in the (co)operad case). Denote
()^{\flat}
the composition with (or pull-back to)
I^{\flat}\hookrightarrow I
.
Fix an object
X\in\Hom(\mathcal{C}^{\flat},\mathcal{D}^{\flat})=\Hom^{(\lax)}(\Xi_{\mathcal{C}}^{\flat},\Xi_{\mathcal{D}}^{\flat})

that is simplicially enriched,
i.e. a collection, for all objects
i\in I
,
of a simplicially enriched functor
X(i):\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}_{i}
.

Denote by
\Hom_{I}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})_{X}\cong\Hom^{\lax}(\Xi_{\mathcal{C}},\Xi_{\mathcal{D}})_{X}
the
set
30
30

30

This is a set, because the functors between fibers are fixed, only the values on morphisms, resp. the laxness constraint, are variable.
of functors (resp. lax natural transformations) that map to
X
.

Each simplicial set
K
define categories

\Hom_{I}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}_{K})=\Hom^{\lax}(\Xi_{\mathcal{C}},(\Xi_{\mathcal{D}})_{K})

where
(\Xi_{\mathcal{D}})_{K}
is the functor that maps
i
to the simplicially enriched category
\mathcal{D}_{i,K}
.

Definition 4.33
.

We define a simplicial set of
naive deformations

\underline{\mathrm{Def}}_{X}(\Xi_{\mathcal{C}},\Xi_{\mathcal{D}})

such that

\Hom(K,\underline{\Def}_{X}(\Xi_{\mathcal{C}},\Xi_{\mathcal{D}}))=\Hom_{I}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}_{K})_{X}=\Hom^{\lax}(\Xi_{\mathcal{C}},(\Xi_{\mathcal{D}})_{K})_{X}.

The adjective “naive” is used to distinguished these deformations from the coherent transformations discussed in Section
4.9
, which are more sophisticated, and encode
higher coherence as well. Sometimes, however, this higher coherence data can be built from a naive deformation, see Proposition
4.85
.

4.34.

Let us unravel the definition: For a simplicial set
K
the following set of data are the same

1.

\Hom(K,\underline{\mathrm{Def}}_{X})

2.

For each morphism
f:x\to y
in
\mathcal{C}
a morphism

\mu_{f}:K\otimes X(x)\to X(y)

which if
f
lies over an identity of
I
is the constant morphism
X(f):X(x)\to X(y)
. If
\mathcal{C}
is simplicially enriched, then we have a morphism

\underline{\Hom}(x,y)\to\underline{\Hom}(K\times X(x),X(y)).

mapping inert morphisms to inert morphisms, and compatible with composition.

3.

For each morphism
f:i\to j
in
I
a
K
-natural transformation:

\mu_{f}:\Hom(K,\underline{\Nat}(X(j)F(f),G(f)X(i)).

(invertible if
f
is inert) compatible with composition.

If the simplicially enriched structure on
J=\mathcal{C}
is discrete — and we will be mainly interested in this case — the deformations
F:J\to\mathcal{D}
over
X:J^{\flat}\to\mathcal{D}^{\flat}
can also be described as follows:

4.35.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a left tensored simplicially enriched category.
Let
(M\in\End(\mathcal{D}),m,u)
be a simplicially enriched monad. Then for each simplicial set
K
and object
G\in\mathcal{D}
, we may define a set of
M\times K
-algebra structures on
G
by morphisms

\mu_{K}:MG\times K\to G
such that

\textstyle{G\times K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(u,\id)}
\scriptstyle{\pr}
\textstyle{MG\times K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{K}}
\textstyle{G}

and

\textstyle{M(MG\times K)\times K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{(M\mu_{K},\id)}
\textstyle{M^{2}G\times K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(m,\id)}
\textstyle{MG\times K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{K}}
\textstyle{MG\times K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{K}}
\textstyle{G}

commute, where in the arrow
\delta
also the simplicially enriched structure of
M
is involved. This is clearly functorial in
K
and thus,
in particular, given
G
, we get a simplicial set of
M
-algebra structures

\underline{\Alg}_{M}(G)_{[n]}=\{\Delta_{n}\times M\text{-algebra structures on $G$}\}

which represents the previous functor on simplicial sets.

4.36.

Let
I
be a small category or (co)operad and
let
\mathcal{D}\to I
be a simplicially enriched cofibration and
p:J\to I
a usual (discrete) cofibration.
Let
I^{\flat}
be the set of objects of
I
and
\nu:I^{\flat}\hookrightarrow I
the canonical functor and
J^{\flat}
, resp.
\mathcal{D}^{\flat}
the pull-backs.
We have a pull-back diagram

\textstyle{J^{\flat}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\scriptstyle{p^{\flat}}
\textstyle{J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{I^{\flat}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{I}

We assume that

\nu^{*}:\mathcal{D}^{J}_{p}\to\mathcal{D}^{J^{\flat}}_{\nu^{*}p}=(\mathcal{D}^{\flat})^{J^{\flat}}_{p^{\flat}}
has a left adjoint
\nu_{!}^{(p)}
(relative left Kan extension, see Proposition
2.76
for sufficient criteria for its existence).
It is well-known that the adjunction is monadic and thus
\mathcal{D}^{J}_{p}
can be reconstructed as the algebras in
(\mathcal{D}^{\flat})^{J^{\flat}}_{p^{\flat}}

w.r.t. the monad

M=\nu^{*}\nu_{!}^{(p)}.

In the special case that
J
is an operad and
I=\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
, this is the interpretation of algebras over the operad
J
as algebras for a monad.

The monad is simplicially enriched w.r.t. the simplicially enriched structure on
\mathcal{D}\to I
.
We have then

\underline{\Def}_{X}(J,\mathcal{D})=\underline{\Alg}_{\nu^{*}\nu_{!}^{(p)}}(X).

4.5
Acyclicity (Construction of homotopies)

4.37.

A functor

Y:\Delta_{\emptyset}(\cong\Delta_{\act}^{\op})\to\Cat^{\PF}.

defines a structure of simplicially enriched category (in fact enriched over
\Set^{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}}
) already on

\Hom_{\Cat^{\PF}}(Y_{[0]},X)

for any category
X
as follows: Its objects are pro-functors
\alpha:Y_{[0]}\to X
and we define simplicial sets of morphisms by

\underline{\Hom}(\alpha,\beta)_{[n]}:=\Hom(\alpha Y(p_{n}),\beta Y(p_{n})).

If
\mathcal{C}
is a (finitely) cocomplete category, then

L_{\mathcal{C}}\circ Y
equips
L_{\mathcal{C}}(Y_{[0]})=\mathcal{C}^{Y_{[0]}}
with the structure of simplicially enriched category by means of Proposition
4.19
, and we have a map of simplicial sets:

\underline{\Hom}(\alpha,\beta)\rightarrow\underline{\Hom}(L_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha),L_{\mathcal{C}}(\beta))

turning
L_{\mathcal{C}}
into a simplicially enriched functor. Here the
\underline{\Hom}
on the RHS is the simplicial enrichment of functor categories
\Hom(\mathcal{C}^{X},\mathcal{C}^{Y_{[0]}})
(
4.31
), but
with the trivial (discrete) structure on
\mathcal{C}^{X}
!

The standard example is the functor
Y=\Xi
obtained from the cooperad
(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}
determined by
\Xi_{[0]}=\Delta^{\op}
,
\Xi(p_{1})=\dec
and
\Xi(p_{\emptyset})={}^{t}\pi
.
We have seen in Proposition
4.25
that
L_{\mathcal{C}}\circ\Xi
yields the
canonical enrichment
in simplicial sets for simplicial objects in
\mathcal{C}
.

For now it was not used that
Y
comes from a cooperad, i.e. that it is compatible with the product
\ast
on
\Delta_{\emptyset}
and
\times
on
\Cat^{\PF}
.
This circumstance has
strong acyclicity implications for the simplicial sets considered above (and also their cousins
\underline{\Def}(\cdots)
discussed in
4.40
below) that are eventually the source
of all homotopies constructed in these lectures. This is in some sense parallel to the theory of acyclic models
[
9
]
, but has the advantage of being entirely constructive, and not limited to Abelian situations.

In particular, two homotopic transformations
\mu,\nu:\alpha\Rightarrow\beta
induce homotopic transformations
L_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha)\Rightarrow L_{\mathcal{C}}(\beta)
.
In particular, if
Y=\Xi
is the standard example, and
\mathcal{W}
is a class of weak equivalences in
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
satisifying (W1) of
4.48
then
for the induced

L(\alpha)^{\prime},L(\beta)^{\prime}:\mathcal{C}^{X}\to\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]

we have
L(\mu)^{\prime}=L(\nu)^{\prime}
.

4.38.

Assume that
also
X
extends to a functor

X:\Delta_{\emptyset}(\cong\Delta_{\act}^{\op})\to\Cat^{\PF}

and assume that
X
and
Y
have the following properties:

1.

They are monoidal w.r.t. the concatenation product
\ast
on
\Delta_{\emptyset}
and
\times
on
\Cat^{\PF}
.
(This is the case if and only if they correspond to exponential fibrations of cooperads
I\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}
. )

2.

The unit
\cdot\cong X_{\emptyset}\rightarrow X_{0}
is given by
{}^{t}\pi
. (By 1. this determines all values of
X
under injective maps.)

Proposition 4.39
.

If
\alpha
and
\beta
extend to an oplax transformation, and lax transformation, respectively:

\widetilde{\alpha}\in\Hom^{\oplax,p_{\emptyset}-\pseudo,\times}(Y,X)\qquad\widetilde{\beta}\in\Hom^{\lax,p_{\emptyset}-\pseudo,\times}(Y,X)

((op)lax, pseudo on
p_{\emptyset}
31
31

31

Unraveling the definition, in view of assumption 2., this means that
\alpha
and
\beta
must be cofinal, i.e. satisfy
\alpha\,{}^{t}\!\pi={}^{t}\!\pi
and
\beta\,{}^{t}\!\pi={}^{t}\!\pi
.
, and compatible with the product structure) then

\underline{\Hom}(\alpha,\beta)

is empty or contractible.

4.40.

Before we give the somewhat technical proof, we discuss a functorial version of
4.37
. Consider a category or (co)operad
I
and functors

C,D\in\Delta_{\emptyset}\to\Hom^{1-\oplax,1-\inert-\pseudo}(I^{\op},(\Cat^{\PF},\times))

which satisfy the properties of
4.38
point-wise in
i
, such that also the oplax-ness constraints are compatible with the product structure.
Let

F:D|_{I^{\flat}}\to C|_{I^{\flat}}

be a natural transformation (i.e. a collection of pro-functors
F(i):D(i)\to C(i)
) compatible with product.
Then define

\underline{\Def}(D,C)_{F,[n]}:=\Hom^{\oplax,\inert-\pseudo}(D_{[n]},C_{[0]})_{F_{[0]}\circ D^{\flat}(p_{n})}

writing
D^{\flat}:=D|_{I^{\flat}}
.
The functoriality for
\alpha:[n]\to[m]
is given by the pre-composition maps

\Hom^{\oplax,\inert-\pseudo}(D_{[m]},C_{[0]})\to\Hom^{\oplax,\inert-\pseudo}(D_{[n]},C_{[0]})

induced by
D(\alpha)\in\Hom^{\oplax}(D_{[n]},D_{[m]})
.
Note that this yields a set, because the “values” of the natural transformations are fixed; the only variable is the oplaxness constraint.
Notice: For this definition, the structure extension of
C_{[0]}
to
\Delta_{\emptyset}
does not play a role, yet!

4.41.

If
\mathcal{C}\to I
is a functor of categories or (co)operads with (finitely) cocomplete fibers, then

D
equips
L_{\mathcal{C}}(D_{[0]})=D(D_{[0]},\mathcal{C})
(the Day convolution) with the structure of simplicially enriched category over
I
(cf. Proposition
4.19
), and we have a map of simplicial sets (cf. Definition
4.33
for the RHS):

\underline{\Def}(D,C)_{F}\rightarrow\underline{\Def}(D(C_{[0]},\mathcal{C}),D(D_{[0]},\mathcal{C}))_{L_{\mathcal{C}}(F)}

in which
D(C_{[0]},\mathcal{C})
is considered discrete
and
D(D_{[0]},\mathcal{C})
receives its simplicial structure from the functor
L_{\mathcal{C}}\circ D
.
Warning: The Day convolution
D(D_{[0]},\mathcal{C})\to I
is only a
simplicially enriched
cofibration over
I
, if
\mathcal{C}\to I
is a cofibration and

D
is actually a functor
\Delta_{\emptyset}\to\Hom(I^{\op},\Cat^{\PF})
i.e. maps morphisms to natural transformations (as opposed to oplax ones).

Whether
D(D_{[0]},\mathcal{C})\to I
is a simplicially enriched
fibration
, cannot be in general detected with the calculus of pro-functors, because this concerns
R
and the simplicial structure is encoded via
L
. If
D_{[0]}
actually corresponds to a fibration (i.e. has morphisms in
\im{}^{t}\iota
), we may form
{}^{t}\!D_{0}
, and this can be done in certain cases.

4.42.

For
I=[1]
, as a special case, we obtain the situation in
4.37
, in case
\widetilde{\alpha}
and
\widetilde{\beta}
are actually natural transformations (not only lax or oplax),
with the following data:

C
and
D
then encode
\widetilde{\alpha}
and
\widetilde{\beta}
as cofibrations over
I=[1]
and
F
is the identity.
Then we have

\underline{\Hom}(\alpha,\beta)=\underline{\Def}(D,C)_{\id}.

4.43.

For
I=[2]
and
\alpha=\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}
and
\beta=\beta_{2}\alpha_{1}
, encoded by
C
and
D
,
\underline{\Def}(D,C)_{\id}
consists of those simplices
in

\underline{\Hom}(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})\times\underline{\Hom}(\alpha_{2},\beta_{2})\times\underline{\Hom}(\alpha,\beta)

such that the pasting

\underline{\Hom}(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})\times\underline{\Hom}(\alpha_{2},\beta_{2})\to\underline{\Hom}(\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\beta_{1})\times\underline{\Hom}(\alpha_{2}\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\beta_{1})\to\underline{\Hom}(\alpha,\beta)

maps the elements (of the same degree) in the former pair of simplicial sets to the one in the latter.
For the map
\underline{\Hom}(\alpha_{2},\beta_{2})\to\underline{\Hom}(\alpha_{2}\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\beta_{1})
to exist, it is essential, that
\beta_{1}
is “simplicially enriched” meaning that it extends to a functor
\Delta_{\emptyset}\times[1]\to\Cat^{\PF}
.

4.44.

There is a canonical element in
\underline{\Def}(D,C)_{F,[-1]}
given by

\Hom^{\oplax}(D_{\emptyset}=\cdot,C_{[0]})_{\underbrace{F_{[0]}\circ D^{\flat}(p_{\emptyset})}_{\cong C^{\flat}(p_{\emptyset})}}

given by
C(p_{\emptyset})
. If that one consists of isomorphisms then it is the unique element in there.

Proposition 4.45
.

In the situation of
4.40
, the fiber of

\underline{\Def}(D,C)_{F}

over the canonical element
C(p_{\emptyset})
in
\underline{\Def}(D,C)_{F,[-1]}
is empty or contractible.

Proof of Proposition
4.39
.

By Lemma
4.47
, 2. below it suffices to construct a section of the natural map

\dec^{*}\underline{\Hom}(\alpha,\beta)\rightarrow\underline{\Hom}(\alpha,\beta)\boxtimes\underline{\Hom}(\alpha,\beta).

Given
\mu_{1}:\alpha Y(p_{n})\Rightarrow\beta Y(p_{n})
and
\mu_{2}:\alpha Y(p_{m})\Rightarrow\beta Y(p_{m})

we have to define a morphism

\nu:\alpha Y(p_{n+m+1})\to\beta Y(p_{n+m+1})

which applied to
Y(\delta_{l})
and
Y(\delta_{r})
gives back
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
.
It is defined as the pasting

\scriptstyle{Y(p_{n+m+1})}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\alpha}_{n+m+1}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\beta}_{n+m+1}}
\scriptstyle{\Rightarrow}
\scriptstyle{Y(p_{n+m+1})}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\Rightarrow}
\scriptstyle{X(p_{n+m+1})}
\textstyle{\Rightarrow}
\scriptstyle{\beta}

(40)

where the middle natural transformation is
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
exploiting the product structure on
\widetilde{\alpha}_{n+m+1}
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{n+m+1}
.
We have to show that be get back
\mu_{1}
after pre-composition with
Y(\delta_{l})
(and similarly,
\mu_{2}
after pre-composition with
Y(\delta_{r})
).

Claim:
The pasting

\scriptstyle{Y(\delta_{l})}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\beta}_{n+m+1}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\alpha}_{n+m+1}}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow}

is equal to the pasting:

\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\alpha}_{n+m+1}}
\textstyle{\Downarrow}
\scriptstyle{Y(\delta_{l})}
\scriptstyle{Y(\delta_{l})}
\scriptstyle{\beta Y(p_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\alpha Y(p_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow^{\mu_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{X(\delta_{l})}
\textstyle{\Downarrow}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\beta}_{n+m+1}}

(41)

Both diagrams are actually products, namely

For the left parts the assertion is thus clear. For the right parts note that by assumption
X(p_{\emptyset})=Y(p_{\emptyset})={}^{t}\!\pi
and that
\widetilde{\alpha}
and
\widetilde{\beta}
are pseudo on

p_{\emptyset}
, thus
Y(p_{\emptyset})\alpha
and
Y(p_{\emptyset})\beta
are thus both isomorphic to
{}^{t}\!\pi
which is a final object. This proves the claim.

The conclusion is made by pre-composing (
40
) with
Y(\delta_{l})
and inserting (
41
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.45
.

By Lemma
4.47
, 2. below, it suffices to construct a section of the natural map

\dec^{*}\underline{\Def}(D,C)_{F}\rightarrow\underline{\Def}(D,C)_{F}\boxtimes\underline{\Def}(D,C)_{F}.

Given
\mu_{1}\in\Hom^{\oplax}(D_{n},C_{0})_{F_{0}\circ D^{\flat}(p_{n})}
and
\mu_{2}\in\Hom^{\oplax}(D_{m},C_{0})_{F_{0}\circ D^{\flat}(p_{m})}
, we have to produce

\nu\in\Hom^{\oplax}(D_{n+m+1},D_{0})_{F_{0}\circ D^{\flat}(p_{n+m+1})}
connecting the two.
This is more or less the same construction as in the proof of Proposition
4.39
:
We have given for each
\alpha:i\to j
:

\scriptstyle{F_{0}(j)\circ D(p_{n},j)}
\scriptstyle{D_{n}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow^{\mu_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{C_{0}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{F_{0}(i)\circ D(p_{n},i)}

\scriptstyle{F_{0}(j)\circ D(p_{m},j)}
\scriptstyle{D_{m}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow^{\mu_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{C_{0}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{F_{0}(i)\circ D(p_{m},i)}

or which is the same:

\scriptstyle{C(p_{n},j)\circ F_{n}(j)}
\scriptstyle{D_{n}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow^{\mu_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{C_{0}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{n},i)\circ F_{n}(i)}

\scriptstyle{C(p_{m},j)\circ F_{m}(j)}
\scriptstyle{D_{m}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow^{\mu_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{C_{0}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{m},i)\circ F_{m}(i)}

and have to construct a 2-morphism in

\scriptstyle{D(p_{n+m+1},j)}
\scriptstyle{D_{n+m+1}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow^{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{F_{0}(j)}
\scriptstyle{C_{0}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{D(p_{n+m+1},i)}
\scriptstyle{F_{0}(i)}

The square is isomorphic to the outer square in

\scriptstyle{C(p_{n}\ast p_{m},j)F_{n+m+1}(j)}
\scriptstyle{D_{n+m+1}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{1},j)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow}
\scriptstyle{C_{1}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{C_{0}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{n}\ast p_{m},i)F_{n+m+1}(i)}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{1},i)}

and it is clear from the
functorialities that the constructed element yields a valid element of
\underline{\Def}(D,C)_{F,n+m+1}
.

We have to see that we get back
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
after applying
\delta_{l},\delta_{r}
. Investigate the case of
\delta_{l}
, the other is analog:

\scriptstyle{D(\delta_{l},j)}
\scriptstyle{D_{n}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{n}\ast p_{m},j)F_{n+m+1}(j)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{D_{n+m+1}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{C_{1}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{1},j)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow}
\scriptstyle{C_{0}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{D(\delta_{l},i)}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{n}\ast p_{m},i)F_{n+m+1}(i)}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{1},i)}

The left squares are the product

\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern
