and
\mu_{2}
map to the oplaxness-constraint given by
C(p_{\emptyset})
. (If that is an isomorphism, then both compositions are the final object and thus there
is only one 2-morphism anyway. )
We can thus write the pasting as

\scriptstyle{C(p_{n},j)F_{n}(j)}
\scriptstyle{D_{n}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow^{\mu_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{1}\ast p_{\emptyset},j)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow}
\scriptstyle{C_{0}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{C_{1}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{1},j)}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow}
\scriptstyle{C_{0}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{n},i)F_{n}(i)}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{1}\ast p_{\emptyset},i)}
\scriptstyle{C(p_{1},i)}

which gives back
\mu_{1}
.
∎

The following Lemma and its Corollary, which is well-known, can also be obtained easily without calculation from the description of homotopies via
\dec
but is a bit different, and also more elementary than
the Propositions discussed before.

Lemma 4.46
.

The canonical map

P:\dec_{n}\Rightarrow[n]\circ\pi:\Delta^{\op}\to\Delta^{\op}

is a homotopy equivalence in the sense of
4.37
(for
Y=\Xi
the standard example).

Proof.

(cf. also the proof of Lemma
A.3
).
Define

S:[n]\circ\pi\Rightarrow\dec_{n}

by the degeneracy
[n]\twoheadleftarrow[x]\ast[n]
which maps
[x]
to the minimal element in
[n]
.
Obviously,
PS=\id
. We have to define a morphism

\xi:\dec_{n}\dec\to\dec_{n}\dec

such that the pre-composition with
{}^{t}\!\pr_{0}
gives back the identity and the pre-composition with
{}^{t}\!\pr_{1}
gives back
SP
.
We define the morphism

\xi:[a]\ast[b]\ast[n]\to[a]\ast[b]\ast[n]

as the identity on
[a]
and
[n]
and mapping
[b]
to the minimal element of
[n]
.
We may in fact extend
\xi
to a morphism

\xi_{\emptyset}:\dec_{\emptyset,n}\dec_{\emptyset}\to\dec_{\emptyset,n}\dec_{\emptyset}.

That one has the
obvious
property that composition with
p_{0}=([\emptyset],\id):\Delta_{\emptyset}^{\op}\to\Delta_{\emptyset}^{\op}\times\Delta_{\emptyset}^{\op}
gives back (the restriction of)
SP
and composition with
p_{1}
gives back the identity. Thus

{}^{t}\!i\dec_{\emptyset,n}\dec_{\emptyset}p_{i}i\to{}^{t}\!i\dec_{\emptyset,n}\dec_{\emptyset}p_{i}i

is
SP
, and
\id
, respectively. But
{}^{t}\!i\dec([n],\id)\dec\cong\dec({}^{t}\!i,{}^{t}\!i)([n],\id)\dec\cong\dec([n],\id){}^{t}\!i\dec\cong\dec([n],\id)\dec({}^{t}\!i,{}^{t}\!i)
using Lemma
A.5
and finally

({}^{t}\!i,{}^{t}\!i)p_{i}i=\pi_{i}
using Lemma
A.2
.
∎

Corollary 4.47
.

1.

The canonical map

\dec^{*}\Rightarrow\pr_{1}^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}

is, point-wise in the second variable, a homotopy equivalence, i.e. all the canonical maps
\dec_{n}^{*}X\to X_{n}
where
X_{n}
is considered constant,
are homotopy equivalences.

2.

If
X
is a simplicial set such that there is a section of the natural morphism

\dec^{*}X\rightarrow X\boxtimes X

then
X
is either empty or contractible.

Proof.

1. is an obvious consequence of Lemma
4.46
. For 2. consider the morphism

\dec_{0}^{*}X\rightarrow X\times X_{0}

which has a section by assumption. The fiber over any element of
X_{0}
of the left hand side is contractible by 1. That is thus also the case
for
X
.
∎

4.6
Weak equivalences and geometric realization

The non-Abelian Eilenberg-Zilber theorem that will be investigated in the next two sections is a statement “up to weak equivalence”. Indeed, even
the Eilenberg-Zilber
morphism
that will be constructed is only defined as a morphism in the homotopy category (unless the situation is
strongly symmetric
, as in the Abelian case). We do not want to
restrict the situation unnecessarily, allowing for simplicial objects
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
in a general 1-category (being mainly interested in
\mathcal{C}=\Set
and
\mathcal{C}
Abelian in these lectures).
The proper language for such a general situation would be, of course, that of model categories. However, as long as we are in a situation (as in these main examples), where “potentially” every simplicial object is cofibrant,
much
less is needed for our purposes:

4.48.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a (finitely) complete and cocomplete category. We consider the following axioms on a class of “weak equivalences”
\mathcal{W}
in
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
.

(W1)

\Delta_{1}\otimes X\to X
(or equivalently
X\to\mathcal{HOM}(\Delta_{1},X)
) is a weak equivalence for all
X
32
32

32

where
\otimes
and
\mathcal{HOM}
are the (co)tensoring of the
canonical simplicial enrichment
2.15
.
.

(W2)

\delta^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
maps point-wise weak equivalences (in either direction) to weak equivalences.

Lemma 4.49
.

The two versions of (W1) are equivalent and also imply that

X\to\Hom(\Delta_{n},X)
, and
\Delta_{n}\otimes X\to X
are weak equivalences for all
n
.

Proof.

Any of the two statements in (W1) implies that homotopy equivalences are weak equivalences, and,
since
\otimes
is associative,
-\otimes X
and
\Hom(-,X)
map homotopy equivalences in
\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}
to homotopy equivalences in
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
, and
s:\Delta_{n}\to\Delta_{0}
is a homotopy equivalence in
\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.50
.

The axioms of
4.48
hold true for
\mathcal{C}=\Set
, and for
\mathcal{C}
Abelian, and the class
\mathcal{W}
of usual weak equivalences, and the class of quasi-isomorphisms, respectively.

Proof.

Case
\mathcal{C}=\Set
.

(W1) holds true because
\otimes=\times
is a left Quillen bifunctor (cf.
[
29
]
), every object is cofibrant, and
\delta_{0},\delta_{1}:\Delta_{0}\hookrightarrow\Delta_{1}
are trivial cofibrations, therefore
\id\times\delta_{0}:X\to X\times\Delta_{1}
is a trivial cofibration, and therefore the first map of (W1) which is a section of
\id\times\delta_{0}
is a weak equivalence.
(W2) is shown e.g. in
[
29
, IV, Proposition 1.7]
.

Case
\mathcal{C}
Abelian.

(W1) If two morphisms
f,g:X\to Y
are
\otimes
-homotopic, i.e. if there is a morphism

\Delta_{1}\otimes X\to Y

such that the restrictions give
f
and
g
, respectively, then the composition
\Delta_{1}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X\to\Delta_{1}\otimes X\to Y

with the Eilenberg-Zilber map yields a homotopy in the usual sense (cf. also Lemma
5.29
) which implies that
f
and
g
induce the same map on homology groups.
Hence homotopy-equivalences w.r.t.
\otimes
are quasi-isomorphisms as well. Then argue as in the proof of Lemma
4.49
.

(W2) The proof of the Eilenberg-Zilber theorem does not need (W2) in the strongly symmetric case (cf. Definition
4.54
).
Thus it suffices to show that
\dec_{*}\cong\tot
(cf. Proposition
5.39
) preserves quasi-isomorphisms in any direction. This is true in
any
Abelian category for non-negatively graded complexes.
∎

Note that we have (for the examples
\mathcal{C}
Abelian, and
\Set
, and more generally in case that
(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathcal{W})
is part of a model category structure) an equivalence of
localizations:

(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}])^{\Delta^{\op}}\cong\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}_{v}^{-1}]

at the vertical weak equivalences (
[
18
, Proposition 7.9.2.]
).
This is not at all true for the 1-categorical localizations. The statement below makes sense for them as well, if one takes
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}_{v}^{-1}]
and the homotopy colimit, i.e. the
derived functor of the colimit.

Proposition 4.51
.

If
4.48
hold then we have a commutative diagram of
\infty
-categories:

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}])^{\Delta^{\op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\colim_{\Delta^{\op}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]}

in which the vertical functors are the canonical ones into the localizations which we consider as
\infty
-categorical localizations.
In other words

\boxed{\delta^{*}=\text{ geometric realization. }}

Proof.

Let
\delta:[n]\mapsto\Delta_{n}
be the canonical cosimplicial simplicial object. We have

\delta^{*}X\cong\int^{n}\Delta_{n}\otimes X_{\bullet,n}.

This is left adjoint to the functor

(\delta_{*}X)_{\bullet,n}\cong\mathcal{HOM}(\Delta_{n},X).

By
4.48
and Lemma
4.49
both functors preserve weak equivalences and thus descend to an adjunction between the localizations.
However, by Lemma
4.49
the morphism
\pr_{2}^{*}\to\delta_{*}
induced by
\Delta_{n}\to\Delta_{0}
is a (point-wise) weak equivalence.
The statement follows thus from the uniqueness of adjoints.
∎

Remark 4.52
.

In a situation in which the Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem holds (e.g. if
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
is symmetric w.r.t.
\mathcal{W}
), thus also
\dec_{*}
computes geometric realization in the localization.

In the Abelian case, the same proof gives also directly that
\dec_{*}=
geometric realization:
We have, denoting
\Delta_{\bullet}^{\circ}:=\mathbb{Z}[\Delta_{\bullet}]
the cosimplicial object in
\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab)^{\Delta}
:

\dec_{*}X\cong\int^{n}\Delta_{n}^{\circ}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X_{\bullet,n}.

(because
\dec_{*}=\tot
obviously commutes with colimits) and this has the right adjoint (cf. also Section
C.5
)

\dec^{?}:X\mapsto\mathcal{HOM}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(\Delta^{\circ},X).

Again both functors preserve quasi-isomorphisms and the second functor is isomorphic to
\pr_{2}^{*}
in the localization.

Remark 4.53
.

The statement in Proposition
4.51
is obviously also equivalent (using that
\delta
is
\infty
-cofinal, see Lemma
A.4
) to the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}])^{\Delta^{\op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\colim_{\Delta^{\op}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]}

where the vertical morphism is induced by the composition
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]
.
In other words, every object in
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]
is the “colimit of itself considered as simplicial diagram”.

4.7
Symmetry

This section contains a discussion of the symmetries of simplicial objects that are at the heart of the Eilenberg-Zilber theorems,
Theorem
4.65
in the general case, and Theorem
5.15
in the Abelian case.
These symmetries are more tractable and efficient in the Abelian case.
Let
\iota:\Delta^{\op}\to\FinSet^{\op}
, where
\FinSet
is the category of non-empty finite sets
33
33

33

for simplicity, the equivalent category with the same objects as
\Delta^{\op}
, be the inclusion.

Definition 4.54
.

We say that
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}
(with chosen class of weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
) is (strongly) symmetric if
there is a functor:

\mathfrak{C}:\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\FinSet^{\mathrm{op}}}

with a natural transformation

\id\to\iota^{*}\mathfrak{C}

which is object-wise a weak equivalence (resp. an isomorphism).

4.55.

Recall the axioms
4.48
.
(W2) is only needed in the non-strongly symmetric case.

Lemma 4.56
.

If
\mathcal{W}
is a class of weak equivalences such that (
4.48
) hold then
there is a weak equivalence
\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\to\id
. In particular,
\delta^{*}\dec^{*}
preserves weak equivalences.

Proof.

Lemma
4.47
shows that
\dec^{*}\to\pr_{1}^{*}
is point-wise in the second variable a homotopy equivalence. Thus by (W1) it is point-wise in the second variable a weak equivalence.
Thus by (W2)

\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\to\delta^{*}\pr_{1}^{*}=\id

is a weak equivalence.
∎

Lemma 4.57
.

\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}
is symmetric and
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
is strongly symmetric for any Abelian category
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

The standard cosimplicial objects

\Delta_{n}^{T}=\{(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{[n]}\ |\ \sum x_{i}=1\}

in
\mathcal{TOP}
and

(\Delta^{\circ}_{n})_{i}=\mathbb{Z}[\Hom_{\Delta}([i],[n])]

in
(\Ab^{\fg})^{\Delta^{\op}}
extend to functors
\Delta_{n}^{T,s}
and
\Delta^{\circ,s}
from
\FinSet
(the first by obvious action of
S_{n}
on the coordinates and the second using the action of Lemma
5.6
).
Defining
\mathfrak{C}:=N_{\Delta_{n}^{T,s}}R_{\Delta_{n}^{T}}
, and
\mathfrak{C}:=N_{\Delta^{\circ,s}}R_{\Delta^{\circ}}
, respectively, we have

\iota^{*}\mathfrak{C}=N_{\Delta_{n}^{T}}R_{\Delta_{n}^{T}}
, and
\iota^{*}\mathfrak{C}=N_{\Delta_{n}^{\circ}}R_{\Delta_{n}^{\circ}}
, respectively, which come equipped with a
natural transformation (unit of the
N
,
R
adjunction)
\id\to\iota^{*}\mathfrak{C}
. It is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets, and an isomorphism in
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
, respectively.
∎

Lemma 4.58
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be Abelian.
For the functor
\mathfrak{C}
of Lemma
4.57
we have

\mathfrak{C}=L_{\mathcal{C}}(({}^{t}\!C)^{\op})=R_{\mathcal{C}}(C^{\op})

where
C
and
{}^{t}C
are the
\Ab
-enriched pro-functors
C:\FinSet\to\Delta
(resp.
{}^{t}\!C:\Delta\to\FinSet
) with
C
left adjoint:

\displaystyle C:n,m

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\Hom(\Delta^{\circ}_{n},\Delta^{\circ}_{m})\in\Hom(\Delta^{\op}\times\FinSet,\Ab)

\displaystyle{}^{t}C:n,m

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\Hom(\Delta^{\circ}_{n},\Delta^{\circ}_{m})\in\Hom(\FinSet^{\op}\times\Delta,\Ab)

and there is an isomorphism of pro-functors
C\,\iota\cong\id
(resp.
{}^{t}\!\iota\,{}^{t}\!C\cong\id
).

In the definition of
C
(resp.
{}^{t}\!C
)
\Delta^{\circ}
in the right slot (resp. left slot) is considered as functor on
\FinSet
as defined by Lemma
5.6
.

Proof.

Recall that
N_{\Delta_{n}^{\circ}}
commutes with colimits and
\otimes
(see proof of Theorem
5.11
).
Hence we have

\mathfrak{C}(X)_{n}=\Hom(\Delta_{n}^{\circ},\int^{m}\Delta_{m}^{\circ}\otimes X_{m})=\int^{m}\Hom(\Delta_{n}^{\circ},\Delta_{m}^{\circ})\otimes X_{m}=L_{\mathcal{C}}(({}^{t}\!C)^{\op})(X)

(which is isomorphic to
X_{n}
as object in
\mathcal{C}
, of course, but now gives a
\FinSet^{\op}
-valued functor).

We also have:

\mathfrak{C}(X)_{n}=\int_{m}\mathcal{HOM}(\Hom(\Delta_{m}^{\circ},\Delta_{n}^{\circ}),X_{m})=R_{\mathcal{C}}(C^{\op})(X)

which is also isomorphic to
X_{n}
. Applying this to
\mathcal{C}=\Ab
, we see that
L_{\Ab}(C^{\op})
is left adjoint to
L_{\Ab}(({}^{t}\!C)^{\op})
, thus

C^{\op}
right adjoint to
({}^{t}\!C)^{\op}
and thus
C
left adjoint to
{}^{t}\!C
.
∎

This shows also that there is a similar operator:

\mathfrak{C}^{n}:\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\cdots\times\Delta^{\op}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\FinSet^{\op}\times\cdots\times\FinSet^{\op}}

by applying the
\Ab
-enriched pro-functor
C\times\cdots\times C
.

For
general
symmetric
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
, we have something weaker:

Lemma 4.59
.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
be symmetric.
There is an operator
34
34

34

and similar for higher powers, which will not be needed.

\mathfrak{C}^{2}:\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\FinSet^{\op}\times\FinSet^{\op}}

with a natural transformation

\id\to(\iota,\iota)^{*}\mathfrak{C}^{2}

which is a composition of natural transformations which are point-wise in one or the other direction weak equivalences.

Proof.

For
X\in\{\Delta^{\op},\FinSet^{\op}\}
denote by

(\mathfrak{C},\id):\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times X}\to\mathcal{C}^{\FinSet^{\op}\times X}

the point-wise application of
\mathfrak{C}
and similarly for
(\id,\mathfrak{C})
.
We then set
\mathfrak{C}^{2}=(\mathfrak{C},\id)(\id,\mathfrak{C})
, noting that the factors do not commute in general.
However,
(\id,\iota)^{*}
commutes with
(\mathfrak{C},\id)
. And thus

(\iota,\iota)^{*}\mathfrak{C}^{2}=(\iota,\id)^{*}(\mathfrak{C},\id)(\id,\iota)^{*}(\id,\mathfrak{C})

and there is a morphism

\id\to(\iota,\iota)^{*}\mathfrak{C}^{2}

defined as the compositon

\id\to(\id,\iota)^{*}(\id,\mathfrak{C})\to(\iota,\id)^{*}(\mathfrak{C},\id)(\id,\iota)^{*}(\id,\mathfrak{C})=(\iota,\iota)^{*}\mathfrak{C}^{2}

The two morphisms are point-wise in the first (resp. second variable a weak equivalence).
∎

Proposition 4.60
.

For any cocomplete category
\mathcal{C}
, the composition

\textstyle{\iota^{*}\iota_{!}\iota^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{*}c}
\textstyle{\iota^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u\iota^{*}}
\textstyle{\iota^{*}\iota_{!}\iota^{*}}

of functors
\mathcal{C}^{\FinSet^{\op}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}

is homotopic to the identity.

Proof.

Recall diagram

\textstyle{(\FinSet,\coprod)^{\op}}
\textstyle{(\Delta,\coprod)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\delta}}
\textstyle{(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\dec}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{forget}}

All cooperads (being exponential) translate to functors

\Delta_{\emptyset}(\cong\Delta^{\op}_{\act})\to\Cat^{\PF}

which are compatible with products,
p_{\emptyset}
is mapped to
{}^{t}\pi
,
and the oplax transformations
\iota_{\delta}
and
\iota_{\dec}
(also compatible with products) have lax mates (consisting point-wise of
{}^{t}\!\iota
) denoted

{}^{t}\!\iota_{\delta}
and
{}^{t}\!\iota_{\dec}
. Since
\iota_{\delta}
is Cartesian (
4.6
),
{}^{t}\!\iota_{\delta}
is natural, and since
\iota_{\dec}
is coCartesian (
4.6
),
\iota_{\dec}
is natural. Therefore, the following are lax and oplax extensions, respectively, of of
\iota\,{}^{t}\!\iota\,\iota
:

\iota_{\dec}\,{}^{t}\!\iota_{\dec}\,\iota_{\dec}\qquad\iota_{\delta}\,{}^{t}\!\iota_{\delta}\,\iota_{\dec}.

Thus Proposition
4.39
shows that
\underline{\Hom}(\iota\,{}^{t}\!\iota\,\iota,\iota\,{}^{t}\!\iota\,\iota)
is connected and thus
any two natural transformations
\iota^{*}\iota_{!}\iota^{*}\Rightarrow\iota^{*}\iota_{!}\iota^{*}
given by morphisms of pro-functors are homotopic.
Notice that the above being “pseudo on
p_{\emptyset}
” translates to the 1-cofinality of
\iota
(Lemma
A.6
).
∎

Corollary 4.61
.

If
\mathcal{W}
is a class of weak equivalences such that
4.48
, 1. holds then
the unit
\id\to\iota^{*}\iota_{!}
is a weak equivalence on objects in the image of
\iota^{*}
.
Furthermore

\id\to(\iota,\iota)^{*}(\iota,\iota)_{!}

is a composition of morphisms which are point-wise in one or the other direction weak equivalences, on objects in the image of
(\iota,\iota)^{*}
.

Proof.

The first statement follows directly from Proposition
4.60
, while the second follows from a similar reasoning as in the proof of Lemma
4.59
.
∎

4.8
The non-Abelian Eilenberg-Zilber theorem

4.62.

There is a diagram

\textstyle{\Delta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\Delta\times\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\dec}
\scriptstyle{(\iota,\iota)}
\textstyle{\FinSet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{s}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\FinSet\times\FinSet}
\scriptstyle{\dec_{s}}

in which the corresponding squares commute. The functors
\dec_{s}=\coprod
and
\delta_{s}
are adjoint on
\FinSet
(as the coproduct is always adjoint to the diagonal by definition) with unit

u:\id\Rightarrow\delta_{s}\dec_{s}

([n],[m])\to([n]\ast[m],[n]\ast[m])

given by the obvious face maps and counit

c:\dec_{s}\delta_{s}\Rightarrow\id

[n]\ast[n]\to[n]

given by (the opposite of) the diagonal.
Notice that the morphism
u
is point-wise in
\Delta
whereas
c
is not, reflecting the fact that
\dec
is not a coproduct in
\Delta
.

For
\FinSet^{\op}
-diagrams we get, in particular, an isomorphism
\delta_{s}^{*}\cong\dec_{s,*}
(or equivalently
\delta_{s,!}\cong\dec_{s}^{*}
).
Assume
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
is symmetric w.r.t. a class of weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
satisfying
4.48
.
While
c
is not a morphism in
\Delta
we may may conjugate the morphism
c^{\op}:\id\to\delta^{*}\dec^{*}
to a morphism
\mlq c^{\op}\mrq

in
\Fun(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}})[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]
by means of the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\id\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\in\mathcal{W}}
\scriptstyle{\mlq c^{\op}\mrq}
\textstyle{\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\in\mathcal{W}}
\textstyle{\iota^{*}\mathfrak{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{*}c^{\op}\mathfrak{C}}
\textstyle{\iota^{*}\delta_{s}^{*}\dec_{s}^{*}\mathfrak{C}=\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\iota^{*}\mathfrak{C}}

(using that
\delta^{*}\dec^{*}
preserves weak equivalences by Lemma
4.56
35
35

35

of course not needed in the strongly symmetric case
). If
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
is strongly symmetric, e.g. if
\mathcal{C}
is Abelian,
then
\mlq c^{\op}\mrq
is an honest morphism (without need to invert weak equivalences).

Definition 4.63

(Alexander-Whitney and Eilenberg-Zilber morphism)
.

1.

The morphism

\mathfrak{Aw}:\delta^{*}\to\dec_{*}

is defined as the composition

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 7.52083pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.52083pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\delta^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.52083pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.52083pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\dec_{*}\dec^{*}\delta^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 91.39642pt\raise 7.41168pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.91168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\dec_{*}u^{\op}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 138.13432pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 108.13432pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 138.13432pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\dec_{*}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

2.

Assume
\mathcal{C}^{\op}
is symmetric w.r.t. a class of weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
satisfying
4.48
.
The morphism

\mathfrak{Ez}:\dec_{*}\to\delta^{*}

in
\Fun(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}})[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]
36
36

36

By abuse of notation, here
\mathcal{W}
denotes the class of morphisms that are object-wise weak equivalences.
is defined as the composition (cf.
4.62
):

\textstyle{\dec_{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mlq c^{\op}\mrq\dec_{*}}
\textstyle{\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\dec_{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\delta^{*}.}

If
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
is strongly symmetric, e.g. if
\mathcal{C}
is Abelian, then we understand
\mathfrak{Ez}\in\Fun(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}})

as an honest natural transformation (no need to invert anything).

If
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
is strongly symmetric then
\mathfrak{Ez}
is still not inverse to
\mathfrak{Aw}
.
If
\mathcal{C}
is Abelian
37
37

37

and provided the symmetry operator
\mathfrak{C}
is the canonical one given by Lemma
4.57
, then
\mathfrak{Aw}
is the familiar Alexander-Whitney morphism, and
\mathfrak{Ez}
is the familiar Eilenberg-Zilber morphism usually defined in terms of shuffles, see Proposition
5.42
.

a fact that will be convenient to prove all kinds of compatibilities using compositions in the 2-categories
\Cor(-,-)
(
2.14
).

The goal of this section is to prove

Theorem 4.65

(Eilenberg-Zilber, general version)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
be symmetric with weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
satisfying
4.48
, for example
\mathcal{C}
Abelian (with
\mathcal{W}
the quasi-isomorphisms), or
\mathcal{C}=\Set
(with
\mathcal{W}
the usual weak equivalences)
38
38

38

If
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
is strongly symmetric (W2) is not needed.
.
Then
\mathfrak{Aw}
and
\mathfrak{Ez}
are mutually inverse isomorphisms

\dec_{*}\cong\delta^{*}

in
\Fun(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}})[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\Set
there are several proofs in the literature, see
[
16
, Theorem 1.1]
,
[
52
, Theorem 1.1]
,
[
54
]
.

In the non-strongly symmetric case, it follows also that
\dec_{*}
preserves morphisms which are weak equivalences point-wise in one direction, because
\delta^{*}
does so by assumption.
For Abelian
\mathcal{C}
we will make the Theorem more precise, and coherent, with a simpler proof, in section
5.2
.

4.66.

This theorem automatically implies a coherent version: The forgetful morphism of cooperads

\AW:=\mathrm{forget}:(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}\Rightarrow(\Delta,\coprod)^{\op}

can be seen as an oplax transformation in
\Hom^{1-\oplax}(\Delta_{\emptyset},\Cat^{\PF})
and induces as oplaxness constraint on
p_{1}
a
morphism
\Aw:\dec\Rightarrow{}^{t}\!\delta
such that
R(\Aw)
is the map
\mathfrak{Aw}
above (cf. also the proof of Theorem
5.15
).
If
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)
is monoidal,
on Day convolutions “
\mathrm{forget}
” induces

\mathfrak{AW}:=R(\mathrm{forget}):D((\Delta,\coprod)^{\op},(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee})\rightarrow D((\Delta,\ast)^{\op},(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee})

which is nothing but

\mathfrak{AW}:(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}

with
\otimes=\delta^{*}(-\boxtimes-)
the point-wise product and
\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}=\dec_{*}(-\boxtimes-)
and the morphism on monoidal products is induced by the map
\mathfrak{Aw}
.
If
\otimes
(with a constant object) preserves weak equivalences, Theorem
4.65
implies thus that these yield monoidal cooperads

\mathfrak{AW}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]:(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}],\otimes)^{\vee}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}],\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}

and the functor is an equivalence. If
\otimes
was the Cartesian product
\times
, this is actually trivial, cf. Theorem
4.70
below.

The following theorem puts this into perspective, but does not imply Theorem
4.65
directly.

Theorem 4.67
.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}
be symmetric with weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
satisfying
4.48
. Set
\mathcal{W}_{s}:=(\iota^{*})^{-1}\mathcal{W}
.
Then the functor induced on the
\infty
-categorical localizations

\iota^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{\FinSet^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}_{s}^{-1}]\to\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]

is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories.

Proof.

We show that
\iota^{*}
and
\mathfrak{C}
constitute inverse functors up to a chain of point-wise weak equivalences.
We have a weak equivalence

\id\Rightarrow\iota^{*}\mathfrak{C}

by definition. This also shows that
\mathfrak{C}
preserves weak equivalences. Furthermore, we have a chain of natural transformations

\mathfrak{C}\iota^{*}\Leftarrow\iota_{!}\iota^{*}\mathfrak{C}\iota^{*}\Leftarrow\iota_{!}\iota^{*}\Rightarrow\id

which are all point-wise in
\mathcal{W}_{s}
, for apply
\iota^{*}
and extend to a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\iota^{*}\mathfrak{C}\iota^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
1
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\iota^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Theorem 4.70

(Trivial Eilenberg-Zilber)
.

If
(\mathcal{C},\times)
is a Cartesian monodial (
\infty
-)category then the functor

\mathfrak{AW}:=R(\mathrm{forget}):(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)^{\vee}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\widetilde{\times})^{\vee}

is an equivalence (i.e. no need to invert weak equivalences).

Proof.

Consider the diagram

\textstyle{(\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op})\coprod(\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}\coprod\pr_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\dec\coprod\dec}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\coprod\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}}

in which the square with the upper horizontal morphism commutes.
We have

A\times B=\delta^{*}d_{*}(\pr_{1}\coprod\pr_{2})^{*}(A,B)=d_{*}(A,B)

and

\dec_{*}A\boxtimes B=\dec_{*}d_{*}(\pr_{1}\coprod\pr_{2})^{*}(A,B).

The morphism
\mathfrak{AW}
is induced by the composition

d_{*}\to d_{*}(\dec\coprod\dec)_{*}(\dec\coprod\dec)^{*}\to d_{*}(\dec\coprod\dec)_{*}(\pr_{1}\coprod\pr_{2})^{*}.

This however induces the
isomorphisms

\id\cong\dec_{*}\pr_{1/2}^{*}
(Lemma
A.3
).

Alternative proof using Proposition
2.30
.
(\mathcal{C},\times)^{\vee}\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}
is a
cofibration
of cooperads via the diagonal

\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
. The Day convolution thus sees only the projections of
the pro-functors
\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}\to\Delta^{\op}
given by
\dec
and
{}^{t}\delta
. Those projections are the identities in both cases by Lemma
A.3
.
∎

The second proof shows that the statement holds more generally, if
\mathcal{C}\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
is a
fibration
of operads.

4.9
Coherent transformations

4.71.

For
F,G:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
usual functors between 1-categories, we have:

\Hom(F,G)=\int_{c}\Hom(F(c),G(c))=\lim_{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\mathcal{C}}\Hom(F(c^{\prime}),G(c)).

We start by extending this formula to a relative setting of (co)fibrations of (co)operads and then to the enriched setting.

Definition 4.72
.

Let
p:\mathcal{C}\to I
be a morphism of operads. The operad

(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/I}.

consists of objects
(c^{\prime},c,\nu)
where
\nu:p(c)\to p(c^{\prime})
is an active morphism.
Morphisms
(c^{\prime},c,\nu)\to(e^{\prime},e,\mu)
are morphisms in
{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I

\textstyle{p(c^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{p(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{p(e^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\textstyle{p(e)}

(42)

together with a lift
\iota_{\bullet}c\to e
(over
\alpha
) and
e^{\prime}\to\iota^{\prime}_{\bullet}c^{\prime}
(over
\alpha^{\prime}
).
(Notice that a push-forward along inert morphisms exists by the definition of operad. )

One checks that
(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/I}\to\mathcal{C}
is a cofibration and that
(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/I}
is an operad.
If
I
is a category then more simply

(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/I}=(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})\times_{/I^{\op}\times I}{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I.

In any case there is a functor

\iota:{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\mathcal{C}\to(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/I}

which is a discrete cofibration of operads. It is classified by a functor of operads

\Hom:(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/I}\to(\Set,\times)

(43)

such that we have

\Hom(X,Y,\tau)^{\times}=\Hom_{\tau}(X,Y)

where the RHS is the set of morphisms mapping to
\tau
in
I
and
(-)^{\times}
is the obvious functor
\Set^{\times}\to\Set
where
\Set^{\times}
is the underlying category (of operators) of
(\Set,\times)
, i.e. with
\Set^{\times}_{[n]}=\Set^{n}
.

Definition 4.73
.

Let

F:(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/I}\to(\mathcal{D},\times)

c^{\prime},c,\nu\mapsto F_{\nu}(c^{\prime},c)

be a functor of operads.
Then the
relative end
of
F
is defined as

\int_{\mathcal{C}/I}F:=\lim_{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\mathcal{C}}(F\circ\iota)^{\times}.

For
I=\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}
it is also called the
operadic end
. Recall that
(-)^{\times}:\mathcal{D}^{\times}\to\mathcal{D}
is the functor on
the category of operators given by
\times
. In the limit expression
{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\mathcal{C}
is just considered as category of operators.

Remark 4.74
.

By Lemma
2.51
there is a localization
\mathrm{tw}^{\op}\,\mathcal{C}\to{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}\mathcal{C}
. Therefore it is also the same as just the limit over the dual twisted arrow category of the
category of operators.

Lemma 4.75
.

We have

\textstyle{\int_{\mathcal{C}/I}F\cong\lim(\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{[1]}}F_{\id_{p(c)}}(c,c)}
\textstyle{\prod_{c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C},c\in\mathcal{C}_{[1]},\nu:p(c^{\prime})\to p(c)}\Hom(\Hom_{\nu}(c^{\prime},c),F_{\nu}(c^{\prime},c)))}

where
\nu
is an active morphism in
I
, where one of the arrows is induced by

\displaystyle\prod_{i}F_{\id_{p(c_{i})}}(c_{i},c_{i})\times\Hom_{\nu}(c,c^{\prime})\to F_{\id_{p(c)}}(c,c)^{\times}\times\Hom_{\nu}(c,c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\to F_{\nu}(c,c^{\prime})

where the
c_{i}
are the components of
c
and the isomorphism

\prod_{i}F_{\id_{p(c_{i})}}(c_{i},c_{i})\cong F_{\id_{p(c)}}(c,c)^{\times}

follows because
F
is a functor of operads.

Proposition 4.76
.

Let
F,G:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor between operads over an operad
I
.
Then

\Hom_{/I}(F,G)\cong\int_{\mathcal{C}/I}\Hom_{\nu}(F(c^{\prime}),G(c)).

We also have a relative Yoneda:

.

Given a functor of operads

4.78.

From the Proposition the following formula follows:

\int_{\mathcal{C}/I}F_{\nu}(c^{\prime},c)\cong\int_{((\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})/I)/\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}\Hom_{\tau}(\Hom_{\nu^{\prime}}(c^{\prime},d^{\prime}),F_{\nu}(c,d)).

(44)

4.79.

Given simplicially enriched functors between simplicially enriched operads
F,G:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, we first define the structure of
simplicially enriched operad on
(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/I}
defining with the notation of (
42
)

\underline{\Hom}((c^{\prime},c,\nu),(e^{\prime},e,\mu)):=\prod_{\Hom_{{}^{\uparrow\downarrow}I}(\nu,\mu)}\underline{\Hom}_{\alpha}(\iota_{\bullet}c,e)\times\underline{\Hom}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(e^{\prime},\iota^{\prime}_{\bullet}c^{\prime}).

It is immediate that the functor

\Hom:(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/I}\to(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)

c,c^{\prime},\nu\mapsto\underline{\Hom}_{\nu_{0}}(c^{\prime}_{0},c_{0}),\dots,\underline{\Hom}_{\nu_{n}}(c^{\prime}_{n},c_{n})

becomes simplicially enriched via composition in
\mathcal{C}
.
For a simplicially enriched functor

F:(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/I}\to(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)

(45)

we define the
relative enriched end
as

\textstyle{\int_{\mathcal{C}/I}F:=\lim(\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{[1]}}F_{\id_{p(c)}}(c,c)}
\textstyle{\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C},c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{[1]},\nu:p(c)\to p(c^{\prime})}\underline{\Hom}(\underline{\Hom}_{\nu}(c,c^{\prime}),F_{\nu}(c,c^{\prime}))).}

We also have
for the enriched natural transformations

\underline{\Hom}_{I}(F,G)\cong\int_{\mathcal{C}/I}\underline{\Hom}_{\nu}(F(c^{\prime}),G(c))

with the
relative enriched
end.

The set of coherent transformations (Cordier-Porter)
\underline{\Coh}_{I}(F,G)
is a coherent enhancement of this (cf.
[
19
]
for the case of simplicially enriched
categories
).

relative coherent end
as

\oint_{\mathcal{C}/I}F:=\int_{((\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})/I)/\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}\underline{\Hom}_{\tau}(\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c^{\prime},d^{\prime}),F_{\nu}(c,d))

where the RHS is the operadic enriched end.

Definition 4.82
.

Given simplicially enriched functors between simplicially enriched operads
F,G:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
over
I
, define the simplicial set of
coherent transformations
over
I
as

\underline{\Coh}_{I}(F,G):=\oint_{\mathcal{C}/I}\underline{\Hom}_{\nu}(F(c^{\prime}),G(c))

using the relative coherent end.

4.83.

We will be interested mainly in the case in which the structure on
\mathcal{C}
is
discrete
in which case

\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu}(c^{\prime},c)=N(c^{\prime}\times_{/\mathcal{C}_{\act}}\mathcal{C}_{\act}\times_{/\mathcal{C}_{\act}}c)_{\nu}

i.e. the fiber of the nerve of the over-/undercategory associated with
c^{\prime}
and
c
in
\mathcal{C}_{\act}
over
\nu
,
and the coherent end is defined in terms of the usual operadic end (not enriched). In this case a coherent transformation
F\Rightarrow G
is given by
morphisms
F(c)\to G(c)
for all objects
c
and for each (active) morphism
c\to c^{\prime}
1-simplices

\textstyle{F(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Rightarrow}
\textstyle{F(c^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(c^{\prime})}

connecting the two compositions and then for each composition
c\to c^{\prime}\to c^{\prime\prime}
a 2-simplex connecting appropriate compositions of the
0-simplices associated with
c
,
c^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime\prime}
, and 1-simplices associated with
c\to c^{\prime}
,
c^{\prime}\to c^{\prime\prime}
and
c\to c^{\prime\prime}
, and so forth. This is very similar to a lax natural transformation which is indeed the special case in which
the enrichment is in 1-categories (considered as simplicial sets).

Lemma 4.84
.

There is an injection

\underline{\Hom}_{I}(F,G)\to\underline{\Coh}_{I}(F,G)

Proof.

The morphism is induced by the obvious simplicially enriched natural transformation

\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu}(-,-)\to\underline{\Hom}_{\nu}(-,-)

given by composition and (
44
).
∎

There is the following important relation between coherent transformations and the naive deformations (Definition
4.33
):

Proposition 4.85
.

Consider simplicially enriched operads
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D},I
with discrete enrichment on
I
.
Let
\mathcal{C}\to I
and
\mathcal{D}\to I
be cofibrations of simplicially enriched operads.
Consider
Y:\mathcal{C}^{\flat}\to\mathcal{D}^{\flat}
and two
Y_{0},Y_{1}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
over
I
that extend
Y
as in
4.32
.

There is a natural morphism

\exp:\underline{\Hom}(\Delta_{1},\underline{\Def}_{Y}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}))_{Y_{0},Y_{1}}\to\underline{\Coh}_{I}(Y_{0},Y_{1}).

Proof.

An element
\mu\in\Delta_{n}\times\Delta_{1}\to\underline{\mathrm{Def}}_{Y}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is given by morphisms

\mu_{c,c^{\prime}}:\underline{\Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(c,c^{\prime})\to\underline{\Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}(\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{n}\times Y(c),Y(c^{\prime}))

(46)

compatible with their projections to
\Hom_{I}(p(c),p(c^{\prime}))
and constant equal to
Y
over identities,
compatible with composition (via the diagonals
\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{n}\to(\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{n})^{2}
),
and specialising to
Y_{0}
, resp.
Y_{1}
when restricted to
\{0\}
and
\{1\}
(constant in
\Delta_{n}
).

It is mapped to the following element
\exp(\mu)\in\underline{\Coh}_{I}(Y_{0},Y_{1})_{[n]}
of the (enriched) end: It maps the collection

\Delta_{m}\times c\to c_{0},\Delta_{m}\times c_{0}\to c_{1},\dots,\Delta_{m}\times c_{m}\to c^{\prime}

(47)

of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}

to the following morphism

\Delta_{n}\times\Delta_{m}\times Y_{0}(c)=Y(c)\to Y_{1}(c^{\prime})=Y(c^{\prime}).

Inserting the morphisms (
47
) into (
46
) gives rise to a collection

\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{n}\times\Delta_{m}\times Y(c_{i})\to Y(c_{i+1})

Composing gives a morphism

(\Delta_{1})^{m+2}\times(\Delta_{n})^{m+2}\times(\Delta_{m})^{m+2}\times Y(c)\to Y(c^{\prime}).

This yields a morphism

\Delta_{n}\times\Delta_{m}\times Y(c)\to Y(c^{\prime})

mapping
\Delta_{n}
to
(\Delta_{n})^{m+2}
diagonally,
\Delta_{m}
to
(\Delta_{m})^{m+2}
diagonally
and to
(\Delta_{1})^{m+2}
via the
m+2
morphisms
\Delta_{m}\to\Delta_{1}
in their natural order, the first being constant 0 and the last constant 1.

We have to show that this is compatible with face maps.
We have a commutative diagram for
j=0,\dots,m
(omitting for simplicity the
\Delta_{n}
-factor):

\textstyle{\Delta_{m-1}\times G(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{j}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{1}^{j}\times\Delta_{1}\times\Delta_{1}^{m-j}\times G(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta_{m}\times G(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta_{1}^{j}\times\Delta_{1}^{2}\times\Delta_{1}^{m-j}\times G(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\Delta_{1}^{j}\times\Delta_{1}\times G(c_{m-j-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
1
\textstyle{\Delta_{1}^{j}\times G(c_{m-j+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(c^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta_{1}^{j}\times\Delta_{1}^{2}\times G(c_{m-j-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta_{1}^{j}\times\Delta_{1}\times G(c_{m-j})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G(c^{\prime})}

with the diagonal
\delta:\Delta_{1}\to\Delta_{1}^{2}
, setting

and
c_{n+1}=c^{\prime}
, in which the square

is commutative by definition of
\underline{\mathrm{Def}}_{Y}
(cf. Definition

).
It is clear that the maps fulfill the defining compatibilies of the enriched end.
∎

4.10
Composition of coherent transformations

4.86.

First we discuss the composition of coherent transformation — for simplicity — in the case in which the simplicial structure on the source is discrete.
In this case two coherent transformation
f:X\Rightarrow Y
and
g:Y\Rightarrow Z
are determined by maps that associate to an
n
-simplex of the nerve of the following form

\mu:i=i_{0}\to\dots\to i_{n}=i^{\prime}

elements

f(\mu)\in\underline{\Hom}(X(i),Y(i^{\prime}))_{[n]}\qquad g(\mu)\in\underline{\Hom}(Y(i),Z(i^{\prime}))_{[n]}

and it is not immediately possible to compose them. This is different after applying the AW components
\delta_{l}:[i]\to[n]
and
\delta_{r}:[{n-i}]\to[{n}]
which yields pairs:

\delta_{l}\mu=(i=i_{0}\to\cdots\to i_{i}\to i^{\prime})\qquad\delta_{r}\mu=(i\to i_{i}\to\cdots\to i_{n}=i^{\prime})

so, by the relations in the relative end, thus

f(\mu)_{i}=f(\mu_{i})\circ X(i_{i}\to i^{\prime})\qquad g(\mu)_{j}=Y(i\to i_{i})\circ g(\mu_{j})

where
\mu_{i}=i=i_{0}\to\cdots\to i_{i}=i_{i}
and
\mu_{j}=i_{i}\to i_{i}\to\cdots\to i_{n}=i^{\prime}
.
Now
f(\mu_{i})
and
g(\mu_{j})
are elements in
\underline{\Hom}(X(i),Y(i_{i}))_{[i]}
, and
\underline{\Hom}(Y(i_{i}),Z(i^{\prime}))_{[n-i]}
, respectively.
In the Abelian case, we can
compose with the Eilenberg-Zilber map and then compose

\displaystyle\Hom(X(i),Y(i_{i}))_{i}\otimes\Hom(Y(i_{i}),Z(i^{\prime}))_{j}

\displaystyle\to\Hom(X(i),Y(i_{i}))_{n}\otimes\Hom(Y(i_{i}),Z(i^{\prime}))_{n}\to\Hom(X(i),Y(i^{\prime}))_{n}.

(Note that in case of the enrichment
F\Aw\Hom_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(X,Y)
the composition morphism even factors over
\Hom(X(i_{0}),Y(i_{i}))_{i}\times\Hom(Y(i_{i}),Z(i_{n}))_{j}
, cf. Lemma
5.29
).

To discuss this a bit more conceptually, and without the assumption that simplicial structure on the source is discrete, first a Proposition:

Proposition 4.87
.

The simplicial object
\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu}(c,d)
(cf. Definition
4.80
) has the following property: There is a morphism

\rho:\dec^{*}\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\tau}(c,e)\to\int^{d}\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu}(c,d^{\prime})\boxtimes\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\mu}(d,e)

(where the coend is the enriched coend) which is an isomorphism, if the structure is discrete. Here the RHS is an enriched colimit over:

\textstyle{{}^{\uparrow}d}
\textstyle{p(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{p(e)}
\textstyle{{}^{\downarrow}d^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p(d^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\scriptstyle{\nu}

Proof.

The LHS at
i,j
is a union of

\displaystyle\cong\coprod_{c_{0},\dots,c_{i},d_{0},\dots,d_{j}}\underline{\Hom}_{\tau_{0}}(c,c_{0})_{n}\times\underline{\Hom}_{\tau_{1}}(c_{1},c_{2})_{n}\times\cdots

\displaystyle\times\underline{\Hom}_{\tau_{i}}(c_{i},d_{0})_{n}\times\underline{\Hom}_{\tau_{i+1}}(d_{1},d_{2})_{n}\times\cdots\times\underline{\Hom}_{\tau_{n}}(d_{n},e)_{n}

over all decompositions of
\tau
, which is

\displaystyle=\int^{d}\coprod_{c_{0},\dots,c_{i},d_{0},\dots,d_{j}}\underline{\Hom}_{\nu_{0}}(c,c_{0})_{n}\times\underline{\Hom}_{\nu_{1}}(c_{1},c_{2})_{n}\times\cdots

\displaystyle\times\underline{\Hom}_{\nu_{i}}(c_{i},d)_{n}\times\underline{\Hom}_{\mu_{0}}(d,d_{0})_{n}\times\underline{\Hom}_{\mu_{1}}(d_{1},d_{2})_{n}\times\cdots\times\underline{\Hom}_{\mu_{j}}(d_{n},e)_{n}

by relative Yoneda, Proposition
4.77
, 2.
Applying the AW components
\delta_{l},\delta_{r}
(which is obviously an isomorphism in the discrete case) gives:

\displaystyle\to\int^{d}\coprod_{c_{0},\dots,c_{i}}\underline{\Hom}_{\nu_{0}}(c,c_{0})_{i}\times\underline{\Hom}_{\nu_{1}}(c_{1},c_{2})_{i}\times\cdots

\displaystyle\times\underline{\Hom}_{\nu_{i}}(c_{i},d)_{i}\times\coprod_{d_{0},\dots,d_{i}}\underline{\Hom}_{\mu_{0}}(d,d_{0})_{j}\times\underline{\Hom}_{\mu_{1}}(d_{1},d_{2})_{j}\times\cdots\times\underline{\Hom}_{\mu_{j}}(d_{n},e)_{j}

Taking the union one arrives at

\int^{d}\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu}(c,d^{\prime})\boxtimes\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\mu}(d,e)

4.88.

Now we are in a position to formalize the composition discussed in
4.86
:

\displaystyle\underline{\Coh}_{I}(F,G)\times\underline{\Coh}_{I}(G,H)=\int_{c,d,e,f}\underline{\Hom}_{\pi(\tau)}(\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c^{\prime},d^{\prime}),\underline{\Hom}_{\nu}(F(c),G(d)))\times

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}_{\pi(\tau^{\prime})}(\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\mu^{\prime}}(e^{\prime},f^{\prime}),\underline{\Hom}_{\mu}(G(e),H(f)))

where the end is a relative enriched end, an enriched limit over:

\textstyle{c\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{d}
\textstyle{e\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f}
\textstyle{p(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{p(d)}
\textstyle{p(e)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{p(f)}
\textstyle{c^{\prime}}
\textstyle{d^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{e^{\prime}}
\textstyle{f^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p(c^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{\prime}}
\textstyle{p(d^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{p(e^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{\prime}}
\textstyle{p(f^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{\prime}}

This can be extended to an enriched limit over:

\textstyle{c\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{e\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f}
\textstyle{p(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{p(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{p(e)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{p(f)}
\textstyle{c^{\prime}}
\textstyle{d^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{e^{\prime}}
\textstyle{f^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p(c^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{\prime}}
\textstyle{p(d^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime}}
\textstyle{p(e^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{\prime}}
\textstyle{p(f^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{\prime}}

And the map to

\textstyle{c\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f}
\textstyle{c^{\prime}}
\textstyle{f^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{p(c)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p(f)}
\textstyle{p(c^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p(f^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

is a fibration.

We arrive at an end over

\displaystyle\to\int_{c,d\to e,f}\underline{\Hom}_{\pi(\tau)}(\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c^{\prime},d^{\prime})\times\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\mu^{\prime}}(e^{\prime},f^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}_{\nu}(F(c),G(d))\times\underline{\Hom}_{\mu}(G(e),H(f)))

where in the products
\times
the factors are assembled using the new maps
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
. Using the composition in the enrichment of
\mathcal{D}
this maps to:

\displaystyle\to\int_{c,f}\underline{\Hom}_{\pi(\tau)}(\int^{d}\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c^{\prime},d^{\prime})\times\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\mu^{\prime}}(d,f^{\prime}),\Hom_{\mu}(F(c),H(f))).

Composing with
\delta^{*}
applied to the map

\rho:\dec^{*}\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c,d^{\prime})\to\int^{d}\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c,d^{\prime})\boxtimes\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\mu^{\prime}}(d,e)

of Proposition
4.87
we have a map to

\displaystyle\to\int_{c,f}\underline{\Hom}_{\pi(\tau)}(\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c^{\prime},f^{\prime}),\underline{\Hom}_{\mu}(F(c),H(f))).

Defining a cosimplicial simplicial set by

X_{n,m}:=\int_{c,f}\Hom_{\pi(\tau)}(\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c^{\prime},f^{\prime})_{n},\underline{\Hom}(F(c),H(f))_{m})

this can be writen as

\int_{n}\underline{\Hom}(\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\Delta_{n},X_{n,\bullet})

(This is also

=\int_{n}X_{\dec\delta n,m}

in degree 0. )

Hence to define a composition one must specify a morphism

\int_{n}\underline{\Hom}(\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\Delta_{n},X_{n,\bullet})\to\int_{n}\underline{\Hom}(\Delta_{n},X_{n,\bullet}).

(48)

1.

Case
\underline{\Hom}=F\Aw\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}
with
\mathcal{C}
Abelian (i.e. the usual simplicial enrichment of complexes):

Here
X_{n,\bullet}
is of the form
FX_{n,\bullet}^{\prime}
where
X_{n,\bullet}^{\prime}
is a cosimplicial object in complexes of Abelian groups, and

\int_{n}\underline{\Hom}(\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\Delta_{n},FX_{n,\bullet}^{\prime})=\int_{n}\underline{\Hom}(\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[\Delta_{n}],X_{n,\bullet}^{\prime})

and we may just compose with the “EZ-counit” (cf.
4.62
and Definition
4.63
):
\mlq c^{\op}\mrq:\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[\Delta_{n}]\to\mathbb{Z}[\Delta_{n}]
.

This composition can be described differently.
In this case, we arrive at an end over

\displaystyle\to\int_{c,d\to e,f}\underline{\Hom}_{\pi(\tau)}(\mathbb{Z}[\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c^{\prime},d^{\prime})]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\mu^{\prime}}(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})],

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}_{\nu}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(F(c),G(d))\boxtimes\underline{\Hom}_{\mu}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(G(e),H(f)))

and can apply
\dec_{*}
to arrive at

\displaystyle\to\int_{c,d\to e,f}\underline{\Hom}_{\pi(\tau)}(\dec_{*}\mathbb{Z}[\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c^{\prime},d^{\prime})]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\mu^{\prime}}(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})],

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}_{\nu}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(F(c),G(d))\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\underline{\Hom}_{\mu}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(G(e),H(f)))

Now apply the composition in
\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}
directly to arrive at

\displaystyle\to\int_{c,e}\underline{\Hom}_{\pi(\tau)}(\int^{d}\dec_{*}\mathbb{Z}[\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c^{\prime},d)]\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}[\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\mu^{\prime}}(d^{\prime},e^{\prime})],

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}_{\nu}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(F(c),H(e)))

Then, as
\dec_{*}\cong\tot
clearly commutes with colimits in the category of complexes of Abelian groups, using Proposition
4.87
, this maps to

\displaystyle\to\int_{c,e}\underline{\Hom}_{\pi(\tau)}(\dec_{*}\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c^{\prime},e^{\prime})],\underline{\Hom}_{\nu}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(F(c),H(e)))

and after compositing with the unit at

\displaystyle\to\int_{c,e}\underline{\Hom}_{\pi(\tau)}(\mathbb{Z}[\widehat{\underline{\Hom}}_{\nu^{\prime}}(c^{\prime},e^{\prime})],\underline{\Hom}_{\nu}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(F(c),H(e)))

It is an exercise to see that this yields the same composition, and also that it is the abstract description of the construction in
4.86
.

2.

General case.
In
[
19
, Theorem 4.4]
you may find a construction of a map (
48
), if
X_{n,\bullet}
is (point-wise in
n
) a weak Kan complex (i.e. a quasi-category).
Since the “EZ-counit” always exists (without passing to the homotopy category)
on symmetric objects
, i.e. in objects in the image of
\iota^{*}
, one could try to proceed as follows:
Resolve the canonical cosimplicial object
\Delta_{n}\hookrightarrow\iota^{*}\Delta_{n,s}
into something symmetric, e.g.
\Delta_{n}\hookrightarrow\iota^{*}\mathfrak{C}\Delta_{n}
(Lemma
4.57
).
If this can be done in such a way that there is a lift (functorial in the cosimplicial direction)

\textstyle{\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\Delta_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{n,\bullet}}
\textstyle{\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\iota^{*}\Delta_{n,s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

assuming, say, that
X_{n,\bullet}
is a (weak) Kan complex, then we can compose the lifted morphism with the “EZ-counit” also here

\textstyle{\Delta_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\iota^{*}\Delta_{n,s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{\mlq c\mrq}
\textstyle{\iota^{*}\delta_{s}^{*}\dec_{s}^{*}\Delta_{n,s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\iota^{*}\Delta_{n,s}}

to get the morphism

\int_{n}\underline{\Hom}(\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\Delta_{n},X_{n,\bullet})\to\int_{n}\underline{\Hom}(\Delta_{n},X_{n,\bullet}).

I ignore whether this is possible. Notice that
point-wise (fixing
n
)
such a lift exists, if we take
\Delta_{n,s}:=\mathfrak{C}\Delta_{n}
(Lemma
4.57
) and if
X_{n,\bullet}
is a Kan complex, for the morphism
\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\Delta_{n}\to\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\iota^{*}\Delta_{n,s}
is clearly injective and a weak equivalence because
\delta^{*}\dec^{*}
preserves weak equivalences by Lemma
4.56
. It is therefore a trivial cofibration.

4.11
Explicit formulæ

The following gives explicit formulas for
\dec_{!}
and
\dec_{*}
in the general case.
If
\mathcal{C}
is Abelian, they can be simplified, cf. Proposition
5.39
.

Proposition 4.89
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a (
\infty
-)category.

1.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is finite cocomplete. Then a left adjoint
\dec_{!}
of
\dec^{*}
exists and is given point-wise by:

(\dec_{!}X)_{n}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}[i]\ast[j]=[n]\\
[i],[j]\in\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}\end{subarray}}(i_{!}X)_{i,j}

where
(i_{!}X)_{i,j}=X_{i,j}
for
[i]\not=\emptyset
and
[j]\not=\emptyset
and
(i_{!}X)_{i,\emptyset}=\colim X_{i,-}
and the same switched.

2.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is finite complete. Then a right adjoint
\dec_{*}
of
\dec^{*}
, which is often called the
Artin-Mazur codiagonal
or, when
\mathcal{C}=\Set
, the
total simplicial set
, exists, and is point-wise given by:

\displaystyle(\dec_{*}X)_{[n]}=

\displaystyle\lim\left(\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.9092pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.9092pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 43.9393pt\raise-24.16669pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 54.15868pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 96.40816pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 179.94012pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.44446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 35.9092pt\raise-31.44446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X_{[n-1],[0]}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 114.65764pt\raise-31.44446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 156.90712pt\raise-31.44446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ \cdots\ }$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\right)

where the maps are
(\id,\delta_{0}):X_{[i],[n-i]}\to X_{[i],[n-i-1]}
and
(\delta_{i+1},\id):X_{[i+1],[n-i-1]}\to X_{[i],[n-i-1]}
.

Proof.

1. follows from
\dec_{\emptyset}
being a cofibration with discrete fibers (cf. Lemma
4.11
) and the formula
\dec_{!}=\iota^{*}\dec_{\emptyset,!}(\iota,\iota)_{!}
(Lemma
A.5
).
For 2. we have to establish a final functor

Z\to[n]\times_{/\Delta^{\op}}(\Delta^{\op})^{2}

where
Z
is the zig-zag shaped category depicted in the statement. This can be done by mapping

(i,j),i+j=n
to the obvious composition
[n]\cong[i]\ast^{\prime}[j]\twoheadleftarrow[i]\ast[j]
and

(i,j),i+j=n-1
to the isomorphism
[n]\cong[i]\ast[j]
. Morphisms are mapped to the obvious face maps. To
see that it is final, factor

Z\to([n]\times_{/\Delta^{\op}}(\Delta^{\op})^{2})^{\prime}\to[n]\times_{/\Delta^{\op}}(\Delta^{\op})^{2}

where
([n]\times_{/\Delta^{\op}}(\Delta^{\op})^{2})^{\prime}
is the full subcategory of those morphisms
\alpha:[n]\leftarrow[i]\ast[j]
such that the composition with the faces
[i]\ast[j]\hookleftarrow[i]
and

[i]\ast[j]\hookleftarrow[j]
is a face. Notice that this is a poset.
This inclusion has a right adjoint given by factoring
[n]\leftarrow[i]\ast[j]
into
[n]\hookleftarrow[i^{\prime}]\ast[j^{\prime}]\twoheadleftarrow[i]\ast[j]
, the epi-mono factorization, and is thus
\infty
-final.
The posets

\alpha\times_{/([n]\times_{/\Delta^{\op}}(\Delta^{\op})^{2})^{\prime}}Z

are all isomorphic to connected subposets of
Z
which are obviously contractible.
∎

5
Abelian foundations

5.1
Dold-Kan

Theorem 5.1

(Dold-Kan)
.

For an Abelian category
\mathcal{C}
, there is an equivalence of categories

\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C})\cong\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}.

One elegant way of formulating this starts with the observation that functors

\Delta^{\op}\to\mathcal{C}

are the same as additive functors

\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\op}]\to\mathcal{C}

and, since in
\mathcal{C}
all idempotents split, the same as additive functors

\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\op}]^{\Kar}\to\mathcal{C}

where
\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\op}]^{\Kar}
is the Karoubi closure, in which splittings for all idempotents are formally adjoined to
\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\op}]
.
Similarly,
\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C})
is the same as the category of additive functors

\mathcal{CH}_{\geq 0}\to\mathcal{C}

where
\mathcal{CH}_{\geq 0}
is the additive category consisting of finite coproducts (in fact biproducts) of objects
D_{i}
,
i\geq 0
with

\displaystyle\Hom(D_{i},D_{j})=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}&\text{if }i\in\{j,j+1\}\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}.

(49)

One can therefore state the Dold-Kan theorem as

Theorem 5.2

(Dold-Kan (variant))
.

There is an equivalence of categories:

\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\op}]^{\Kar}\cong\mathcal{CH}_{\geq 0}.

Instead of trying to prove this directly, it is, however, easier to realize the dual categories
\Delta
and
\mathcal{CH}^{\op}_{\geq 0}
inside

\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab^{\fg})
. This also gives explicit functors.

5.3.

We first define

\Gamma:\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}\to\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C})

called functor of
normalized chains
,
setting:
\Gamma(C)_{n}:=C^{\nd}_{n}
and

\dd:=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\delta_{i}
. It is an easy verification that
\dd^{2}=0
and that
\dd
descends to a map
C^{\nd}_{n}\to C^{\nd}_{n-1}
.
The functoriality in
C
is clear.

This allows us to define the following cosimplicial object in
\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{\Ab^{\fg}})
, applying
\Gamma
w.r.t.
\mathcal{C}=\Ab^{\fg}
:

Definition 5.4
.

\Delta^{\circ}_{n}:=\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}[\Delta_{n}]).

The following follows directly from the definition:

Lemma 5.5
.

We have explicitly

(\Delta^{\circ}_{n})_{m}=\mathbb{Z}[\Hom_{\Delta}^{\inj}([m],[n])]

whose basis can be identified with subsets
S\subset\{0,\dots,n\}
of Cardinality
m+1
.
The differential is given on basis elements by

\dd:[S]\mapsto\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}[S\setminus\{x_{i}\}]

where
S=\{x_{0},\dots,x_{m}\}
with
x_{0}<\dots<x_{m}
.

This not only defines a cosimplicial object but even

Lemma 5.6
.

The association (
5.4
), a priori a functor
\Delta\to\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab^{\fg})
, extends to a functor

\Delta^{\circ}:\FinSet\to\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab^{\fg}).

Proof.

An arbitrary map
\alpha:\{0,\dots,n\}\to\{0,\dots,n^{\prime}\}
induces

(\Delta^{\circ}_{n})_{m}\to(\Delta^{\circ}_{n^{\prime}})_{m}

mapping (a basis element identified as in Lemma
5.5
)

[S]\mapsto\begin{cases}[\alpha(S)]&\text{if $\#\alpha(S)=\#S$ and the induced permutation is even,}\\
-[\alpha(S)]&\text{if $\#\alpha(S)=\#S$ and the induced permutation is odd,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(The “induced permutation” is the permutation of
\alpha(S)
bringing the elements
\alpha(x_{0}),\dots,\alpha(x_{n})
into the correct order).
One has to check that this respects the differential. This holds by construction if
\alpha
is order preserving, i.e. a morphism of
\Delta
. Furthermore, for the neighboring transposition
(i\ i+1)
the formula

(i\ i+1)=-\id+d_{i}s_{i}+d_{i+1}s_{i}

(50)

holds true — which is easily checked on a basis —
and morphisms in
\FinSet
are obviously generated by morphisms in
\Delta
and neighboring transpositions. Therefore this respects the differential in any case.
∎

5.7.

Similarly, also
\mathcal{CH}_{\geq 0}^{\op}
can be realized inside
\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab^{\fg})
, defining

D_{i}:=\left(\cdots\to 0\to\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0\to\cdots\to 0\right)

where the
\mathbb{Z}
are in degree
i
and (for
i>0
)
i-1
. The equality (
49
) is obvious. Note that

\Hom(D_{i},C)\cong C_{i}

(51)

for all
i\geq 0
and
C\in\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab)
.

To prove Dold-Kan using
\Delta^{\circ}
, the following is crucial:

Key Lemma 5.8
.

We have
42
42

42

\Delta^{\circ}
is a cosimplicial object.
The non-degenerate part, and also references to Lemma
4.5
, are meant w.r.t. the
simplicial
object
(\Delta^{\circ})^{\op}:\Delta^{\op}\to\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab^{\fg})^{\op}.

\Delta^{\circ,\nd}_{n}\cong D_{n}.

Proof.

It follows from formula (
50
) that, on the non-codegenerate part
\Delta^{\circ,\nd}_{n}
, i.e. the joint kernel of all the degeneracies,
S_{n}
acts by the sign character.

(\Delta^{\circ}_{n})_{n}
is one dimensional, generated by
[\{0,\dots,n\}]
which does transform according to the sign character.

(\Delta^{\circ}_{n})_{n-1}
is generated by
[\{0,\dots,\widehat{i},\dots,n\}]
,
i=0,\dots,n
. Applying the transposition
(i\ j)
we see that (for an element transforming according to the sign character) the coefficient
of
[\{0,\dots,\widehat{i},\dots,n\}]
must be
(-1)^{i-j}
times the coefficient of
[\{0,\dots,\widehat{j},\dots,n\}]
. Hence
(\Delta^{\circ,\nd}_{n})_{n-1}
is one dimensional as well, generated by

\dd[\{0,\dots,n\}]
. The basis element
[S]
for any
S\subset\{0,\dots,n\}
with at least two elements
i,j
missing is fixed by the transposition
(i\ j)
. Hence

(\Delta^{\circ,\nd}_{n})_{m}=0
for
m<n-1
.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

The induced functor

\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\circ}]:\mathbb{Z}[\Delta]\to\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab)

is fully-faithful.

Proof.

Fix
n
and consider the morphism of simplicial Abelian groups

\mathbb{Z}[\Hom_{\Delta}([m],[n])]\to\Hom(\Delta^{\circ}_{m},\Delta^{\circ}_{n}).

By Lemma
4.5
it suffices to show that it is an isomorphism for the non-degenerate quotient, i.e.

\mathbb{Z}[\Hom^{\inj}_{\Delta}([m],[n])]\cong\Hom(\Delta^{\circ,\nd}_{m},\Delta^{\circ}_{n}).

Since
\Delta^{\circ,\nd}_{m}\cong D_{m}
by Lemma
5.8
, this follows directly from the Definition of
\Delta^{\circ}_{n}
(cf. Lemma
5.5
).
∎

Lemma
5.8
together with Lemma
4.5
(applied to the
simplicial
object
(\Delta^{\circ})^{\op}:\Delta^{\op}\to\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab^{\fg})^{\op}
) gives an isomorphism

\gr\Delta^{\circ}_{m}\cong\bigoplus_{\Delta_{m}\twoheadrightarrow\Delta_{n}}D_{n}

and since the
D_{n}
are obviously projective by (
51
), we have
non-canonically

\displaystyle\Delta^{\circ}_{m}\cong\bigoplus_{\Delta_{m}\twoheadrightarrow\Delta_{n}}D_{n}.

(52)

This gives a proof of Dold-Kan in the first variant because this decomposition exists thus in
\mathbb{Z}[\Delta]^{\Kar}
by Corollary
5.9
.

5.10.

As a complex
C=\Delta_{n}^{\circ}
has also the canonical filtration:

F^{m}C:=\left(\cdots\to C_{m+2}\to C_{m+1}\to\dd(C_{m+1})\to 0\to\cdots\to 0.\right)

Isomorphism (
52
) shows a posteriori that on
\Delta^{\circ}_{n}
, the filtration by codegeneracy degree and the canonical filtration agree.

We now proceed to give a second, more explicit, proof of Dold-Kan in the following variant

Theorem 5.11

(Dold-Kan (2nd variant))
.

The functors

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C})}
\scriptstyle{N}

given by

N(C):\Delta_{n}\mapsto\mathcal{HOM}_{r}(\Delta_{n}^{\circ},C)

and

R(A):=\int^{n}\Delta_{n}^{\circ}\otimes A_{[n]}

constitute an adjoint equivalence of categories.

5.12.

Here the following is used. We have an obvious functor in two variables

\mathcal{HOM}_{l}:\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C})\to\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab)

(induced by the canonical enrichment
\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C}\to\Ab
) which has a (partial) left adjoint

\otimes:\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab^{\fg})\times\mathcal{C}\to\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C})

(induced by the tensoring
\mathcal{C}\times\Ab^{\fg}\to\mathcal{C}
) which has another right adjoint

\mathcal{HOM}_{r}:\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab^{\fg})^{\op}\times\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}.

(if
\mathcal{C}=\Ab^{\fg}
, then this is just the usual group of morphisms of complexes).

Proof.

The fact that
R
(realization) is left adjoint to
N
(nerve) holds in much greater generality and nothing has to be assumed about the cosimplicial object
\Delta^{\circ}:\Delta\to\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab)
.
The equivalence needs more assumptions. In the more general setting, usually
\Delta^{\circ}
is called
dense
, if
N
is fully-faithful.

Here the statement follows immediately from the following three facts:

(i)

N
is conservative.

(ii)

\mathcal{HOM}_{r}(\Delta^{\circ}_{n},-)
commutes with
\otimes
43
43

43

in the sense that
\mathcal{HOM}_{r}(\Delta^{\circ}_{n},A\otimes C)\cong\mathcal{HOM}(\Delta^{\circ}_{n},A)\otimes C
via the canonical morphism, for
A\in\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{AB})
and
C\in\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C})
and colimits.

(iii)

\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\circ}]:\mathbb{Z}[\Delta]\to\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ab^{\fg})
is fully-faithful.

Proof of the facts: (i) means that the functors
\mathcal{HOM}_{r}(\Delta_{n}^{\circ},-)
are jointly conservative. This is
certainly the case for the functors
\mathcal{HOM}_{r}(D_{n},-)
. However, by (
52
), there are split epimorphisms
\Delta_{n}^{\circ}\to D_{n}
.
(ii) follows also immediately from (
52
) because obviously
\mathcal{HOM}_{r}(D_{n},-)
commutes with colimits and
\otimes
.
(iii) is Corollary
5.9
.

To prove the equivalence, it suffices to see that (ii) and (iii) imply that
R
is fully-faithful: We have (functorially in
n
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{HOM}_{r}(\Delta_{n}^{\circ},\int^{m}\Delta_{m}^{\circ}\otimes C_{[m]})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\int^{m}\mathcal{HOM}(\Delta_{n}^{\circ},\Delta_{m}^{\circ})\otimes C_{[m]}

(by ii)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\int^{m}\mathbb{Z}[\Hom_{\Delta}([n],[m])]\otimes C_{[m]}

(by iii)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\int^{m}\Hom_{\Delta}([n],[m])\times C_{[m]}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\,C_{[n]}

(coend Yoneda)

Remark 5.13
.

The functor
R
(realization) is nothing else then the functor
\Gamma
introduced earlier. In fact we have proven that

\mathcal{HOM}(\Delta_{n}^{\circ},R(C))=C_{[n]}

functorially in
[n]
and
D_{n}=\ker(\Delta_{n}^{\circ}\to\Delta_{k}^{\circ})
joint kernel of all
degeneracies
\Delta_{n}\twoheadrightarrow\Delta_{k}
.
Therefore

R(C)_{n}=\Hom(D_{n},R(C))=C_{[n]}^{\nd}

and the differential, which comes from the morphism
D_{n}\to D_{n+1}
, is given by the alternating sum of the
\delta_{i}
.

5.2
The coherent Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem for Abelian categories

We have seen in Lemma
4.58
that the symmetry operator
\mathfrak{C}
(cf. Lemma
4.57
) can, in the Abelian case, be given by an Abelian pro-functor (in two ways). Likewise, the natural transformations
\mathfrak{Aw}
and
\mathfrak{Ez}
can be given by morphisms of Abelian pro-functors
where, by abuse of notation,
\dec
denotes
\mathbb{Z}[\dec]:\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\op}]\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\op}]\to\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\op}]
and
\delta
denotes
\mathbb{Z}[\delta]:\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\op}]\to\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\op}]\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\Delta^{\op}]
:

\displaystyle\Aw:

\textstyle{\dec\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\op}}
\textstyle{\dec\,{}^{t}\!\dec\,\,{}^{t}\!\delta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}^{t}\!\delta}

\displaystyle\Ez:

\textstyle{\,{}^{t}\!\delta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}^{t}\!\delta\,{}^{t}\!\dec\,\dec}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\dec}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\,{}^{t}\!\delta\,{}^{t}\!\dec\,{}^{t}\!\iota\,C^{\op}\,\dec=\,{}^{t}\!\iota\,{}^{t}\!\delta_{s}\,{}^{t}\!\dec_{s}\,C^{\op}\,\dec\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c^{\op}}
\textstyle{\,{}^{t}\!\iota\,C^{\op}\,\dec}

It is immediate that for an Abelian category
\mathcal{C}
,
R_{\mathcal{C}}(\Aw)
is the map
\mathfrak{Aw}
, and
R_{\mathcal{C}}(\Ez)
is the map
\mathfrak{Ez}
, both defined in Definition
4.63
.
We will prove later (cf. Proposition
5.42
) that the latter are nothing but the classical Alexander-Whitney and Eilenberg-Zilber maps.

We have
\Ez\Aw=\id
already on the level of profunctors and:

Definition/Lemma 5.14
.

The following map
\Xi_{1}\in\underline{\Hom}(\delta,\delta)_{[1]}
(
4.37
)

\textstyle{\delta\dec\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\dec_{13,24}\delta_{12,34}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec_{13,24}\circ(\id,{}^{t}\!(\Aw\Ez))}
\textstyle{\dec_{13,24}\delta_{12,34}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\delta\dec}

is called the
Shih-operator
and constitutes a homotopy between
{}^{t}\!(\Aw\Ez)
and
\id
.

The fact that this yields a homotopy as claimed follows from (the proof of) Proposition
4.39
. It will be reproven in the following Theorem which yields a coherent version. Later we will derive an explicit formula (Proposition
5.46
)

Theorem 5.15
.

The (appropriate part of the) diagram of operads (
37
) extends to a diagram of
\Ab
-enriched operads and oplax pro-functors (cf.
2.43
)

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[(\FinSet,\coprod)]}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[(\Delta,\coprod)]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\delta}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[(\Delta,\ast)]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\dec}}
\scriptstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{forget}}

with
C\,\iota_{\dec}\cong\id
and setting

\AW:=\mathrm{forget}^{\op}\qquad\EZ:=(C\,\iota_{\delta})^{\op}

we have:

1.

Both morphisms are the identity on objects.

2.

Over
\delta_{1}:[2]\to[1]
, we reobtain the maps
\Aw
(
5.2
) and
\Ez
(
5.2
) as the oplaxness constraint.

3.

We have
\EZ\AW=\id
and there is a homotopy (see
4.40
for the Definition of
\underline{\Def}
on the level of pro-functors):

\Xi\in\underline{\Def}(\mathbb{Z}[(\Delta^{\op},\coprod)],\mathbb{Z}[(\Delta^{\op},\coprod)])_{\id,[1]}

connecting
(\EZ\AW)^{\vee}
and
\id
, which yields over
\delta_{1}
the Shih-operator defined in Definition
5.14
. There is also a homotopy

\Xi^{\vee}\in\underline{\Def}(\mathbb{Z}[(\Delta,\coprod)^{\op}],\mathbb{Z}[(\Delta,\coprod)^{\op}])_{\id,[1]}

connecting
\EZ\AW
and
\id
. (In both cases the simplicial structure comes from Proposition
4.29
.)

Proof.

All operads, being exponential, are given by functors

\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}=\Delta^{\op}\to(\AbCat^{\PF},\times)

mapping
\delta_{1}
to
\delta
,
{}^{t}\!\dec
and
\delta_{s}={}^{t}\!\dec_{s}
(on
\FinSet
).
The identification for the pro-functors associated with
\delta_{1}
in the bottom row are more precisely:

\displaystyle\iota(\delta):[n],[m];[k]\mapsto

\displaystyle\Hom_{(\Delta,\coprod)}([n],[m];[k])

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\Hom([n],[k])\times\Hom([m],[k])\cong\Hom(([n],[m]);\delta([k]))

\displaystyle{}^{t}\!\iota(\dec):[n],[m];[k]\mapsto

\displaystyle\Hom_{(\Delta,\ast)}([n],[m];[k])\cong\Hom(\underbrace{[n]\ast[m]}_{=:\dec([n],[m])};[k])

and the morphism “forget” is induced by the inclusions
[n]\hookrightarrow[n]\ast[m]
and
[m]\hookrightarrow[n]\ast[m]
(morphisms in
\Delta
).
The morphism (
5.2
) is the same map.

The existence of
C
and the isomorphism
C\iota_{\dec}\cong\id
will be shown in Lemma
5.17
below.
In the following diagram the left hand side composition is the laxness constraint from
C\,\iota_{\delta}
and the right hand side composition is the previous map (
5.2
):

\textstyle{{}^{t}\!\delta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
1
\textstyle{{}^{t}\!\delta\,{}^{t}\!\iota\,C^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\delta}}

Lemma 5.17
.

Proof.

It suffices to construct natural isomorphisms

(the constraint for
C

, whose mate in turn is the adjoint

)
and natural isomorphisms

\pi\,C\cong\pi.

Equivalently, we may construct:

\displaystyle L_{\Ab}(({}^{t}\!\dec)^{\op})L_{\Ab}(({}^{t}\!C)^{\op})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle L_{\Ab}(({}^{t}\!C)^{\op})L_{\Ab}(({}^{t}\!\dec)^{\op})

\displaystyle\rho:\dec^{*}_{s}\mathfrak{C}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}\dec^{*}

and
where

Given a complex

in

as

with the natural action of
\FinSet

\Delta_{n}^{\circ}
. We have

(\mathfrak{C}\dec^{*}X)_{[i],[j]}=\Hom(\Delta_{i}^{\circ}\boxtimes\Delta_{j}^{\circ},\dec^{*}X)=\Hom(\dec_{!}\Delta_{i}^{\circ}\boxtimes\Delta_{j}^{\circ},X)

with the natural action of

on

with the action of

via

There is a canonical isomorphism

and the assertion boils down to its compatibility with the actions of

.

Furthermore, we have

\pi\cong\pi\,\iota\,C\cong\pi\,C

This yields the morphism.
It has a mate which can also be described as

\pi\cong{}^{t}\!C\,{}^{t}\!\iota\,\pi\cong{}^{t}\!C\,\pi

It is also an isomorphism due to the finality of

We leave to the reader to check the compatibilities.

(

5.3
The higher Shih operators

Theorem
5.15
states that the value of

L_{(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee}}(\Xi^{\vee})\in\underline{\Def}((\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee},(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee})_{\id,[1]}

at
\delta_{1}:[2]\to[1]
is the Shih operator defined in Definition

.
We will also discuss briefly the “higher information” contained in
\Xi^{\vee}
. The most
useful form is to map the transformation of
L_{(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee}}(\Xi^{\vee})
as follows:

5.18.

Recall (Corollary
5.16
) that the two morphisms of cooperads

\textstyle{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\id}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{EZ}\circ\mathfrak{AW}}

on the right).
This is mapped via (

) to a coherent transformation

(Proposition

, 1.) to change the enrichment to

which is
a weak enrichment. The transport works because the trivial enrichment is chosen on the source.

We ignore units, that is, consider the cooperads as cooperads over
\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\circ,\op}
forgetting the structure involving the counit.
The components of this coherent tranformation are thus determined in dimension
[k]
by a map which sends an element

\textstyle{[k+1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[k+1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{i_{0}+1}}
\textstyle{[k]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{i_{k-1}+1}}
\textstyle{[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[1]}

in
N([k+1]\times_{/\Delta_{\act}^{\circ,\op}}\Delta_{\act}^{\circ,\op}\times_{/\Delta_{\act}^{\circ,\op}}[1])_{[k]}

— which we identify with the vector
