which corresponds (via

5.19. (Higher Shih operators)

For a sequence
b_{0}>b_{1}>\cdots>b_{k-1}\subset\{0,\dots,n-1\}
, and
i_{0},\dots,i_{k-1}
, with
0\leq i_{j}\leq k-j-1
, we would like to iterate the morphism

H^{n}_{(b),(0)}:=(\id_{[b]}\ast\mathfrak{Ez}\,\mathfrak{Aw})s_{b}
.
Define
46
46

46

we have the factorization
\Delta^{\op}\to(\Delta^{\op})^{k-1}\to(\Delta^{\op})^{k}
where the second map doubles the
i_{0}
-th entry.
This gives a transport

(-\circ\delta_{i_{0}}^{*}):\Hom(\delta_{k-1}^{*},\delta_{k-1}^{*})\to\Hom(\delta_{k}^{*},\delta_{k}^{*}).

inductively:

\displaystyle H^{n}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}:

\displaystyle(\delta_{k}^{*}X)_{[n-k]}\to(\delta_{k}^{*}X)_{[n]}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(\underbrace{1}_{i_{0}\text{ times}},(\id_{[b_{0}]}\ast\mathfrak{Ez}\,\mathfrak{Aw}),\underbrace{1}_{k-i_{0}-1\text{ times}})s_{b_{0}}(-\circ\delta_{i_{0}}^{*})H^{n-1}_{\underline{b}^{\prime},\underline{i}^{\prime}}

(65)

5.20.

Each such sequence
\underline{b}=(b_{0},\dots,b_{k-1})
determines a shuffle
\sigma_{\underline{b}}:[k]\to[n]
,
\tau_{\underline{b}}:[n-k]\to[n]

Proposition 5.21
.

We have

\boxed{(\mathfrak{Ez}^{*}H)_{\underline{i}}^{n}=\sum_{\underline{b}}\sgn(\sigma_{\underline{b}})H_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}.}

Proof.

This follows from the construction of
\exp
(cf. Proposition

) and from the fact that
\mathfrak{Ez}
is the classical Eilenberg-Zilber map defined in terms of shuffles (Proposition

).
∎

and relate them to the classical Szczarba operators.

5.4
The different simplicial (weak) enrichments on complexes

Let
(\mathcal{M},\otimes)
be a symmetric monoidal category. Recall the definition of
operad enriched in
(\mathcal{M},\otimes)
.
Each enriched operad
\mathcal{C}
has an underlying usual operad with
\Hom
-sets being
\Hom(1,\underline{\Hom}(X,Y))
for
Y\in\mathcal{C}_{[1]}
and it induces a functor

\underline{\Hom}:(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}\to(\mathcal{M},\otimes)

(for the definition of
(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}
, see Definition
4.72
). An enriched operad such that the underlying operad comes from
a monoidal category is not necessarily cofibered as enriched category over
\mathcal{O}
. This is the case if and only if it is classified by a functor of operads

\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}\to(\SCat,\times)

in which case the monoidal product has the structure of simplicially enriched functor.

Definition 5.22
.

We say that an operad
\mathcal{C}
is
weakly enriched
over
(\mathcal{M},\otimes)
, if there is a functor of operads

\underline{\Hom}:(\mathcal{C}^{\op}\times\mathcal{C})_{/\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}\to(\mathcal{M},\otimes)

such that the composition with
\Hom(1,-)
gives back the usual operadic
\Hom
(
43
).

Equivalently, it may be seen as a collection of
\underline{\Hom}(X,Y)
for pairs of objects with
Y\in\mathcal{C}_{[1]}
, but where composition is only defined partially with
“constant morphisms” for
Z\in\mathcal{C}_{[1]}
:

\displaystyle\Hom(1,\underline{\Hom}(X_{1},Y_{1}))\times\cdots\times\Hom(1,\underline{\Hom}(X_{n},Y_{n}))\times\underline{\Hom}(Y,Z)

\displaystyle\to\underline{\Hom}(X,Z)

(66)

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}(X_{1},Y_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\underline{\Hom}(X_{n},Y_{n})\times\Hom(1,\underline{\Hom}(Y,Z))

\displaystyle\to\underline{\Hom}(X,Z)

(67)

Notice that, for a weak enrichment on a
category

\mathcal{C}
, the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{M}
is irrelevant (except for the unit).
However, this
is not true for weak enrichments of
operads
, in (
67
)
\otimes
is the tensor product w.r.t. which the weak enrichment is defined.
Similarly, there is the notion of
weakly enriched functor
.

5.23.

For a morphism of operads
G:(\mathcal{M},\otimes)\to(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\otimes^{\prime})
(i.e. a lax monoidal functor) and an operad
\mathcal{C}
(weakly)
(\mathcal{M},\otimes)
-enriched
there is a (weak)
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\otimes^{\prime})
-enrichment given by

G\underline{\Hom}(X,Y).

For a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of cooperads (weakly) enriched over
(\mathcal{M},\otimes)
there is an induced functor
GF
of
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\otimes^{\prime})
-enriched (co)operads.

5.24.

Everything has a dual counterpart for cooperads. For example a weak enrichment on a cooperad
\mathcal{C}
is a functor of
operads
:

\underline{\Hom}:(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{\op})_{/\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}\to(\mathcal{M},\otimes).

Equivalently, it may be seen as a collection of
\underline{\Hom}(X,Y)
for pairs of objects with
X\in\mathcal{C}_{[1]}
, but where composition is only defined partially with
“constant morphisms”:

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}(X,Y)\times\Hom(1,\underline{\Hom}(Y_{1},Z_{1}))\times\cdots\times\Hom(1,\underline{\Hom}(Y_{n},Z_{n}))

\displaystyle\to\underline{\Hom}(X,Z)

\displaystyle\Hom(1,\underline{\Hom}(X,Y))\times\underline{\Hom}(Y_{1},Z_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\underline{\Hom}(Y_{n},Z_{n})

\displaystyle\to\underline{\Hom}(X,Z)

5.25.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)
be an Abelian tensor category. Recall the Day convolutions

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}

\displaystyle=D((\Delta,\coprod)^{\op},(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee})

(68)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}

\displaystyle=D((\Delta,\ast)^{\op},(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee})

(69)

(i.e.
\otimes:=\delta^{*}-\boxtimes-
is the point-wise product of simplicial objects, and
\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}:=\dec_{*}-\boxtimes-
, where
\dec_{*}\cong\tot
, is the usual tensor product of complexes).
We will define natural (weak)
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)
-enrichments, as well as
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})
-enrichments on these cooperads:

Definition 5.26
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)
be an Abelian tensor category.

For objects

and

\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}}
whose
n
-simplices are given by

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}^{\otimes}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}}(X,Y)_{[n]}

\displaystyle=\Hom(\Delta_{n}\otimes X,Y_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes Y_{m})

(70)

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}}(X,Y)_{[n]}

\displaystyle=\Hom(\Delta_{n}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X,Y_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes Y_{m})

(71)

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}}(X,Y)_{[n]}

\displaystyle=\Hom(\Delta_{n}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X,Y_{1}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\cdots\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Y_{m})

(72)

We define a composition (in

) given
47
47

for simplicity, for
1
-ary compositions on the left, the others are defined completely analogously

where in (

There is an operad version of these enrichement as well, defined in the same way.
Notice that for

.

Remark 5.28
.

In case of the enrichment
\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}}
, the Hom-objects identify via Dold-Kan with the usual Hom-complexes identifying
an element

f:\Delta_{n}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X\to Y_{1}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\cdots\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Y_{k}
with its restriction

\widetilde{f}:\{0,\dots,n\}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X\to Y_{1}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\cdots\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Y_{k}

(76)

which is a degree
n
morphism of complexes. We have (by definition) a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Hom(\Delta_{n}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dd_{l}^{*}=\sum(-1)^{i}\circ(\delta_{i}\otimes\id)^{*}}
\textstyle{\Hom(\Delta_{n-1}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(X,Y)_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(X,Y)_{n-1}}

where
\partial
is the differential in the Hom-complex, and also

\boxed{ \partial\widetilde{f}=\mathrm{d}\circ\widetilde{f}-(-1)^{n}\widetilde{f}\circ\mathrm{d}.}

(77)

which follows from the fact that (
76
) is a morphism of complexes itself, i.e.

f\dd_{l}+(-1)^{n}f\dd_{r}=\dd f
which translates into (
77
).

Lemma 5.29

(Yoneda product)
.

The composition (
75
)
defined for
\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}}
(resp.
\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})}
) factors through
\Aw
in the sense that

\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(B,C)\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}}
\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(A,C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(B,C)\otimes\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Aw}
\scriptstyle{(\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}})^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(A,C)}

is commutative, where

1.

for cooperads:

\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}:\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(Y_{1},Z_{1})\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\cdots\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(Y_{m},Z_{m})\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(X,Y)\to\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(X,Z)

is a morphism of complexes defined as follows (for
m=2
for simplicity). A pure tensor in complex degree
n
on the left can be given as
i+j+k=n
and
f:Y_{1}\to Z_{1}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}
of degree
i
,
g:Y_{2}\to Z_{2}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}
of degree
j
,
h:X\to Y_{1}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Y_{2}
of degree
k
. Then the composition in
\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(X,Z)_{n}
is given by

(f\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}g)\circ h

2.

for operads:

\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}:\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(Y;Z)\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(X_{1},Y_{1})\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\cdots\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(X_{m},Y_{m})\to\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(X,Z)

is a morphism of complexes defined as follows (for
m=2
for simplicity). A pure tensor in complex degree
n
on the left can be given as
i+j+k=n
and
h:Y_{1}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Y_{2}\to Z
of degree
i
,
f:X_{1}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\to Y_{1}
of degree
j
,
g:X_{2}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\to Y_{2}
of degree
k
. Then the composition in
\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(X,Z)_{n}
is given by

h\circ(f\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}g)

where in both cases
(f\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}g)
is defined by (Koszul convention, cf. also
6.13
below):

(f\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}g)(x\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}y):=(-1)^{\deg(g)\deg(x)}f(x)\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}g(y).

Proof.

The reason is that we
defined
the composition (
75
) using the Alexander-Whitney diagonal. Details are left to the reader.
∎

From the Lemma follows that also

\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(B,C)\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Ez}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}}
\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(A,C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(B,C)\otimes\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}})^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(A,C)}

is commutative or, in other words,
\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}=\Ez^{*}(\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}})^{\prime}
.

5.30.

By transport via
F,\Aw,\Ez
we thus get thus six enrichments

\begin{array}[]{l|lll}\hbox{\diagbox[]{{\shortstack[l]{\text{cooperad}}}}{{\shortstack[r]{\text{enriched in}}}}}&(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)&(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)&(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})\\
\hline\cr(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}&F\underline{\Hom}^{\otimes}&\underline{\Hom}^{\otimes}&\Ez^{*}\underline{\Hom}^{\otimes}\\
(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}&F\Aw^{*}\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}&\Aw^{*}\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}&\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\end{array}

and three weak enrichments

\begin{array}[]{l|lll}\hbox{\diagbox[]{{\shortstack[l]{\text{cooperad}}}}{{\shortstack[r]{\text{weakly enriched in}}}}}&(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)&(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)&(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})\\
\hline\cr(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}&F\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}&\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}&\Ez^{*}\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\end{array}

where
F:(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)\to(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)
is the forgetful functor, which is lax monoidal:

F(A)\times F(B)\to F(A\otimes B).

Proposition 5.31
.

1.

There is a functor of weakly
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)
-enriched cooperads, which is the identity on objects and underlying morphism-sets:

\mathfrak{Ez}^{*}:\underline{\Hom}^{\otimes}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}}(X,Y)\to\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}}(X,Y).

(78)

2.

There is a functor of weakly
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)
-enriched cooperads, which is the identity on objects and induces the
\mathfrak{Aw}
-morphism on underlying morphism-sets.

\mathfrak{Aw}:\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}}(X,Y)\to\Aw^{*}\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}}(X,Y)

(79)

3.

\Ez^{*}
applied to the composition is a functor of
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})
-enriched cooperads

\Ez^{*}(\mathfrak{Aw}):\Ez^{*}\underline{\Hom}^{\otimes}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}}(X,Y)\to\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}}(X,Y)

(but the composition
\underline{\Hom}^{\otimes}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}}(X,Y)\to\Aw^{*}\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}}(X,Y)
is only a weakly
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)
-enriched functor)

Proof.

1. Morphism (
78
) is given by mapping
\Delta_{n}\otimes X\to Y_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes Y_{n}
to the pre-composition

\Delta_{n}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X\to\Delta_{n}\otimes X\to Y_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes Y_{n}

with the Eilenberg-Zilber morphism. The only non-trivial case to see that this a morphism of weak enrichments is the composition on the left with a 2-ary morphism in
\Hom(X;Y,Z)
, i.e. to show that the following diagram

\textstyle{X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\Delta_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{Ez}}
\textstyle{X\otimes\Delta_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(Y\otimes Z)\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}(\Delta_{n}\otimes\Delta_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{Ez}}
\scriptstyle{\switch}
\textstyle{Y\otimes Z\otimes\Delta_{n}\otimes\Delta_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{(Y\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\Delta_{n})\otimes(Z\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\Delta_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{Ez}\otimes\mathfrak{Ez}}
\textstyle{Y\otimes\Delta_{n}\otimes Z\otimes\Delta_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\otimes Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\otimes Z^{\prime}}

is commutative. The commutativity of the middle square is Lemma
5.34
below.

2. Morphism (
79
) is given by mapping
\Delta_{n}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X\to Y_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes Y_{n}

to the composition

\Delta_{n}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X\to Y_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes Y_{n}\to Y_{1}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\cdots\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Y_{n}

with the Alexander-Whitney morphism.
The only non-trivial case to see that this a morphism of weak enrichments is to show that the following diagram

\textstyle{X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\Delta_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\Delta_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(Y\otimes Z)\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}(\Delta_{n}\otimes\Delta_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{Aw}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\mathfrak{Aw}}
\scriptstyle{\switch}
\textstyle{Y\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Z\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\Delta_{n}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\Delta_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\sigma}}
\textstyle{(Y\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\Delta_{n})\otimes(Z\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\Delta_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{Aw}}
\textstyle{Y\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\Delta_{n}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Z\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\Delta_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\otimes Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{Aw}}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Z^{\prime}}

is commutative. The commutativity of the middle square is Lemma
5.35
below.

3. is left to the reader.
∎

Lemma 5.34
.

The following is commutative:

\textstyle{\dec_{*}\delta_{12,34}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\delta^{*}\delta_{12,34}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\delta^{*}\delta_{12,34}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

B
\textstyle{\delta^{*}\delta_{13,24}^{*}}

Here all maps denoted by = are the canonical identifications. It suffices to see that A and B are commutative. Actually these are the same diagram.
We have to see that

\textstyle{\delta_{12,34}\delta\dec\delta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{u_{13,24}\delta_{13,24}\delta}
\textstyle{\delta_{12,34}\delta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\delta_{13,24}\delta}

commutes as diagram of functors
\Delta\to\Delta^{4}
(no FinSet symmetry is involved here).
This is checked straightforwardly.
∎

Definition 5.36
.

For later applications we will need truncated versions of the (weak) simplicial enrichments discussed so far:
We define truncated (weak)
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)
-enrichments for the cooperads (in which the counits have been discarded):

(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\circ,\vee}\text{ and }(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\circ,\vee}

setting:

\displaystyle(\underline{\Hom}^{\otimes,t}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\circ,\vee}})_{k}(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}(\underline{\Hom}^{\otimes}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\circ,\vee}})_{k}(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})&k<n\\
0&\text{otherwise }\end{cases}

\displaystyle(\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}},t}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\circ,\vee}})_{k}(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}(\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\circ,\vee}})_{k}(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})&k<n\\
0&\text{otherwise }\end{cases}

with composition defined as for the untruncated case
and a truncated
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})
-enrichment for the cooperad
(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\circ,\vee}
(in which the counits have been discarded):

\displaystyle(\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}},t}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\circ,\vee}})_{k}(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}(\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\circ,\vee}})_{k}(X;Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})&k<n\\
0&\text{otherwise }\end{cases}

with composition defined as for the untruncated case.
Note that this works only because we have discarded the counits (no map in
\Delta^{\circ,\op}
decreases the arity).

Lemma 5.37
.

1.

\underline{\Hom}^{\otimes,t}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\circ,\vee}}
defines a
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)
-enrichment;

2.

\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}},t}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\circ,\vee}}
defines a weak
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)
-enrichment;

3.

\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}},t}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\circ,\vee}}
defines a
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})
-enrichment.

Proof.

This follows from the fact (cf. Proposition
5.39
) that if
X
and
Y
are such that
Y_{i}=0
for
i>n
and
Y_{i}=0
for
i>m
then
(X\otimes Y)_{i}=0
and
(X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Y)_{i}=0
for
i>n+m
.
∎

5.5
Explicit formulæ

be an Abelian category.

) with

(F^{k}X)_{[n]}=\sum_{\sigma:[d]\twoheadleftarrow[n]}\sigma(X_{[d]})

. It follows from the dual assertion (

) that, considering

as a complex, this coincides with the truncation:

F^{k}X=\{\cdots\to 0\to X_{k}\to X_{k-1}\to\cdots\to X_{0}\}.

For the natural two-dimensional filtration
F^{i,j}\delta^{*}X:=\delta^{*}F^{i,j}X
(cf.
5.38
) we have

(\gr^{i,j}\delta^{*}X)_{n}\cong\bigoplus_{\sigma,\tau}X_{i,j}

where
\sigma:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[i],\tau:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[j]
runs through the jointly injective pairs of surjections.
In particular:
(F^{i,j}\delta^{*}X)_{n}=(\delta^{*}X)_{n}
for
i\geq n
and
j\geq n
and
(F^{i,j}\delta^{*}X)_{n}=0
for
i+j<n
and

(F^{i,n-i}\delta^{*}X)_{n}\cong\bigoplus_{\sigma,\tau}X_{i,n-i}\ \qquad(\gr^{n,n}\delta^{*}X)_{n}\cong X_{n,n}.

In this case (
i+j=n
) the jointly injective pairs of surjections
\sigma:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[i],\tau:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[j]
are called
i,j
-
shuffles
.
The isomorphisms are determined by requiring that

\textstyle{(\delta^{*}X)_{[n]}=X_{[n],[n]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma,\tau}
\textstyle{X_{[i],[j]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\delta^{*}X)_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{i,j}}

commutes modulo
F^{i,j-1}(\delta^{*}X)_{n}+F^{i-1,j}(\delta^{*}X)_{n}
(which is zero if
i+j=n
), where the vertical morphisms are the projections onto non-degenerate elements.

We start by discussing the translation of the functors

\displaystyle\dec^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}

\displaystyle\delta^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}

via Dold-Kan. We have

\displaystyle(\dec^{*}C)_{m,n}

\displaystyle=\Hom(D_{m,n},\dec^{*}C)=\Hom(\dec_{!}D_{m,n},C)

\displaystyle(\delta^{*}C)_{m}

\displaystyle=\Hom(D_{m},\delta^{*}C)=\Hom(\delta_{!}D_{m},C)

Furthermore, by Yoneda and the commuation of

with colimits:

\displaystyle\delta_{!}\Delta_{m}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{m,m}^{\circ}

\displaystyle\dec_{!}\Delta_{m,n}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{m+n+1}^{\circ}

\displaystyle\delta_{!}D_{m}

\displaystyle=\ker(\Delta_{m,m}^{\circ}\to\Delta_{m^{\prime},m^{\prime}}^{\circ})

\displaystyle\dec_{!}D_{m,n}

\displaystyle=\ker(\Delta_{m+n+1}^{\circ}\to\Delta_{m^{\prime}+n^{\prime}+1}^{\circ})

[n]\twoheadrightarrow[n^{\prime}]
, resp.

. The filtrations on

and

\displaystyle=\ker(F^{k,l}\Delta_{m,m}^{\circ}\to F^{k,l}\Delta_{m^{\prime},m^{\prime}}^{\circ})

\displaystyle F^{k}\dec_{!}D_{m,n}

\displaystyle=\ker(F^{k}\Delta_{m+n+1}^{\circ}\to F^{k}\Delta_{m^{\prime}+n^{\prime}+1}^{\circ})

We get

\displaystyle\gr\delta_{!}D_{m}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{[m]\twoheadrightarrow[k],[m]\twoheadrightarrow[l]\}\\
\text{that do not factor over $\im(\delta^{*})$ }\end{subarray}}D_{k,l}

\displaystyle\gr\dec_{!}D_{m,n}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{[m+n+1|\twoheadrightarrow[k]\}\\

identifying the maximum of

[n]
.

5.40.

For surjections
\sigma:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[i]
and
\tau:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[j]
that are jointly injective, and such that
i+j=n
, i.e. in the case of
i,j
-shuffles,

\sigma
and
\tau
determine each other, because we can index degeneracies by non-empty subsets
I,J\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
(the intervals contracted by
\tau
, and
\sigma
, respectively) and under the two conditions,
\sigma
and
\tau
must correspond to complementary sets.
In other words

\sigma=s_{\sigma_{j}}\cdots s_{\sigma_{1}}\qquad\tau=s_{\tau_{i}}\cdots s_{\tau_{1}}

for
J=\{\sigma_{1}+1,\dots,\sigma_{j}+1\}
, and
I=\{\tau_{1}+1,\dots,\tau_{i}+1\}
, respectively.

In particular, we get for
i,j
with
i+j=n
surjections

p_{i,j}:\delta_{!}D_{n}\to D_{i}\boxtimes D_{j}

induced by
s^{l}:[m]\twoheadrightarrow[i],s^{r}:[m]\twoheadrightarrow[j]
the extremal degeneracies corresponding to the subsets
\{i+1,\dots,n\}
and
\{1,\dots,i\}
, respectively.

Lemma 5.41
.

There are splittings

s_{i,j}:D_{i}\boxtimes D_{j}\to\delta_{!}D_{n}

(of the maps
p_{i,j}
) induced by
\delta_{l,i}\times\delta_{r,j}:[{i}]\times[j]\to[{n}]\times[{n}]
, the extremal faces.
Every other map

\delta_{!}D_{n}\to D_{i^{\prime}}\boxtimes D_{j^{\prime}}

given by pairs
\sigma:[{m}]\twoheadrightarrow[{i^{\prime}}]
and
\tau:[{m}]\twoheadrightarrow[{j^{\prime}}]
that are jointly injective and such that
l+k=m
, composes to zero with
s_{i,j}
.

Proof.

\delta_{l,i}\times\delta_{r,j}
composed with a diagonal degeneracy factors through a degeneracy of either
[i]
or
[j]
, hence there is an induced map as indicated.
The composition with any pair of surjective maps
[n]\times[n]\to[k]\times[l]
, jointly injective, corresponding to subsets
S
and
T
, is a degeneracy unless
S\subseteq\{1,\dots,i\}
and

T\subseteq\{i+1,\dots,n\}
. If
\#S+\#T=n
we must have equality.
∎

Proof.

\scriptstyle{\mathrm{counit}}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{\delta_{!}\dec_{!}(\Delta_{i}^{\circ}\boxtimes\Delta_{j}^{\circ})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{\can}}

\delta_{!}
of the canonical degeneracy, seen as a morphism

.
Here every other shape clearly commutes. For the upper triangle notice that this is the image under

\scriptstyle{u^{\op}=\delta_{l,i}\boxtimes\delta_{r,j}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{i}\boxtimes\Delta_{j}.}

2. It suffices to see that the following commutes:

\textstyle{\delta_{!}(D_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{\sigma,\tau}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma,\tau)\cdot(\sigma,\tau)}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{Ez}}

\scriptstyle{s_{\can}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\dec^{*}(D_{n})}

First, observe that the morphism
\sigma,\tau:\Delta_{n}^{\circ}\boxtimes\Delta_{n}^{\circ}\to\Delta_{i}^{\circ}\boxtimes\Delta_{j}^{\circ}
(
i+j=n
) really induces a map

\delta_{!}(D_{n})\to D_{i}\boxtimes D_{j}

because for every degeneracy

(for

, and where either

or

) there is a degeneracy

such that
we have a factorization

\textstyle{[n]\times[n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[i]\times[j]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[{n^{\prime}}]\times[{n^{\prime}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[{i^{\prime}}]\times[{j^{\prime}}]}

First observe that

\delta_{!}(D_{n})\subset F^{n-1}\Delta_{n}^{\circ}\boxtimes\Delta_{n}^{\circ}

because every pair of degeneracies

and

with

must factor through a diagonal degeneracy.

Since the filtration by codegeneracy is the same as the canonical complex filtration (cf.
5.10
), it suffices to show therefore that

\textstyle{\Delta_{n}^{\circ}\boxtimes\Delta_{n}^{\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{\sigma,\tau}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma,\tau)\cdot(\sigma,\tau)}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{Ez}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i+j=n}\Delta_{i}^{\circ}\boxtimes\Delta_{j}^{\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{\can}}
\textstyle{\dec^{*}(\Delta_{n}^{\circ})}

commutes in double complex degree
\geq n
51
51

51

Contrary to the situation with the Alexander-Whitney map it is not commutative in general!
.
Since
(\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{i+j+1}=0
for
i+j=n
we actually have

(\dec^{*}\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{i,j}\cong(\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{i+j}

(82)

for

and 0 for

, and thus we are left to show commutativity in complex degree

with

.

The map (
82
) may be described as
c\circ\dd_{r}
(see the proof of Proposition

, 2.) where

is the morphism fitting into

\textstyle{(\dec^{*}\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{[i],[j-1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\dec^{*}\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{i,j-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{(\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{[i+j]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{i+j}}

Thus we have to see that the composition

c\circ\mathrm{EZ}\circ\dd_{r}:(\delta_{!}\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{i,j}\to(\delta_{!}\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{i,j-1}\to(\dec^{*}\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{i,j-1}\to(\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{i+j}.

(83)

is equal to the composition

\displaystyle c\circ s_{\can}\circ\dd_{r}\circ(\sum_{\sigma,\tau}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma,\tau)\cdot(\sigma,\tau)):

(84)

\displaystyle(\delta_{!}\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{i,j}\to(\Delta_{i}^{\circ})_{i}\times(\Delta_{j}^{\circ})_{j}\to(\Delta_{i}^{\circ})_{i}\times(\Delta_{j}^{\circ})_{j-1}\to(\dec^{*}\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{i,j-1}\to(\Delta_{n}^{\circ})_{i+j}.

The composition of the last two morphisms in (
83
) is by definition (cf. Lemma
5.6
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\text{subsets of $\{0,\dots,n\}$}\\
\text{of cardinality $i+1$ and $j$}\end{subarray}\right]

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\text{subsets of $\{0,\dots,n\}$}\\
\text{ of cardinality $i+j+1$}\end{subarray}\right]

\displaystyle{[S]\otimes[T]}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\pm[S\cup T]&\text{if $S\cap T=\emptyset$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where the sign is determined by the parity of the permutation bringing
S\ast T
into the order of
S\cup T
.
Given subsets
I,J\subset\{0,\dots,n\}
of cardinality
i+1
and
j+1
with
I\cap J=\{x\}
,

c\circ\mathrm{EZ}\circ\dd_{r}
maps
[I]\otimes[J]
thus to
[\{0,\dots,n\}]
with sign
(-1)^{k-1}\mathrm{sgn}(\kappa)
,
where
\kappa
is the permutation that brings
I\coprod(J\setminus\{x\})
into the correct order, and
x
is the
k
-th element of
J
.
It maps subsets with larger intersection to zero.

The composition of the last three morphisms in (
84
) maps the generator
[\{0,\dots,i\}]\otimes[\{0,\dots,j\}]
to the generator
[\{0,\dots,n\}]
. By Lemma

below, the first morphism maps
a pair
I
,
J
either to zero, or, if
I\cap J=\{x\}
, there is exactly one shuffle that maps it to

with the same sign

and all others map it to zero.
∎

Lemma 5.43
.

Let
I,J\subset\{0,\dots,n\}
be two subsets of Cardinality
i+1
and
j+1
with
i+j=n
.

1.

If
I\cap J=\{x\}
there
is exactly one
i,j
-shuffle
\sigma,\tau
such that
\sigma([I])=[\{0,\dots,i\}]
and
\tau([J])=[\{0,\dots,j\}]
. (All other shuffles satisfy either
\sigma([I])=0
or
\tau([J])=0
.)
Then

\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma,\tau)=(-1)^{k-1}\mathrm{sgn}(\kappa),

where
\kappa
is the permutation that brings
I\coprod(J\setminus\{x\})
into the correct order, and
x
is the
k
-th element of
J
.

2.

If
\{x,y\}\subset I\cap J
then

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma,\tau\\
\sigma([I])=[\{0,\dots,i\}]\\
\tau([J])=[\{0,\dots,j\}]\end{subarray}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma,\tau)=0.

Proof.

(i) In this case, the subsets are covering.
If
x\not=0
, we have
x-1\in I
and
x-1\not\in J
or vice versa. Thus
\sigma
must not contract
x-1<x
and hence
\tau
must do so (or vice versa). By induction to the left,
\sigma
and
\tau
are determined for
i\leq x
.
If
x\not=n
, we have
x+1\in I
and
x+1\not\in J
or vice versa. Thus
\sigma
must not contract
x<x+1
and hence
\tau
must do so (or vice versa). By induction to the right,
\sigma
and
\tau
are determined for
i\geq x
.
The equality of the sign expressions follows from the definition of the sign of a shuffle
52
52

52

The shuffle corresponds to complementary subsets
I^{\prime}
(contracting intervals for
\tau
) and
J^{\prime}
(contracting intervals for
\sigma
) of
\{1,\dots,n\}
and the sign is determined by the permutation
\kappa^{\prime}
that
brings
I^{\prime}\cup J^{\prime}
into the correct order. The procedure just described determines order-preserving isomorphisms
I^{\prime}\cong I\setminus\{x\}
and
J^{\prime}\cong J\setminus\{x\}
in such a way that the transposed permutation brings
I\setminus\{x\}\cup J\setminus\{x\}
into the correct order. If
x
was the
l
-th element of
I
and
k
-th element of
J
, it has to move from position
l
to
l+k-1
, hence an additional sign of
(-1)^{k-1}
.
.

(ii) In this case, there is an element
z\in\{0,\dots,n\}\setminus(I\cup J)
.
The shuffles correspond to complementary subsets
I^{\prime},J^{\prime}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
of cardinality
i
and
j
(the contracting intervals of
\tau
and
\sigma
respectively).
The shuffles satisfying the property from the sum induce jointly injective surjections
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma\delta_{z}:[{n-1}]\twoheadrightarrow[{i}]
and
\tau^{\prime}=\tau\delta_{z}:[{n-1}]\twoheadrightarrow[{j}]
. As such they correspond to subsets
I^{\prime\prime},J^{\prime\prime}\subset\{1,\dots,n-1\}
of cardinality
i-1
and
j-1
with
I^{\prime\prime}\cap J^{\prime\prime}=\emptyset
.

The interval
q
of
\Delta_{n-1}
which is neither in
I^{\prime\prime}
nor
J^{\prime\prime}
thus must correspond to a new interval that is not mapped to an interval by
\delta_{z}
(in particular,
z\not=0
and
z\not=n
) because all others
are
contracted by one of the compositions. To each of these pairs
I^{\prime\prime}
,
J^{\prime\prime}
thus correspond precisely two pairs
I^{\prime}
and
J^{\prime}
where the two intervals that
\delta_{z}(q)
is composed of, are distributed evenly into
I^{\prime}
and
J^{\prime}
.
The corresponding shuffles have opposite sign.
∎

5.47.

Before discussing the proof, recall the explicit description of homotopies obtained via
\dec^{*}
from
4.28
.
A homotopy in the classical sense is extracted by the element

\{0,1\}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X\to\Delta_{1}^{\circ}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X\to\Delta_{1}^{\circ}\otimes X

where the second morphism is the EZ morphism. In degree
n
the corresponding map

is given, with the notation of

, by (sum over

-shuffles)

\{0,1\}\otimes x_{n-1}\mapsto\alpha(x_{n-1}):=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}G_{[n],i}(s_{i}x_{n-1})=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}F_{[i],[n-i-1]}(s_{i}x_{n-1})

where
x_{n-1}\in X_{[n-1]}
denotes a representing element.

Proof of Proposition
5.46
.

5.42
and the Definition
5.14
, we have for
r+s+1=n

\displaystyle L_{\mathcal{C}}(\Xi)_{[r],[s]}:(\dec^{*}\delta^{*}X)_{[r],[s]}=X_{[r]\ast[s],[r]\ast[s]}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle X_{[r]\ast[s],[r]\ast[s]}=(\dec^{*}\delta^{*}X)

\displaystyle x_{n}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p+q=s\\
\sigma,\tau\end{subarray}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma,\tau)(\sigma d_{l},\tau d_{r})x_{n}

where
\sigma,\tau,\delta_{l},\delta_{r}
are intended w.r.t. the

-variable and the
\sigma,\tau
run through all

-shuffles.

In view of the discussion in

this translates to

) in the bisimplicial object

\displaystyle\{0,1\}\otimes x_{n-1}\mapsto\sum_{b=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{b}L_{\mathcal{C}}(\Xi_{1})_{[b],[n-b-1]}(s_{b}x_{n-1})

5.48.

The formula for
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
can be given in a different form:
The summands of
\mathcal{Q}^{n}
run over
p,q
-shuffles with
p+q=n
.
Those are determined by subsets
Z\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
giving the intervals where
\sigma
is degenerate and thus
\tau
is not.
Equivalently, they are given by sequences
\underline{y}=(z_{0},\dots,z_{n-1})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and we have

\sigma=s_{0}^{z_{0}}\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}s_{0}^{z_{n-1}}

where
s_{0}^{0}=\id_{[1]}
and
s_{0}^{1}:[1]\twoheadrightarrow[0]
is the usual degeneracy.
From this we get

\sgn(\sigma)=(-1)^{\sum_{i<j}z_{j}(z_{i}+1)}

which may be derived from the formula

\sgn(\sigma_{0}\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}\sigma_{n-1})=\prod_{i}\sgn(\sigma_{i})\prod_{i<j}(-1)^{|\sigma_{j}|\cdot|\overline{\sigma_{i}}|}

(85)

where
\sigma_{i}:[n_{i}]\to[|\sigma_{i}|]
are degeneracies and
\overline{\sigma}
is the complementary shuffle.
We also have for
\underline{b}=(b_{0},\dots,b_{k-1})\subset\{0,\dots,n-1\}
, with
b_{0}>b_{1}>\cdots>b_{k-1}
the formula

\sgn(\sigma_{\underline{b}})=(-1)^{\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}b_{i}-(k-1-i)}\qquad\sgn(\tau_{\underline{b}})=(-1)^{\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}n-k-i-b_{i}}

(86)

where
\sigma_{\underline{b}},\tau_{\underline{b}}
is the associated shuffle (
5.20
).

Lemma 5.49
.

Let
n
be a positive integer. Consider the set of
\underline{y}=(y_{0},\dots,y_{n-1})
with
y_{i}\in\{0,1\}
.
We have

\mathcal{Q}^{n}=\sum_{\underline{y}}(-1)^{\sum_{i<j}y_{j}(y_{i}+1)}(B_{\underline{y}}^{0}\otimes B_{\underline{y}}^{1})

(87)

with
B_{\underline{y}}^{0}:[n+1]\to[m]
for some
m
, and
B_{\underline{y}}^{1}:[n+1]\to[n+1]
recursively defined by:

B_{()}^{0}=B_{()}^{1}=\id_{[0]}
and

B_{\underline{y}}^{j}=\begin{cases}s_{n-1}B_{\underline{y}^{\prime}}^{j}&j<y_{0}\\
B_{\underline{y}^{\prime}}^{j}\ast\id_{[0]}&j=y_{0}\\
s_{n-1}B_{\underline{y}^{\prime}}^{j}\delta_{0}&j>y_{0}\end{cases}

(88)

Each summand is degenerate on the left at
i
, if
y_{i}=0
and degenerate on the right at
i
, if
y_{i}=1
.
Implicitly in the formula (
87
) the
B_{\underline{y}}^{0}
are composed with the inclusion
[m]\hookrightarrow[n+1]
.

Proof.

An instructive exercise.
∎

5.50.

We would like an explicit formula also for the higher Shih operators
5.19
, however, defining
\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}
in the same way as in (
65
) would not be correct because the equality of

H^{n}_{b}
with
\mathcal{H}^{n}_{b}
holds only up to degenerate elements. However we can define (for any
m
):

\mathcal{H}^{n}_{b}:\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([n-1],[m])]\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([{n-1}],[m])]\to\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([n],[m])]\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([n],[m])]

by writing generators as

\tau\otimes\kappa=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}\cdot(\tau^{\prime}\otimes\kappa^{\prime})
with
(\tau^{\prime}\otimes\kappa^{\prime})
non-degenerate and setting:

\mathcal{H}_{b}^{n}(\tau\otimes\kappa):=s_{i_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots s_{i_{k}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathcal{H}_{b^{\prime}}^{n-k^{\prime}}\cdot(\tau^{\prime}\otimes\kappa^{\prime})

where the
i
and
b
have been transformed by the rule:

\mathcal{H}_{b}^{n}s_{i}:=\begin{cases}s_{i}\mathcal{H}_{b-1}^{n-1}&i<b,\\
s_{i+1}\mathcal{H}_{b}^{n-1}&i\geq b.\end{cases}

Defining then
\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}
by the same recursive formula as (
65
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}:

\displaystyle(\delta_{k}^{*}X)_{[n-k]}\to(\delta_{k}^{*}X)_{[n]}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(\underbrace{s_{b_{0}},\dots,s_{b_{0}}}_{i_{0}\text{ times}},\mathcal{H}_{b_{0}}^{n},\underbrace{s_{b_{0}},\dots,s_{b_{0}}}_{k-i_{0}-1\text{ times}})(-\circ\delta_{i_{0}}^{*})\mathcal{H}^{n-1}_{\underline{b}^{\prime},\underline{i}^{\prime}}.

with
\underline{b}^{\prime}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{k-1})
and
\underline{i}^{\prime}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k-1})
,
we arrive at:

Proposition 5.51
.

The following diagram is commutative

\textstyle{(\delta^{*}_{k}X)_{[n-k]}=X_{[n-k],[n-k]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}}
\textstyle{(\delta_{k}^{*}X)_{[n]}=X_{[n],[n]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\delta^{*}_{k}X)_{n-k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H^{n}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}}
\textstyle{(\delta_{k}^{*}X)_{n}}

Notice that, again, the lower map is not a morphism of complexes!

5.52.

There is a relation between the higher Shih operators
H_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}
and the Szczarba operators defined in
[
53
]
(cf. also
[
33
]
) as we will now explain.
The conceptual relation will be clarified by the discussion in Section
6.10
. Cf. also
[
24
]
for a different discussion of this relation.

5.53.

We say that an element in
\Hom([{n}],[m])^{k+1}
, considered as generating summand of
\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([n],[m])]^{\otimes(k+1)}

is
i
-degenerate at
j
, if it is of the form

(\tau_{0},\dots,s_{i}\tau_{j},\dots,\tau_{k}).

Define an idempotent

\mathcal{P}_{i}:\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([{n}],[m])]\to\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([{n}],[m])]

defined on a basis by

\tau\mapsto\begin{cases}0&\text{ if $\tau$ is $i$-degenerate, }\\
\tau&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Define also

\mathcal{P}:\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([{n}],[m])]^{\otimes(k+1)}\to\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([n],[m])]^{\otimes(k+1)}

by

\mathcal{P}=\prod_{i=0}^{k}1^{\otimes i}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{n-k-1+i}\otimes 1^{\otimes k-i}

i.e. it throws away all summands that are
n-k-1+i
degenerate at
i
for
i=0,\dots,k
.

Definition 5.54
.

Define an active morphism
\Sz^{j}_{\underline{i}}:[k]\to[j]
(i.e.
\Sz^{j}_{\underline{i}}(0)=0
and

\Sz^{j}_{\underline{i}}(k)=j
) by
\Sz^{0}_{()}:=\id_{[0]}
, and the recursion

\Sz^{j}_{\underline{i}}:=\begin{cases}(\Sz^{j}_{\underline{i}^{\prime}}\ast\id_{[0]})s_{j}&j<i_{0}+1\\
(\Sz^{j-1}_{\underline{i}^{\prime}}\ast\id_{[0]})&j=i_{0}+1\\
(\Sz^{j-1}_{\underline{i}^{\prime}}\ast\id_{[0]})s_{j-1}\delta_{i_{0}+1}&j>i_{0}+1\end{cases}

(89)

in which
\underline{i}^{\prime}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k-1})
.

One can also write the recursion in a slightly different, equivalent form:

\Sz^{j}_{\underline{i}}:=\begin{cases}s_{k-1}\Sz^{j}_{\underline{i}^{\prime}}&j<i_{0}+1\\
\Sz^{j-1}_{\underline{i}^{\prime}}\ast\id_{[0]}&j=i_{0}+1\\
s_{k-1}\Sz^{j-1}_{\underline{i}^{\prime}}\delta_{i_{0}+1}&j>i_{0}+1\end{cases}

(90)

Remark 5.55
.

This definition is similar to the one given by Szczarba in
[
53
]
used by Hess and Tonks in
[
33
, Definition 5]
. In fact

\Sz^{j}_{\underline{i}}(x)={}^{t}\!D^{k+1}_{k-j,\underline{i}}

where
{}^{t}x
for a map
x:[k]\to[j]
is the conjugation with the reversal of simplices and
D
is the operator defined in [loc. cit.].

5.56.

A sequence

\textstyle{[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{i_{k-1}+1}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[k]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{i_{0}+1}}
\textstyle{[k+1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[k+1]}

of active faces, or written as vector
\underline{i}=(i_{0},\dots,i_{k-1})

can be interpreted as a connected leveled binary tree with
k
vertices of degree 3:
For example

\textstyle{2}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{-\otimes-}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(-\otimes-)\otimes-}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(-\otimes-)\otimes(-\otimes-)}

corresponds to the vector
(2,0,0)
.
For a vector
\underline{b}=(b_{0},\dots,b_{k-1})
with
\widetilde{k}>b_{0}>b_{1}>\cdots>b_{k-1}\geq 0
, we say that
\underline{i}=(i_{0},\dots,i_{k-1})
is a
\underline{b}
-reduction of
\widetilde{\underline{i}}

if for
i\notin\{b_{0},\dots,b_{k-1}\}
the vertex in row
i
has no left children and deleting those vertices (with their corresponding left leaf and their row), we obtain the tree associated with
\underline{i}
.
The remaining leaves form a subsequence
c_{0}<c_{1}<\dots<c_{k}
of
\{0,\dots,\widetilde{k}\}
.
For example the displayed tree has the
(2)
-reduction
(0)
,
(1,2)
-reduction
(0,0)
,
(0,2)
-reduction
(1,0)
, and
(0,1,2)
-reduction
(2,0,0)
, but does not have a
(0,1)
-reduction.

The
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
such that
\underline{i}=(i_{0},\dots,i_{k-1})
is a
\underline{b}
-reduction are in bijection with vectors
(y_{0},\dots,y_{\widetilde{k}-k-1})

with
0\leq y_{0},\dots,y_{\widetilde{k}-2-b_{0}}\leq k
, …,
0\leq y_{\widetilde{k}-k-b_{k-1}},\dots,y_{\widetilde{k}-k-1}\leq 0

Example:

\begin{array}[]{r|l|l}\widetilde{k}-1&0\leq y_{0}\leq k&\\
\widetilde{k}-2&0\leq y_{1}\leq k&\\
\widetilde{k}-3&b_{0}&0\leq i_{0}\leq k-1\\
\widetilde{k}-4&0\leq y_{2}\leq k-1\\
\widetilde{k}-5&b_{1}&0\leq i_{1}\leq k-2\\
\widetilde{k}-6&0\leq y_{3}\leq k-2\\
\widetilde{k}-7&0\leq y_{4}\leq k-2\\
\widetilde{k}-8&b_{2}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
1&b_{k-1}&0\leq i_{k-1}\leq 0\\
0&0\leq y_{\widetilde{k}-k-1}\leq 0\end{array}

The
i_{i}
and
y_{i}
together determine
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
. In each row the
y_{i}
determines the position where a node (with no left children) is added (starting from
i=0
).
With a leveled tree given by
\underline{i}=(i_{0},\dots,i_{k-1})
we associate the sign

\sgn(\underline{i}):=(-1)^{\sum_{j}i_{j}}

(91)

and we define
\underline{i}^{\vee}=(k-1-i_{0},k-2-i_{1},\dots,i_{k-1})
.

Proposition 5.57
.

We have for arbitrary
n
, setting
\widetilde{k}:=n-k-1
:

\mathcal{P}\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}\equiv\begin{cases}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}}\text{ such that }\\
\text{$\underline{i}$ is a $\underline{b}$-reduction of $\widetilde{\underline{i}}$}\end{subarray}}\varepsilon(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}q_{0})\otimes\cdots\otimes(\Sz^{c_{k}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}q_{k})&\underline{b}_{0}<\widetilde{k}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

modulo degenerates, where
q_{j}=s_{j,j+1}^{k+1}
. In particular the expession is zero unless
\widetilde{k}\geq k
(i.e.
n\geq 2k+1
).

Here the
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
are vectors of length
\widetilde{k}
and
c_{0}<\dots<c_{k}
are determined by
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
as in
5.56
, and

\varepsilon=(-1)^{(\widetilde{k}+1)k}\sgn(\underline{i}^{\vee})\sgn(\widetilde{\underline{i}}^{\vee})\sgn(\sigma_{\underline{b}}).

The
\Sz_{\overline{i}}^{j}\ast^{\prime}q_{j}
have values in
[c_{j}]\ast^{\prime}[1]=[c_{j}+1]
and are considered as
[n-k]
-valued via
the inclusion
[c_{j}+1]\subset[n-k]
.

The proof is a bit involved and has been shifted to appendix
B
. Unless the reader is interested in the
combinatorics of the formula, it is not very enlightening.

For the special case
n=2k+1
(least non-zero case) we get a simpler form:

Corollary 5.58
.

We have for
n=2k+1
:

\boxed{\mathcal{P}\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}\equiv\begin{cases}(\Sz^{0}_{\underline{i}}\ast^{\prime}q_{0})\otimes\cdots\otimes(\Sz^{k}_{\underline{i}}\ast^{\prime}q_{k})&\underline{b}=(k-1,k-2,\dots,0)\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}}

modulo degenerates, where
q_{j}=s_{j,j+1}^{k+1}
with the same conventions as in Proposition
5.57
.

Notice that in that case
\widetilde{k}=k
,
\widetilde{\underline{i}}=\underline{i}
, and
\varepsilon=\sgn(\sigma_{\underline{b}})=1
.

5.59.

The formula in Proposition
5.57
has the following important property.
Defining

\mathcal{K}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}:=(-1)^{(\widetilde{k}+1)k}\sgn(\underline{i}^{\vee})\sgn(\sigma_{\underline{b}})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}}\\
\text{$\underline{i}$ is a $\underline{b}$-reduction of $\widetilde{\underline{i}}$}\end{subarray}}\sgn(\widetilde{\underline{i}}^{\vee})(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Sz^{c_{k}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}})

(with the same inclusion
[c_{j}]\subseteq[\widetilde{k}]
, such that this is in
\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([\widetilde{k}],[\widetilde{k}])^{k+1}]
), i.e. the essential part of
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}
and define

\mathcal{K}^{n}_{k}:=\sum_{\underline{i},\underline{b}}\sgn(\underline{i}^{\vee})\sgn(\sigma_{\underline{b}})\mathcal{K}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}

where the sum is over all (correct) vectors of length
n-k-1
and
k
, respectively.

We can then write

\mathcal{K}^{n}_{k}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}}\\
c_{0}<\cdots<c_{k}\end{subarray}\\
}\sgn(\widetilde{\underline{i}}^{\vee})(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Sz^{c_{k}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}})

(92)

where the sum runs over all
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
of length
n-k-1
and
\{c_{0},\dots,c_{k}\}\subset\{0,\dots,n-k-1\}
over subsets of indices of leaves running first through nodes at their left child.
Notice that these determine uniquely a
\underline{b}
consisting of the corresponding row indices and a reduction
\underline{i}
.

Proposition 5.60
.

For each integer
k\geq 0
the following holds true
54
54

54

notice that the summands are zero for
n>k
, and that
\sum_{\underline{i}}\sgn(\underline{i})
is 1 for
k\leq 1
and 0 otherwise. However, constants are degenerate for
k\geq 1
anyway.

(1+\varepsilon)^{\otimes(k+1)}\mathcal{K}^{2k+1}_{k}\equiv\sum_{n}\mathcal{K}^{n+k+1}_{n}

modulo degenerates and constants
or equivalently

\sum_{n}(1-\varepsilon)^{\otimes(n+1)}\mathcal{K}^{n+k+1}_{n}\equiv\mathcal{K}^{2k+1}_{k}

modulo degenerates and constants. Here
\varepsilon:\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([k],[k])]\to\mathbb{Z}
is the augmentation and the calculation takes place in
\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([k],[{k}])]^{\otimes n}
.

Proof.

Follows from (
92
) and the following Lemma
5.61
.
∎

Lemma 5.61
.

Let
\underline{i}=(i_{0},\dots,i_{k-1})
be a vector which we see as a leveled tree as in (
5.56
).

1.

\Sz_{\underline{i}}^{j}
is non-degenerate at
e
if and only if the path from the
j
-th leaf towards the root passes through a right child at
e
.

2.

The expression

\Sz_{\underline{i}}^{c_{0}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Sz_{\underline{i}}^{c_{m}}

for
\{c_{0},\dots,c_{m}\}\subset\{0,\dots,k\}
is non-degenerate if and only if there is a
\underline{b}=(b_{0},\dots,b_{k-m-1})
such that

\underline{i}
has a
\underline{b}
-reduction with associated
\{c_{j}\}
, or in other words, if for each
j\not\in\{c_{0},\dots,c_{m}\}
the path from the
j
-node towards the root passes first through a left child (
\underline{b}
consists then of the corresponding row indices).

Proof.

1. follows immediately from the recursive formula and 2. follows from 1.
∎

6
Basic examples of classical bar and cobar

6.1
Classical (co)bar for
\Set

6.1.

Consider
(\Set,\times)
, the category of sets equipped with the product.
We will investigate the (very simple) classical bar construction
\barconst_{(\Set,\times)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}
and cobar construction
\cobarconst_{(\Set,\times)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}
(Definition
3.5
).
This also determines — in principle — the bar and cobar constructions

\barconst_{(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}
and
\cobarconst_{(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}
of simplicial sets because the product, and thus the bar and cobar functors, are computed point-wise.
Since the point (unit of
\times
) is final, by Corollary
4.14
,
\rho^{*}
is an equivalence:

\rho^{*}:((\Set,\times)^{\vee})^{(\Delta,*)^{\op}}\cong((\Set,\times)^{\vee})^{{}^{\downarrow\uparrow}\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}.

Furthermore, we have

((\Set,\times)^{\vee})^{(\Delta,*)^{\op}}\cong\Coalg(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)

by the trivial Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem
4.70
, and finally

\Coalg(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)\cong\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}

because
\times
is the product.

Lemma 6.2
.

We have an isomorphism (making the identifications in
6.1
)

\boxed{N\cong(\rho^{*})^{-1}\circ\barconst}

of functors

\mathrm{Mon}\to\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}

where
N
is the nerve.

Proof.

Clear from the definitions.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

For
A\in\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}
a simplicial set, the monoid

\cobarconst\rho^{*}A
(making the identifications in
6.1
) is generated by
A_{[1]}
modulo the relations

\displaystyle a_{0}=1

for
a_{0}\in A_{[0]}

\displaystyle\delta_{1}(a_{2})=\delta_{2}(a_{2})\cdot\delta_{0}(a_{2})

for
a_{2}\in A_{[2]}

In particular, if
A
is path-connected, then this is the fundamental monoid of the simplicial set.

Proof.

Recall the discussion
3.19
. The coequalizer (
30
) with its induced algebra structure is obviously the monoid described in the statement. Notice that the “comultiplication”

A_{[2]}\to A_{[1]}\times A_{[1]}

is given by
(\delta_{2},\delta_{0})
in this case (trivial Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem
4.70
).
∎

Let
G:\mathrm{Mon}\to\mathrm{Grp}
the functor “group completion” and
F:\Set\to\mathrm{Grp}
the free group functor.

Lemma 6.4
.

For a simplicial set
A
such that the map
A\to A_{[-1]}
is simply connected (where
A_{[-1]}=\colim A
) and choosing a splitting
A_{[-1]}\to A_{[0]}
,
we have an isomorphism
55
55

55

where the quotient has to be interpreted in the
only possible way as the quotient modulo the smallest normal subgroup containing
F(A_{[-1]})
or, what is the same here, imposing the relations
[x]\sim 1
for all
x\in A_{[-1]}
or, in other words, taking
F(A_{[0]}\setminus A_{[-1]})
.
:

G\cobarconst\rho^{*}A=F(A_{[0]})/F(A_{[-1]})

induced by

\displaystyle\phi:A_{[1]}

\displaystyle\to F(A_{[0]})/F(A_{[-1]})

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto(\delta_{0}x)\cdot(\delta_{1}x)^{-1}.

Proof.

Both sides commute with coproducts and thus it suffices to see the simply-connected case.
By Lemma
6.3
the group
G\cobarconst\rho^{*}A
is generated by

A_{[1]}
modulo the relations

\displaystyle a_{0}=1

for
a_{0}\in A_{[0]}

\displaystyle\delta_{1}(a_{2})=\delta_{0}(a_{2})\cdot\delta_{2}(a_{2})

for
a_{2}\in A_{[2]}

The morphism
\phi
obviously respects these relations.
We have to see that it is bijective.

If
\phi(x)=1
then
x
is a product of products
x_{1}^{\pm 1}\dots x_{n}^{\pm 1}
with the property that
\delta_{1}(x_{1}^{\pm 1})=\delta_{0}(x_{n}^{\pm 1})
and
\delta_{0}(x_{i}^{\pm 1})=\delta_{1}(x_{i+1}^{\pm 1})
where we understood
\delta_{i}(x^{-1})=\delta_{1-i}(x)
. The
x_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,x_{n}^{\pm 1}
thus constitute closed paths which means that
x_{1}^{\pm 1}\cdots x_{n}^{\pm 1}\equiv 1
modulo the relations by assumption (simple connectivity).

For
x\in X_{[0]}
there is a path
x_{1}^{\pm 1}\dots x_{n}^{\pm 1}
from an element in the image of
X_{[-1]}
to
x
. It follows then that

x_{1}^{\pm 1}\dots x_{n}^{\pm 1}
is mapped to
x
under the map
\phi
.
∎

6.5.

By Lemma
4.47
, for
A=\dec^{*}(X)_{[k],\bullet}
, we have
A_{[-1]}=X_{[k]}
, and the map
A\to A_{[-1]}
is simply connected.
However, in this case, there are extra degeneracies:
s_{\can}=s_{k}:A_{[-1]}\to A_{[0]}
(which we take as splitting),
s_{k}:A_{[0]}\to A_{[1]}
,
s_{k}:A_{[1]}\to A_{[2]}
, and
we can make this thus more explicit:
Since for
x\in A_{[1]}
,

\underbrace{\delta_{k+3}}_{\delta_{2}^{A}}(s_{\can}x)=s_{\can}(\underbrace{\delta_{k+2}}_{\delta_{1}^{A}}x),\underbrace{\delta_{k+2}}_{\delta_{1}^{A}}(s_{\can}x)=s_{\can}(\underbrace{\delta_{k+1}}_{\delta_{0}^{A}}x),\text{ and }\underbrace{\delta_{k+1}}_{\delta_{0}^{A}}(s_{\can}x)=x,

we have the relations

x=s_{\can}(\delta_{0}x)s_{\can}(\delta_{1}x)^{-1}\quad x^{-1}=s_{\can}(\delta_{0}x)s_{\can}(\delta_{1}x)^{-1}

in
G\cobarconst\rho^{*}A
. Hence we see directly that
x_{1}^{\pm 1}\dots x_{n}^{\pm 1}=1
in
G\cobarconst\rho^{*}A
(for
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
as in the proof). Hence
\phi
is injective.
Furthermore, for
x\in A_{[0]}
, we have
\phi(s_{\can}x)=x\cdot(s_{\can}(\delta_{0}x))^{-1}
where the second factor is in
F(A_{[-1]})
. Hence
\phi
is surjective.
Furthermore

\displaystyle F(A_{[0]})/F(A_{[-1]})

\displaystyle\to G(\coprod_{n}A_{[1]}^{n}/\cdots)

\displaystyle[x]

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{\can}x

is an inverse of
\phi
.

6.2
Classical (co)bar for
\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}

6.6.

We get an adjunction

\textstyle{\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cobarconst\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{Mon}^{\Delta^{\op}}}
\scriptstyle{\dec_{*}\circ(\rho^{*})^{-1}\circ\barconst}

where now
\cobarconst=\cobarconst_{(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}
and
\barconst=\barconst_{(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}
are computed point-wise because
\times
has this property. We will investigate this adjunction explicitly in this section.

Definition 6.7
.

The functor

M^{\mathrm{Kan}}:=\cobarconst\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}:\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}\to\mathrm{Mon}^{\Delta^{\op}}

is called the
geometric cobar construction
.

The functor is closely related to Kan’s loop group functor hence the notation.
Let
F:\Set\to\mathrm{Grp}
the free group functor which we denote by the same letter on diagrams.

Definition 6.8
.

1.

Let
X\in\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}
be simplicial set. Define a simplicial group called
Kan’s loop group
by

G^{\mathrm{Kan}}(X)_{[k]}:=F(X_{[k+1]})/F(X_{[k]})

with the simplicial structure given by

\delta_{i}^{\prime}(x)=\begin{cases}(\delta_{k}x)(\delta_{k+1}x)^{-1}&i=k;\\
\delta_{i}x&i<k.\end{cases}

2.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Mon}^{\Delta^{\op}}
be simplicial monoid. Define a simplicial set called the
classifying space
by

(\overline{W}X)_{[n]}:=X_{[n-1]}\times X_{[n-2]}\times\cdots\times X_{[0]}

where the simiplicial structure is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{i}(x_{n-1},\dots,x_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(x_{n-2},\dots,x_{0})&i=0,\\
\delta_{i}(x_{n-1}),\dots,\delta_{1}(x_{n-i-1}),x_{n-i-1}\cdot\delta_{0}(x_{n-i}),x_{n-i-2},\dots,x_{0})&1\leq i<n,\end{cases}

\displaystyle s_{i}(x_{n-1},\dots,x_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(1,x_{n-1},\dots,x_{0})&i=0,\\
(s_{i-1}(x_{n-1}),\dots,s_{0}(x_{n-i}),1,x_{n-i-1},\dots,x_{0})&1\leq i<n.\end{cases}

Let
G:\mathrm{Mon}\to\mathrm{Grp}
be the point-wise group completion functor which we denote by the same letter on diagrams.

Proposition 6.9
.

We have for
\cobarconst=\cobarconst_{(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}
an isomorphism

\boxed{G^{\mathrm{Kan}}\cong G\circ\cobarconst\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}}

of functors

\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}\to\mathrm{Grp}^{\Delta^{\op}}

See also
[
52
, Proposition 5.3]
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
6.4
(cf. also
6.5
). The simplicial structure can be (for
i=0,\dots,k
) read off from:

\textstyle{G\coprod(\dec^{*}X)_{[k],[1]}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{i}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces F(X_{[k+1]})/F(X_{[k]})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{k}}
\textstyle{G\coprod(\dec^{*}X)_{[k-1],[1]}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{k}\cdot\delta_{k+1}^{-1}}
\textstyle{F(X_{[k]})/F(X_{[k-1]})}

Proposition 6.10

(Duskin
[
21
]
)
.

We have for
\barconst=\barconst_{(\Set^{\Delta^{\op}},\times)\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}}
an isomorphism

\boxed{\overline{W}\cong\dec_{*}\circ(\rho^{*})^{-1}\circ\barconst}

of functors

\mathrm{Mon}^{\Delta^{\op}}\to\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}.

Proof.

Let
X
be a simplicial monoid. We have by Lemma
6.2
applied point-wise that
Y=(\rho^{*})^{-1}\circ\barconst(X)
is
the bisimplicial set
Y_{[n],[m]}=N(X_{[n]})_{[m]}
. By Proposition
4.89

\displaystyle(\dec_{*}Y)_{[n]}\cong\lim\left(\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.38837pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.38837pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise and there are maps

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}:(\overline{W}(X))_{[n]}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle(g_{n-1},\dots,g_{0})

\displaystyle\mapsto
