and other-wise to something degenerate.
For these we have
\sgn(\underline{i}_{1}^{\prime})\sgn(\underline{i}_{2}^{\prime})=(-1)^{(a+1)b}\sgn(\underline{i})

in such a way that

\displaystyle\sum_{\underline{i}}(s^{-1})^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}(k+1)}\sgn(\underline{i})\mu(\delta_{0}\underline{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a+b=k-1}(s^{-1})^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}(k+1)}(-1)^{(a+1)b}\sgn(\underline{i}_{1})\sgn(\underline{i}_{2})(\mu(\underline{i}_{1}^{\prime})\otimes\mu(\underline{i}_{2}^{\prime}))F(m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{a+b=k-1}(-1)^{(a+1)b}\sgn(\underline{i}_{1})\sgn(\underline{i}_{2})\cdot

\displaystyle((s^{-1})^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}(a+1)}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}(s^{-1})^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}(b+1)})(\mu(\underline{i}_{1}^{\prime})\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\mu(\underline{i}_{2}^{\prime}))(s\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}s)(s^{-1}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}s^{-1})F(m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{a+b=k-1}(-1)^{(a+1)b}(-1)^{a(b+1)}(-1)(\alpha_{a}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\alpha_{b})\widetilde{F(m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a+b=k-1}(-1)^{k-1}(\alpha_{a}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\alpha_{b})\widetilde{F(m)}

\delta_{k}\underline{i}
is equal to

\textstyle{[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{i_{k-1}+1}}
\textstyle{[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[k]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{i_{0}+1}}
\textstyle{[k+1].}

Thus
\delta_{k}\underline{i}=\delta_{i_{0}+1}\circ\underline{i}
and therefore

(1^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}i_{0}}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}G(m)\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}1^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}k-i_{0}-1})\mu(\underline{i}^{\prime})=\mu(\delta_{0}\underline{i})

where
\underline{i}^{\prime}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k-1})
.
and therefore

\sum_{\underline{i}}(-1)^{k}\sgn(\underline{i})(s^{-1})^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}(k+1)}\mu(\delta_{k}\underline{i})=\sum_{\underline{i}}(s^{-1})^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}(k+1)}(-1)^{k}\sgn(\underline{i})(1^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}i_{0}}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}G(m)\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}1^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}k-i_{0}-1})\mu(\underline{i}^{\prime})

=\sum_{\underline{i}}(-1)^{k}\sgn(\underline{i})(-1)^{i_{0}}(1^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}i_{0}}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}(s^{-1}\otimes s^{-1})G(m)s\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}1^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}k-i_{0}-1})(s^{-1})^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}k}\mu(\underline{i}^{\prime})

=\sum_{\underline{i}^{\prime}}(-1)^{k}\sgn(\underline{i}^{\prime})\widetilde{G(m)}(s^{-1})^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}k}\mu(\underline{i}^{\prime})

=(-1)^{k}\widetilde{G(m)}\alpha_{k}

where
\widetilde{G(m)}
now has been extended — as derivation — to
s^{-1}G
.

Putting everything together, we arrive at

(-1)^{k}(\dd_{G}\alpha_{k+1}-\alpha_{k+1}\dd_{F})=(-1)^{k}\widetilde{G(m)}\alpha_{k}-(-1)^{k}\sum_{i+j=k}(\alpha_{i}\otimes\alpha_{j})\widetilde{F(m)}

i.e.

\sum_{i+j=k}(\alpha_{i}\otimes\alpha_{j})\widetilde{F(m)}-\alpha_{k+1}\dd_{F}=\widetilde{G(m)}\alpha_{k}-\dd_{G}\alpha_{k+1}

so indeed
(\alpha_{k})
is a morphism of
A_{\infty}
-coalgebras, observing that (on
F
say)
\dd_{1}=-\dd
and
\dd_{2}=\widetilde{F(m)}
.

The proof that this association is compatible with composition is omitted for the moment.
∎

6.10
Functoriality (Szczarba and Hess-Tonks morphisms)

6.42.

In this section, we relate the following two objects for a simplicial set
X\in\Set^{\Delta^{\op}}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{EZ}^{\vee}\circ\mathbb{Z}[M^{\mathrm{Kan}}(X)]\cong

\displaystyle\,\mathfrak{EZ}^{\vee}\circ\mathbb{Z}[\cobarconst\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}X],

\displaystyle\cobarconst^{\mathrm{Adams}}\circ P\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]\cong

\displaystyle\,\cobarconst_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X].

which, in more traditional language, would be called
CM^{\mathrm{Kan}}(X)
and
\Omega C(X)

— the translation of the (co)algebra structures via AW and EZ understood.

6.43.

Recall from the Abelian Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem
5.16
that we have functors (with mate) of (co)operads:

\mathfrak{AW}:(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}\to(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}\qquad\mathfrak{AW}^{\vee}:(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})\to(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)

and

\mathfrak{EZ}:(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}\to(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}\qquad\mathfrak{EZ}^{\vee}:(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)\to(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})

6.44.

We have seen in Lemma
6.38
that (quite obviously because
\mathbb{Z}[-]
is cocontinuous and monoidal):

\cobarconst_{\otimes}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]\cong\mathbb{Z}[\cobarconst\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}X].

Thus, it remains to compare the following two dg-algebras

\displaystyle\mathfrak{EZ}^{\vee}\circ\cobarconst_{\otimes}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X],

\displaystyle\cobarconst_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X].

Definition 6.45
.

1.

The following composition of morphisms of dg-algebras is called the
Szczarba morphism

\phi
:

\textstyle{\cobarconst_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{EZ}-\text{functoriality (Proposition\penalty\ \ref{PROPFUNCTCOBAR})}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{EZ}^{\vee}\circ\cobarconst_{\otimes}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\mathfrak{EZ}_{1}\circ\dec^{*}_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
Lemma
C.11
\textstyle{\mathfrak{EZ}^{\vee}\circ\cobarconst_{\otimes}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\circ\mathfrak{EZ}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sh(\mathbb{Z}[X])}
The Shih-operator in disguise, see
6.47
\textstyle{\mathfrak{EZ}^{\vee}\circ\cobarconst_{\otimes}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]}

where we wrote
\dec^{*}_{\otimes}:=\mathfrak{AW}_{2}\circ\dec^{*}
.

2.

The following composition of morphisms of dg-algebras is called the
Hess-Tonks morphism

\psi
:

\textstyle{\mathfrak{EZ}^{\vee}\circ\cobarconst_{\otimes}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{AW}-\text{functoriality (Proposition\penalty\ \ref{PROPFUNCTCOBAR})}}
\textstyle{\cobarconst_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\mathfrak{AW}_{1}\circ\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sh(\mathbb{Z}[X])^{-1}}
The Shih-operator in disguise, see
6.47
\textstyle{\widehat{\cobarconst}_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\mathfrak{AW}_{1}\circ\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\circ\mathfrak{EZ}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
Lemma
C.11
\textstyle{\widehat{\cobarconst}_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]}

Here
\widehat{\cobarconst}_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}
is a natural completion of
\cobarconst
defined in Definition
6.25
.

We will see later in Corollary
6.49
that, the first morphism is the same as the one given by Szczarba in
[
53
]
(up to different indexing convention, see
5.55
). That there is a relation between the Shih and Szczarba constructions has been observed before, cf.
[
24
]
. It is not completely clear (to me), however, how the
construction of the deformation in
[
24
]
relates to the construction in
6.47
below.

It seems likely that the second morphism, optimistically called Hess-Tonks morphism, gives the morphism defined in
[
33
, 2.1]
(at least up to the same reindexing) but this remains to be checked in detail.

Remark 6.46
.

Notice that, in 2., there is no direct map of the form

\textstyle{\cobarconst_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\mathfrak{AW}_{1}\circ\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lightning}
\textstyle{\cobarconst_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\rho^{*}\circ\dec^{*}_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]}

because the analogous diagram to the one in Lemma
C.11
with the Alexander-Whitney morphisms does not commute!

6.47.

The transport of the Shih operator:

It remains to see how the deformation
\Xi^{\vee}
(cf. Theorem
5.15
, 3.) gives rise to the dashed morphisms in Definition
6.45
.

Recall from
5.18
the construction of the higher Shih operators which constitute a coherent transformation
\mathfrak{Ez}^{*}H=\mathfrak{Ez}^{*}\exp(L_{(\mathcal{C},\otimes)^{\vee}}(\Xi^{\vee}))\in\underline{\Coh}_{0}(\id,\mathfrak{EZ}\circ\mathfrak{AW})
where on the source the discrete enrichment and on the destination the weak enrichment
F\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}}
(
5.30
) is chosen.

For a dg-coalgebra
C\in\Coalg(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)
, it yields a coherent transformation
\mathfrak{Ez}^{*}H(C)\in\underline{\Coh}_{0}(C,\mathfrak{EZ}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ C)
for the discrete enrichment on
\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}
and for the weak enrichment
F\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}_{(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}}
on the target.

Forgetting the counit, we get a coherent transformation

\mathfrak{Ez}^{*}H(C)\in\underline{\Coh}_{0}(C,\mathfrak{EZ}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ C)

for the truncated weak enrichment
F\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}},t}_{(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\circ,\vee}}
(cf. Definition
5.36
) on the target.

Reason:
We have obviously
\mathbb{Z}[N([1]\times_{/\Delta^{\circ}_{\act}}\Delta^{\circ}_{\act}\times_{/\Delta^{\circ}_{\act}}[k+1])]_{n}=0
for
n>k
because every element of degree
>k
in this nerve must be degenerate.

Proposition
C.7
states that
\tau\dec^{*}_{\otimes}
is a weakly enriched functor

(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\circ,\vee}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\underline{\otimes}}})^{\circ,\vee}

for the weak
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)
-enrichment
\Hom^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}},t}_{(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\circ,\vee}}
on the left and the
(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)
-enrichment
\Aw^{*}\Hom^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}},t}_{(\Ab^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\underline{\otimes}}})^{\circ,\vee}}
(second variable) on the right.

The coherent transformation
\mathfrak{Ez}^{*}H(C)
yields thus a coherent transformation

\tau\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\mathfrak{Ez}^{*}H(C)\in\underline{\Coh}_{0}(\tau\dec^{*}_{\otimes}C,\tau\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\mathfrak{EZ}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ C).

Applying Proposition
6.40
produces a morphism of
A_{\infty}
-coalgebras, by definition a morphism of dg-algebras:

\Sh(C):\mlq T^{\prod}\mrq(s\tau\dec^{*}_{\otimes}C)\to\mlq T^{\prod}\mrq(s\tau\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\mathfrak{EZ}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ C).

Note that because the first component
\Sh(C)_{1}
is the identity, this morphism of
A_{\infty}
-coalgebras is invertible.

Theorem 6.48
.

The morphism
\Sh(C)
constructed above is bounded (that is, it maps
\cobarconst
to
\cobarconst
(without completion)) and we have a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{H^{0}(T^{\oplus}(s\tau\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\circ\mathfrak{EZ}\circ\mathfrak{AW}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sh(\mathbb{Z}[X])}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}(\tau_{\geq 1}\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X])_{1}^{\otimes k}/I^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces s\tau_{\geq 1}\mathbb{Z}[X]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{\can}}
\scriptstyle{\Sz(\mathbb{Z}[X])}
\textstyle{H^{0}(T^{\oplus}(s\tau\dec^{*}_{\otimes}\circ\mathbb{Z}[X]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}(\tau_{\geq 1}\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X])_{1}^{\otimes k}/I}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}(\tau_{\geq 1}\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X])_{[1]}^{\otimes k}/\widetilde{I}}
\scriptstyle{1+\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\sim}

where the map
\Sz
on the right has components

\Sz_{k}:X_{[k]}\to\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}(\tau_{\geq 1}\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X])_{[1]}^{\otimes k}

given by

\Sz_{k}(s^{-1}x)=\begin{cases}-1+x&k=1\\
\sum_{\underline{i}}\sgn(\underline{i}^{\vee})s_{\can}(\Sz_{\underline{i}}^{0}\ast^{\prime}\id_{\Delta_{1}})(s^{-1}x)\otimes\cdots\otimes(\Sz_{\underline{i}}^{k}\ast^{\prime}\id_{\Delta_{1}})(s^{-1}x)&k>1\end{cases}

for the Szczarba-operators
\Sz_{\underline{i}}^{j}
defined in Definition
5.54
.

Corollary 6.49
.

The Szczarba morphism in Definition
6.45
is up a different indexing (cf.
5.55
) the morphism given by Szczarba in
[
53
]
, see also
[
33
, Theorem 7]
.

Proof of Theorem
6.48
.

The components

(\mathfrak{Ez}^{*}H)_{\underline{i}}\in\underline{\Hom}_{k}(\mathbb{Z}[X],\mathbb{Z}[X^{k+1}])

of
\mathfrak{Ez}^{*}H
, i.e. the higher Shih operators, are
by Proposition
5.21
given by

(\mathfrak{Ez}^{*}H)_{\underline{i}}^{n}=\sum_{\underline{b}}\sgn(\sigma_{\underline{b}})H_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}.

and by Proposition
5.51
and Proposition
5.57
we have that (the projection of)
\sgn(\sigma_{\underline{b}})H_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}
is given by

\sgn(\sigma_{\underline{b}})\mathcal{P}\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}\equiv\begin{cases}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}}\text{ such that }\\
\text{$\underline{i}$ is a $\underline{b}$-reduction of $\widetilde{\underline{i}}$}\end{subarray}}\varepsilon(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}q_{0})\otimes\cdots\otimes(\Sz^{c_{k}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}q_{k})&\underline{b}_{0}<\widetilde{k}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

modulo degenerates, where
q_{j}=s_{j,j+1}^{k+1}
and

\varepsilon=(-1)^{(\widetilde{k}+1)k}\sgn(\underline{i}^{\vee})\sgn(\widetilde{\underline{i}}^{\vee}).

Thus
\mu(\underline{i})
is represented by maps

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}[X]_{n-k}

\displaystyle\to(\mathbb{Z}[X]^{\otimes k+1})_{n}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\underline{b}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}}\text{ such that }\\
\text{$\underline{i}$ is a $\underline{b}$-reduction of $\widetilde{\underline{i}}$}\end{subarray}}\varepsilon(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}q_{0})\otimes\cdots\otimes(\Sz^{c_{k}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}q_{k})

modulo degenerates and the kernel of
\mathcal{P}
, where the
\underline{b}
run over sequences
(b_{0},\dots,b_{k})
with
\widetilde{k}>b_{0}>\dots>b_{k}\geq 0
only.

Under the functor
\tau\dec^{*}_{\otimes}
of weakly enriched operads of Proposition
C.7
this is mapped to a morphism:

\underline{\Hom}_{k}(\tau_{\leq 1}\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X],(\tau_{\leq 1}\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X])^{\mathbin{\tilde{\underline{\otimes}}}k+1}).

Contemplating the construction in
C.6
we can first map it to

\underline{\Hom}_{k}(\tau_{\leq 1}\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X],(\tau_{\leq 1}\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X])^{\otimes k+1})

(97)

by the morphism in Proposition
C.5
and then apply the
\mathfrak{AW}_{2}
morphism.
The image in (
97
) is thus represented by morphisms (we will only need the case in which the first component is zero)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}[X]_{i+j+1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[X]_{i+j}

\displaystyle\to(\mathbb{Z}[X]^{\otimes k+1})_{i+j+k+1}\oplus(\mathbb{Z}[X]^{\otimes k+1})_{i+j+k}

\displaystyle(0,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto(0,(-1)^{ik}\sum_{\underline{b}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}}\text{ such that }\\
\text{$\underline{i}$ is a $\underline{b}$-reduction of $\widetilde{\underline{i}}$}\end{subarray}}\varepsilon(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}q_{0})\otimes\cdots\otimes(\Sz^{c_{k}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}q_{k}))

where now
\widetilde{k}=i+j-1
. We compose this with the bottom line map in the commutative diagram (cf. Proposition
5.42
):

\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z}[X]^{\otimes k+1})_{[i+j+k]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{\can}}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z}[X]^{\otimes k+1})_{[i+j+k+1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{(0)},\dots,\delta^{(k)}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{j_{0}+\cdots+j_{k}=j+k}(\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X])_{[i+j_{0}+1]}\otimes\cdots\otimes(\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X])_{[i+j_{k}+1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z}[X]^{\otimes k+1})_{i+j+k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X]^{\otimes k+1})_{i,j+k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{AW}_{2}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{j_{0}+\cdots+j_{k}=j+k}((\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X])_{\bullet,j_{0}}\otimes\cdots\otimes(\dec^{*}\mathbb{Z}[X])_{\bullet,j_{k}})_{i}}

with

\delta^{(e)}=\id_{[i]}\ast\delta^{j+k}_{\sum_{f<e}j_{f},\sum_{f\leq e}j_{f}}

where, however, only summands with
j_{0},\dots,j_{k}\geq 1
occur because of the truncation. Now set
j=1
(hence
i=\widetilde{k}
) because this is the column appearing in the cobar construction.
In this case, there is only one summand and we have

\delta^{(e)}s_{\can}=\begin{cases}(\id_{[\widetilde{k}]}\ast\delta^{k+1}_{0,1})s_{\can}&e=0,\\
\id_{[\widetilde{k}]}\ast\delta^{k+1}_{e-1,e}&e>1.\end{cases}

This shows that elements in the kernel of
\mathcal{P}
are mapped to degenerate (in the second index of
\dec^{*}
) elements. Furthermore

\displaystyle(\id_{[\widetilde{k}]}\ast\delta^{k+1}_{e,e+1})\circ s_{\can}\circ(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}q_{e})

\displaystyle=(\id_{[\widetilde{k}]}\ast\delta^{k+1}_{e,e+1})\circ(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast s_{e,e+1}^{k+1})\circ s_{\can}

\displaystyle=(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast\id_{[1]})\circ s_{\can}=s_{\can}\circ(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}\id_{[1]}).

The composition is equal to

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}[X]_{\widetilde{k}+1}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[X]_{[\widetilde{k}+2]}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{Z}[X]_{[\widetilde{k}+2]}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto(-1)^{\widetilde{k}k}\sum_{\underline{b}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}}\text{ such that }\\
\text{$\underline{i}$ is a $\underline{b}$-reduction of $\widetilde{\underline{i}}$}\end{subarray}}\varepsilon s_{\can}(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}\id_{[1]})\otimes\cdots\otimes(\Sz^{c_{k}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}\id_{[1]})

modulo degenerates.

Under the morphism in Proposition
6.40
this yields a morphism of
A_{\infty}
-coalgebras with components (in degree 1 and
\widetilde{k}
)

\alpha_{k+1}=(s^{-1})^{k+1}(-1)^{k}\sum_{\underline{i},\underline{b}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}}\text{ such that }\\
\text{$\underline{i}$ is a $\underline{b}$-reduction of $\widetilde{\underline{i}}$}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\frac{(k-1)k}{2}}\sgn(\widetilde{\underline{i}}^{\vee})s_{\can}(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}\id_{[1]})\otimes\cdots\otimes(\Sz^{c_{k}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}\id_{[1]}))

(Notice that
\sgn(\underline{i}^{\vee})=(-1)^{\frac{(k-1)k}{2}}\sgn(\underline{i})
).
The
(s^{-1})^{k+1}
contributes (Koszul convention) a sign of
(-1)^{\frac{k(k+1)}{2}}
,
so

\alpha_{k+1}=\sum_{\underline{i},\underline{b}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}}\text{ such that }\\
\text{$\underline{i}$ is a $\underline{b}$-reduction of $\widetilde{\underline{i}}$}\end{subarray}}\sgn(\widetilde{\underline{i}}^{\vee})s_{\can}((\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}\id_{[1]})(s^{-1}x)\otimes\cdots\otimes(\Sz^{c_{k}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}}\ast^{\prime}\id_{[1]})(s^{-1}x)).

If we take
x\in X_{\widetilde{k}+1}
we have:

(1-\varepsilon)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{n+1}(x)=\begin{cases}-1+x&\widetilde{k}=1\\
\sum_{\underline{i}}\sgn(\underline{i}^{\vee})(\Sz^{0}_{\underline{i}}(s^{-1}x)\otimes\cdots\otimes\Sz^{k}_{\underline{i}}(s^{-1}x))&\widetilde{k}>1\end{cases}

by Proposition
5.60
.
The
-1
is the constant term for k=1. For higher
k
the constant term is degenerate and thus can be omitted.
∎

Proposition 6.50
.

We have
\psi\phi=\id
and there is a chain homotopy
\psi\phi\Rightarrow\id
.

Proof.

Omitted for the moment.
∎

Appendix A
Exactness of some diagrams

Lemma A.1
.

The following natural morphism is an isomorphism of 1-profunctors (and also
\infty
-profunctors):

{}^{t}\!\pi\cong{}^{t}\!i\,\emptyset.

Proof.

The diagram

\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\cdot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\emptyset}
\scriptstyle{\Nearrow}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}}

is
\infty
-exact, because
\emptyset
is final in
\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}
.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

The following natural morphism is an isomorphism of 1-profunctors (and also
\infty
-profunctors):

{}^{t}\!\pr_{1}\,{}^{t}\!i\cong{}^{t}\!(i,i)\,p_{1}.

Proof.

We have to see that the diagram

\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i,\pi)}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}\times\cdot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}=(\id,\emptyset)}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i,i)}
\scriptstyle{\Nearrow}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}\times\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}}

is
\infty
-exact, which is the product of the one of Lemma
A.1
with a trivially exact one.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

The following natural morphism is an isomorphism of 1-profunctors (and also
\infty
-profunctors):

\dec\,{}^{t}\!\pr_{i}\cong\id.

In particular, we also have
\dec\,{}^{t}\!\pi_{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}\cong\,{}^{t}\!\pi_{\Delta^{\op}}
, in other words,
\dec
is
\infty
-cofinal.

Proof.

We have to show that the diagram

\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{i}}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}}

is
\infty
-exact.
Since
\pr_{i}
is a cofibration, it suffices to see that

\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[n]}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}}

is exact (where
\dec_{n}:=([n],\id)\circ\dec
) for all
n
.
Using criterion (Lemma
2.8
, 4.) it boils down to show that the following category is contractible:
Its objects are morphisms

[a]\leftarrow[b]\ast[n]

such that the composition
[a]\leftarrow[n]
is fixed, and morphisms are the morphisms
[b]\leftarrow[b^{\prime}]
making

\textstyle{[a]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[b]\ast[n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[b^{\prime}]\ast[n]}

commute.
Now write
[a]=[a^{\prime}]\ast^{\prime}[a^{\prime\prime}]
(where
\ast^{\prime}
identifies the endpoints)
such that
a^{\prime\prime}
is minimal with a factorization of the given morphism:

[a]=[a^{\prime}]\ast^{\prime}[a^{\prime\prime}]\leftarrow[a^{\prime\prime}]\leftarrow[n].

A morphism
[a]\leftarrow[b]\ast[n]
is then completely determined by a morphism
[a^{\prime}]\leftarrow[b]
to the extent that the category in question is isomorphic
to

[a^{\prime}]\times_{/\Delta^{\op}}\Delta^{\op}

which is contractible, having an initial object.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

\delta
is
\infty
-cofinal, i.e. we have that the natural morphism

\delta\,{}^{t}\!\pi\cong{}^{t}\!\pi.

is an isomorphism of
\infty
-profunctors.

Proof.

We have to show that the diagram

\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\cdot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\Downarrow}
\textstyle{\cdot}

is
\infty
-exact.

Using criterion (Lemma
2.8
, 4.) it boils down to show that the following category is contractible:

[n],[m]\times_{/\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op},\delta}\Delta^{\op}

This category is cofibered over
\Delta^{\op}
with fibers being the set
\Hom_{\Delta}(-,[n]\times[m])
. But the
latter simplicial set is the nerve of the category
[n]\times[m]
which is obviously contractible, having an initial object (or here equivalently a final object).
Moreover, for any category there
is a natural transformation
\int_{\Delta^{\op}}N(I)\to I
which is a weak equivalence (i.e. the nerve applied to it is a weak equivalence).
∎

Lemma A.5
.

The following natural morphism is an isomorphism of 1-profunctors (and also
\infty
-profunctors):

\dec\,{}^{t}\!(i,i)\cong{}^{t}\!i\,\dec_{\emptyset}.

Proof.

Look at the compositions:

\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec}
\scriptstyle{(i,i)}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i,i)}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}\times\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec_{\emptyset}}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}}

and

\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i\pr_{i}}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec}
\scriptstyle{(i,i)}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{i}}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}\times\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec_{\emptyset}}
\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}_{\emptyset}}

We have to show that the common right square is
\infty
-exact.
Since
(i,i)^{*}
,
p_{1}^{*}
, and
p_{2}^{*}
, are jointly conservative, it suffices to see that the left and composite squares are
\infty
-exact in both cases. This is either because
i
and
(i,i)
are fully-faithful, or a consequence of Lemmas
A.2
–
A.3
.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

\iota:\Delta^{\op}\to\FinSet^{\op}
is 1-cofinal, i.e.

\textstyle{\Delta^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\FinSet^{\op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdot}

is 1-exact.

Proof.

To show that the categories

\Delta\times_{/\FinSet}[n]

are connected.
Let
[m]\to[n]
and
[k]\to[n]
be morphisms in
\FinSet
. They give rise to a morphism

[m]\ast[k]\to[n]

in
\FinSet
(
\ast
is the coproduct in
\FinSet
). We get a commutative diagram

\textstyle{[m]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[m]\ast[k]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[k]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[n]}

where the inclusions are morphisms in
\Delta
. The category, being non-empty, is thus connected.
∎

Appendix B
Leveled trees, Shih, and Szczarba

This section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
5.57
.
Actually a slight generalization, Proposition
B.5
will be stated and proven, in which the
e
and
\sigma
are needed
to make the induction work.

B.1.

We need the following generalization of (
5.53
).
Let
k,e,m
be non-negative integers.
For
\sigma:[k+e]\twoheadrightarrow[k]
, we define the operator

\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}:\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([n],[m])]^{\otimes(k+1)}\to\mathbb{Z}[\Hom([n],[m])]^{\otimes(k+1)}

by

\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}=\prod_{i=0}^{k+e}1^{\otimes\sigma(i)}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{n-k-e-1+i}\otimes 1^{\otimes k-\sigma(i)}

i.e. it throws away all summands that are
n-k-e-1+i
degenerate at
\sigma(i)
for
i=0,\dots,k+e
.

We also let
\delta:=\Hom(\sigma,[1]):[k+1]\hookrightarrow[k+e+1]
the dual active face map, here with

\delta(j+1)=\delta(j)+\#\sigma^{-1}(j).

For
\sigma=\id
,
e=0
we also write
\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}
.
We denote the jumps

\alpha_{j}:=\delta(j+1)-\delta(j)=\#\sigma^{-1}(j).

We have

\sigma=s^{\alpha_{0}}\ast^{\prime}\id_{[1]}\ast^{\prime}s^{\alpha_{1}}\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}\id_{[1]}\ast^{\prime}s^{\alpha_{k}},

where
\alpha_{i}
is the number of preimages, denoting
s^{\alpha_{i}}:[\alpha_{i}-1]\twoheadrightarrow[0]
.

By formula (
85
) for the signum, we have thus

\sgn(\sigma)=\prod_{i}(-1)^{(k-i)(\alpha_{i}-1)}.

B.2.

In a situation as in
5.56
, given a vector
\underline{b}=(b_{0},\dots,b_{k-1})
with
\widetilde{k}>b_{0}>\dots>b_{k-1}\geq 0
—
which is also seen as a shuffle
\sigma_{\underline{b}}:[n]\to[k]
,
\tau_{\underline{b}}:[n]\to[n-k]
(notice the
n
instead of
\widetilde{k}
) where
\tau_{\underline{b}}
is degenerate precisely at the intervals
b_{i}+1
—
and
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
of length
\widetilde{k}\geq k
, such that

\underline{i}
is a
\underline{b}
-reduction of
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
, then given
\sigma:[k+e]\to[k]
, we get a unique extension

\widetilde{\sigma}:[\widetilde{k}+e]\to[\widetilde{k}]

satisfying

\#(\widetilde{\sigma})^{-1}(i)=\begin{cases}\#\sigma^{-1}(j)&i=c_{j}\text{ for some $j$ }\\
1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Denoting

\beta_{j}:=c_{j}-c_{j-1}-1

(setting
c_{-1}=-1
), we have

\widetilde{\sigma}=\id_{[\beta_{0}]}\ast^{\prime}s^{\alpha_{0}}\ast^{\prime}\id_{[\beta_{1}+1]}\ast^{\prime}s^{\alpha_{1}}\ast^{\prime}\cdots\ast^{\prime}\id_{[\beta_{k}+1]}\ast^{\prime}s^{\alpha_{k}}

and get by formula (
85
) for the signum

\sgn(\widetilde{\sigma})=\prod_{i<j}(-1)^{(\beta_{j}+1)(\alpha_{i}-1)}

which specializes to the formula before, if all
\beta_{j}=0
. In particular,

\sgn(\sigma)\sgn(\widetilde{\sigma})=\prod_{i<j}(-1)^{\beta_{j}(\alpha_{i}-1)}.

(98)

Definition B.3
.

For a vector (i.e. leveled tree)
\underline{i}=(i_{0},\dots,i_{k})
and face
\delta:[k+1]\to[m]
(arbitrary
m\geq k+1
)
define

\Sz_{\underline{i},\delta}^{j}=\Sz_{\underline{i}}^{j}\delta|_{[j]}.

Directly from (
90
) follows:

Lemma B.4
.

\Sz_{\underline{i},\delta}^{j}=\begin{cases}\Sz_{\underline{i}^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}^{j}\ast\delta_{0}^{\alpha_{i_{0}}-1}&j=i_{0}+1\\
s_{i_{0}}\Sz_{\underline{i}^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(99)

with
\delta^{\prime}=\delta_{i_{0}+1}\delta
and
j^{\prime}=s_{i_{0}}(j)
and
\alpha_{i_{0}}=\delta(i_{0}+1)-\delta(i_{0})
.

The following gives Proposition
5.57
in the special case
e=0
and
\sigma=\id
.

Proposition B.5
.

With the notation from
B.1
, we have for arbitrary
n
, setting
\widetilde{k}:=n-k-1-e
:

\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}\equiv\begin{cases}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}}\text{ such that }\\
\text{$\underline{i}$ is a $\underline{b}$-reduction of $\widetilde{\underline{i}}$}\end{subarray}}\varepsilon(\Sz^{c_{0}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}},\widetilde{\delta}}\ast^{\prime}q_{\sigma,0})\otimes\cdots\otimes(\Sz^{c_{k}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}},\widetilde{\delta}}\ast^{\prime}q_{\sigma,k})&\underline{b}_{0}<\widetilde{k}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

modulo degenerates, where
q_{\sigma,j}=s_{\delta(j),\delta(j+1)}^{k+1+e}
. In particular the expession is zero unless
\widetilde{k}\geq k
(i.e.
n\geq 2k+e+1
).

Here the
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
are vectors of length
\widetilde{k}
and
c_{0},\dots,c_{k}
are determined by
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
as in
5.56
and

\varepsilon=(-1)^{(\widetilde{k}+1)k}\sgn(\underline{i}^{\vee})\sgn(\widetilde{\underline{i}}^{\vee})\sgn(\sigma)\sgn(\widetilde{\sigma})\sgn(\sigma_{\underline{b}})

The
\Sz_{\overline{i},\delta}^{j}\ast^{\prime}q_{\sigma j}
have values in
[\widetilde{\delta}(c_{j})]\ast^{\prime}[\#\sigma^{-1}(j)]=[\widetilde{\delta}(c_{j}+1)]
and are considered as
[n-k]
-valued via
the inclusion
[\widetilde{\delta}(c_{j}+1)]\subset[n-k]
.
\widetilde{\delta}
is the (active) morphism
[\widetilde{k}+1]\hookrightarrow[\widetilde{k}+e+1]
dual to
\widetilde{\sigma}
(
B.1
).

Before giving the proof, we need a couple of Lemmas:

Lemma B.6
.

Let
\tau\in\Hom([n-1],[m])
. Then

\mathcal{H}_{b}^{n}(\tau)
is a sum of terms of the form
\tau^{\prime}\otimes\tau^{\prime\prime}
that are

•

for
i<b
,
i
-degenerate at the left and right if
\tau
is degenerate at
i
and not
i
-degenerate neither on the left nor right, otherwise.

•

for
i=b
,
i
-degenerate at the left (at least)

•

for
i>b
either
i
-degenerate at the right or at the left (not both) if
\tau
is not-
i-1
-degenerate and fully
i
-degenerate, if
\tau
is
i-1
-degenerate.

Furthermore, for
i\geq b
each term is at least
i
-degenerate at the right for
i=b
or such that
\tau
is not
i-1
-degenerate.

The proof is left to the reader.
We can also say that
\mathcal{H}_{b}^{n}(\tau)
is a sum of terms of the form
s_{b}\tau^{\prime}\otimes s_{i}\tau^{\prime\prime}
for
i\geq b
and such that either
i=b
(thus fully degenerate) or
\tau
is not
i-1
-degenerate.

Lemma B.7
.

Let
\tau_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\tau_{k}
be a summand of
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}
.
Then for
i>b_{0}
at most one
\tau_{j}
is not
i
-degenerate.

Proof.

Induction over Lemma
B.6
(because of
i>b_{0}>\dots>b_{k-1}
, in each step, the third case is relevant).
∎

Lemma B.8
.

If
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}\not\equiv 0
modulo degenerates then
b_{0}<n-k-e-1
.

Proof.

We have

\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}(\tau)=(s_{b_{0}}^{\otimes i_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{b_{0}}^{n}\otimes s_{b_{0}}^{\otimes k-1-i_{0}})\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b}^{\prime},\underline{i}^{\prime}}^{n-1}.

Let
\tau_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\tau_{k-1}
be a term of
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b}^{\prime},\underline{i}^{\prime}}^{n-1}
.
The operator
\mathcal{H}_{b_{0}}^{n}(\tau_{i_{0}})
is (modulo degenerates) a sum over terms of the form
s_{b_{0}}\tau^{\prime}\otimes s_{i}\tau^{\prime\prime}
for
i>b_{0}
such that
\tau_{i_{0}}
is not
i-1
-degenerate (Lemma
B.6
, and the comment after) and thus
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}(\tau)
is a sum over terms of the form

x:=s_{b_{0}}\tau_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes(\underbrace{s_{b_{0}}\tau^{\prime}}_{\text{ at }i_{0}}\otimes\underbrace{s_{i}\tau^{\prime\prime}}_{\text{ at }i_{0}+1})\otimes\cdots\otimes s_{b_{0}}\tau_{k-1}

However, since
i-1>b_{1}
,
\tau_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\tau_{k-1}
is (either degenerate or)
i-1
-degenerate at all except one slot (Lemma
B.7
).
Thus all
\tau_{j}
for
j\not=i_{0}
are
i-1
-degenerate and thus
x
is
i
-degenerate except at
i_{0}
and
i_{0}+1
.
If we assume
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}x\not\equiv 0
,
we must have
\sigma(i)=i_{0}
or
\sigma(i)=i_{0}+1
,
and also
\sigma(b_{0})=i_{0}+1
because
x
is obviously
b_{0}
-degenerate at all other slots.
Hence
\sigma(i)=i_{0}+1
(because
i>b_{0}
) and hence
\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}x\equiv 0
anyway.
∎

Recall from Lemma
5.49
the definition of the operators
B_{\underline{z}}^{j}
.

Lemma B.9
.

Let
n,\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}
be positive integers.

\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{n}=\sum_{\underline{z}}(-1)^{\sum_{i<j}z_{j}(z_{i}+1)+\alpha_{0}\sum z_{i}}(B_{\underline{z}}^{0}\ast^{\prime}s_{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}}^{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}}\otimes B_{\underline{z}}^{1}\delta_{0}^{\alpha_{0}}\ast^{\prime}s_{0,\alpha_{0}}^{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}})

in which
\underline{z}=(z_{0},\dots,z_{n-\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}-1})
runs over all vectors with
z_{i}\in\{0,1\}
.
Implicitly, in the formula the
B_{\underline{z}}^{0}\ast^{\prime}s_{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}}^{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}}
is composed with the inclusion
[m+\alpha_{1}]\hookrightarrow[n+1]
.

Proof.

Follows from Lemma
5.49
identifying the
\underline{z}
-summand above with the

(\underbrace{0,\dots,0}_{\alpha_{0}\text{ times}},\underbrace{1,\dots,1}_{\alpha_{1}\text{ times}},z_{0},\dots,z_{n-\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}-1})-\text{summand}.\qed

Lemma B.10
.

Given
\underline{i}=(i_{0},\dots,i_{k-1})
,
\widetilde{k}\geq k
, and
\underline{b}=(b_{0},\dots,b_{k-1})
with
\widetilde{k}>b_{0}>\dots>b_{k-1}\geq 0
, and denoting
\underline{i}^{\prime}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k-1})

and
\underline{b}^{\prime}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{k-1})
,
we have a bijection

\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}}\\
\text{$\underline{i}^{\prime}$ is a $\underline{b}^{\prime}$-reduction of $\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}}$}\\
\overline{z}\in\{0,1\}^{x}\end{subarray}\right\}\cong\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}}\\
\text{$\underline{i}$ is a $\underline{b}$-reduction of $\widetilde{\underline{i}}$}\end{subarray}\right\}

where
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
has length
\widetilde{k}
(resp.
\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}}
has length
\widetilde{k}-1
), and
x
is the cardinality of

\{j_{0},\dots,j_{x-1}\}=\{0\leq j<\widetilde{k}-b_{0}-1\text{ s.t.{} }y_{j}^{\prime}=i_{0}\}

(100)

(cf.
5.56
for the definition of the
y_{j}^{\prime}
). Then we have

\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}=\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}|_{[b_{0}]}\ast^{\prime}s

(101)

where
\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}|_{\Delta_{b_{0}}}:[b_{0}]\to[c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}-x]

for a degeneracy
s:[\widetilde{k}-b_{0}-1]\to[x]
.

Given
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma s_{i_{0}}
as in
B.1
with dual faces
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}=\delta_{i_{0}+1}\delta
.
Recall their extensions
\widetilde{\delta}:[\widetilde{k}+1]\to[n+1]
and
\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}:[\widetilde{k}]\to[n]
defined in
B.2
.
Then we have:

\Sz^{c_{j}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}},\widetilde{\delta}}=\begin{cases}s_{b_{0}}\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}|_{[b_{0}]}\ast^{\prime}(sB_{\underline{z}}^{0})&j=i_{0},\\
\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}|_{[b_{0}]}\ast(sB_{\underline{z}}^{1}\delta_{0}^{\alpha_{i_{0}}-1})&j=i_{0}+1,\\
s_{b_{0}}\Sz^{c_{s_{i_{0}}(j)}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(102)

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\sgn(\underline{i}^{\vee})\sgn(\widetilde{\underline{i}}^{\vee})\sgn(\sigma)\sgn(\widetilde{\sigma})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{b_{0}-\widetilde{k}+1+\sum_{i<j}z_{j}(z_{i}+1)+\sum_{i}\alpha_{i_{0}}z_{i}}\sgn((\underline{i}^{\prime})^{\vee})\sgn((\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}})^{\vee})\sgn(\sigma^{\prime})\sgn(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\prime}).

(103)

Proof.

The bijection is given, identifying the extensions
\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}}
, and
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
, respectively, with collections
(y_{0}^{\prime},\dots,y_{\widetilde{k}-k-1}^{\prime})
, and
(y_{0},\dots,y_{\widetilde{k}-k-1})
, respectively, as in
5.56
, by

y_{j}:=\begin{cases}y_{j}^{\prime}&j\geq\widetilde{k}-b_{0}-1,\\
y_{j}^{\prime}&j<\widetilde{k}-b_{0}-1\text{ and }y_{j}^{\prime}<i_{0},\\
y_{j}^{\prime}+z_{i}&j<\widetilde{k}-b_{0}-1\text{ and }y_{j}^{\prime}=i_{0},\text{ i.e.{} }j=j_{i}\text{ for some $i$; cf.{} (\ref{eqx}) },\\
y_{j}^{\prime}+1&j<\widetilde{k}-b_{0}-1\text{ and }y_{j}^{\prime}>i_{0}.\end{cases}.

The representation (
101
) follows directly from the recursive property of
\Sz
and the construction of
\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}
.
We show (
102
) and (
103
) by induction on the length
\widetilde{k}
of
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
, starting from
b_{0}+1
.

The induction base is
\widetilde{k}=b_{0}+1
, whence
c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}=c_{i_{0}}=c_{i_{0}+1}-1
,
\widetilde{\underline{i}}=(c_{i_{0}},\widetilde{i}^{\prime}_{0},\dots,\widetilde{i}^{\prime}_{\widetilde{k}-1})
,
\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}=\delta_{c_{i}+1}\widetilde{\delta}
,

\underline{z}=()
,
s=\id_{[0]}
, and (
99
) yields:

\Sz^{c_{j}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}},\widetilde{\delta}}=\begin{cases}\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}\ast\delta_{0}^{\alpha_{i_{0}}-1}&j=i_{0}+1,\\
s_{b_{0}}\Sz^{c_{s_{i_{0}}(j)}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

These yield (
102
). For the sign, notice that we have
\alpha_{j}^{\prime}=\alpha_{\delta_{i_{0}+1}(j)}
for
j\not=i_{0}
and
\alpha_{i_{0}}^{\prime}=\alpha_{i_{0}}+\alpha_{i_{0}+1}
and
\beta_{j}^{\prime}=\beta_{\delta_{i_{0}+1}(j)}
for
j\not=i_{0}+1
and
\beta_{i_{0}+1}=0
. We have to show:

c_{i_{0}}+i_{0}\overset{!}{\equiv}\widetilde{k}-k+\sum_{i<j}(\alpha_{i}-1)\beta_{j}+\sum_{i<j}(\alpha^{\prime}_{i}-1)\beta^{\prime}_{j}\qquad\text{modulo 2}

\overset{!}{\equiv}\widetilde{k}-k-\sum_{i_{0}<j}\beta_{j}+\alpha_{i_{0}}\beta_{i_{0}+1}\qquad\text{modulo 2}

However, we have
c_{i_{0}}=i_{0}+\sum_{i\leq i_{0}}\beta_{i}
and
\sum b_{i}=\widetilde{k}-k
, so the statement holds.

We will now turn to
the case
\widetilde{k}>b_{0}+1
and and assume that the Lemma has been proven for length
\widetilde{k}-1
.

Denote

\widetilde{\underline{i}}=(\widetilde{i}_{0},\widetilde{i}^{\prime\prime}_{0},\dots,\widetilde{i}^{\prime\prime}_{\widetilde{k}-2})
,
\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}}=(\widetilde{i}_{0}^{\prime},\widetilde{i^{\prime}}^{\prime\prime}_{0},\dots,\widetilde{i^{\prime}}^{\prime\prime}_{\widetilde{k}-3})
, with relevant derived quantities
c_{j}^{\prime\prime}
and
c_{j}^{\prime\prime\prime}
,

\underline{y}=(y_{0},y_{0}^{\prime\prime},\dots,y_{\widetilde{k}-k-2}^{\prime\prime})
,
\underline{y}^{\prime}=(y_{0}^{\prime},y_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime},\dots,y_{\widetilde{k}-k-2}^{\prime\prime\prime})
, etc.

Define

\delta^{\prime\prime}:=\delta\delta_{\delta(y_{0})+1}\qquad\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}:=\delta^{\prime}\delta^{\prime}_{\delta^{\prime}(y_{0}^{\prime})+1}.

Thus the corresponding
\alpha_{y_{0}}
increases by 1 while
\beta_{y_{0}}
decreases by 1 and
we have:

\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime\prime}:=\delta_{c_{y_{0}}}\widetilde{\delta}\qquad\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime\prime\prime}:=\delta^{\prime}_{c_{y_{0}}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}.

By construction, we have
c_{y_{0}}=\widetilde{i}_{0}+1
,
y_{0}^{\prime}=s_{i_{0}}(y_{0})
and
c_{y_{0}^{\prime}}^{\prime}=\widetilde{i}_{0}^{\prime}+1

and one of the three cases:

I

y_{0}\notin\{i_{0},i_{0}+1\}
,
\overline{z}=\overline{z}^{\prime\prime}
,
\alpha_{i_{0}}^{\prime\prime}=\alpha_{i_{0}}
;

II

y_{0}=i_{0}
,
\overline{z}=(0,z^{\prime\prime}_{0},\dots,z^{\prime\prime}_{x-2})
,

;

III

y_{0}=i_{0}+1
,
\overline{z}=(1,z^{\prime\prime}_{0},\dots,z^{\prime\prime}_{x-2})
,
\alpha_{i_{0}}^{\prime\prime}=\alpha_{i_{0}}
.

We distinguish also the two cases:

A

j\not=y_{0}
;

B

j=y_{0}
.

We proceed to insert the recursive property of the
\Sz
(
99
), respecively of the

(

), in all 6 cases:

IA)

,

s_{\widetilde{k}-1}\Sz^{c_{j}^{\prime\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}}^{\prime\prime},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime\prime}}\overset{!}{=}\begin{cases}s_{b_{0}}\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}}^{\prime\prime},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime\prime\prime}}|_{[b_{0}]}\ast^{\prime}(s_{\widetilde{k}-2-b_{0}}s^{\prime\prime}B_{\underline{z}^{\prime\prime}}^{0})&j=i_{0},\\

,

Note that e.g.

For the sign equation (

), note that passing from

and

\displaystyle\sgn(\widetilde{\underline{i}}^{\vee})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\widetilde{k}-c_{y_{0}}+1}\sgn((\widetilde{\underline{i}}^{\prime\prime})^{\vee}),

\displaystyle\sgn((\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}})^{\vee})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\widetilde{k}-1-c_{y_{0}^{\prime}}^{\prime}+1}\sgn((\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}}^{\prime\prime})^{\vee}),

\displaystyle\sgn(\sigma)\sgn(\widetilde{\sigma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sgn(\sigma^{\prime\prime})\sgn(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\prime\prime})(-1)^{\sum_{y_{0}<j}\beta_{j}+\sum_{i<y_{0}}(\alpha_{i}-1)},

\displaystyle\sgn(\sigma^{\prime})\sgn(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sgn(\sigma^{\prime\prime\prime})\sgn(\widetilde{\sigma}^{\prime\prime\prime})(-1)^{\sum_{y_{0}^{\prime}<j}\beta_{j}^{\prime}+\sum_{i<y_{0}^{\prime}}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime}-1)}.

Inserting

and

we see that the LHS of (

) picks up a sign of

and the RHS of (

) picks up a sign (taking into account that

decreases by 1) of

In case I and II their product is obviously

. In case III their product is

.
In turn, in case II, the expression

does not change (notice that

also increases by 1).
In case III the expression

changes by a
(-1)^{\alpha_{i_{0}}}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.58
.

In order to be non-degenerate, we must have
b_{0}<\widetilde{k}
by Lemma
B.8
. By Lemma
B.6
we can write

\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}=\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}(s_{b_{0}}^{\otimes i_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{b_{0}}^{n}\otimes s_{b_{0}}^{\otimes k-1-i_{0}})\mathcal{P}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b}^{\prime},\underline{i}^{\prime}}^{n-1}

where
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma s_{i_{0}}
and thus

\delta^{\prime}=\delta_{i_{0}+1}\delta
.
We will show the statement for
k=0
,
\underline{i}=()
, where the LHS is
\id_{[n]}
by definition. Then
\widetilde{\underline{i}}=(0,1,\dots,\widetilde{k}-1)
and thus

\sgn(\underline{i}^{\vee})=1,\qquad\sgn(\widetilde{\underline{i}}^{\vee})=1,

\sigma:[e]\to[0]
is the contraction, and
\widetilde{\sigma}:[\widetilde{k}+e]\to[\widetilde{k}]

is
\id_{[{k}]}\ast^{\prime}\sigma
, hence,
\sgn(\sigma)=\sgn(\widetilde{\sigma})=1
. Furthermore

\Sz_{\widetilde{\underline{i}},\widetilde{\delta}}^{c_{0}}=\id_{[\widetilde{k}]}\qquad s_{\delta(0),\delta(1)}^{e+1}=\id_{[{e+1}]}.

By induction on
k
, we may assume that the statement is true for
k-1,n-1,e+1,\overline{i}^{\prime}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k-1})
, with
\widetilde{k}^{\prime}=\widetilde{k}-1
. hence

\mathcal{P}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b}^{\prime},\underline{i}^{\prime}}^{n-1}\equiv\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}}\\
\text{$\underline{i}^{\prime}$ is a $\underline{b}^{\prime}$-reduction of $\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}}$}\end{subarray}}\varepsilon(\Sz^{c_{0}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}\ast^{\prime}s_{\delta^{\prime}(0),\delta^{\prime}(1)}^{k+1+e})\otimes\cdots\otimes(\Sz^{c_{k-1}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}\ast^{\prime}s_{\delta^{\prime}(k-1),\delta^{\prime}(k)}^{k+1+e})

therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\sigma}\mathcal{H}_{\underline{b},\underline{i}}^{n}\equiv\sum_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}}}\varepsilon^{\prime}(s_{b_{0}}\Sz^{c_{0}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}\ast^{\prime}s_{\delta(0),\delta(1)}^{k+1+e})\otimes\cdots\otimes

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{P}^{\widetilde{k}+\delta(i_{0})}_{\alpha_{i_{0}},\alpha_{i_{0}+1}}\mathcal{H}_{b_{0}}^{n}(\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}\ast^{\prime}s_{\delta(i_{0}),\delta(i_{0}+2)}^{k+1+e})\right)

\displaystyle\otimes\cdots\otimes(s_{b_{0}}\Sz^{c_{k-1}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}\ast^{\prime}s_{\delta(k),\delta(k+1)}^{k+1+e}).

where

\mathcal{P}_{a,b}^{c}=\prod_{i=0}^{a-1}(\mathcal{P}_{c+i}\otimes 1)\prod_{i=0}^{b-1}(1\otimes\mathcal{P}_{c+a+i})

(and recall
\alpha_{j}=\delta(j+1)-\delta(j)
).

We will show

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{P}^{\widetilde{k}+\delta(i_{0})}_{\alpha_{i_{0}},\alpha_{i_{0}+1}}\mathcal{H}_{b_{0}}^{n}(\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}\ast^{\prime}s_{\delta(i_{0}),\delta(i_{0}+2)}^{k+e+1})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\underline{z}}\varepsilon_{\underline{z}}(\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}},\widetilde{\delta}}\ast^{\prime}s_{\delta(i_{0}),\delta(i_{0}+1)}^{k+e+1})\otimes(\Sz^{c_{i_{0}+1}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}},\widetilde{\delta}}\ast^{\prime}s_{\delta(i_{0}+1),\delta(i_{0}+2)}^{k+e+1})

(104)

with

\varepsilon_{\underline{z}}=(-1)^{\sum_{i<j}z_{j}(z_{i}+1)+\sum_{i}\alpha_{i_{0}}z_{i}}

where, in each summand, the extension
\widetilde{\underline{i}}
is determined by
\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}}
and

\underline{z}
as in Lemma
B.10
.
Equation (
104
) together with (
103
)
and case 3. of (
102
) in Lemma
B.10
achieve the induction step and produce a global sign of

\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}(-1)^{b_{i}-\widetilde{k}-i+1}.

On the other hand by (
86
)

\sgn(\sigma_{\underline{b}})=\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}(-1)^{b_{i}-(k-i-1)}

where
\sigma_{\underline{b}}:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[n-k]
. This yields a product of
(-1)^{(\widetilde{k}+k)k}=(-1)^{(\widetilde{k}+1)k}
.

It remains to show equation (
104
).
By Lemma
B.10
, we can write
\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}=\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}|_{[{b_{0}}]}\ast^{\prime}s
for a suitable degeneracy
s:[{\widetilde{k}-b_{0}-1}]\twoheadrightarrow[{x}]
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\widetilde{k}+\delta(i_{0})}_{\alpha_{i_{0}},\alpha_{i_{0}+1}}\mathcal{H}_{b_{0}}^{n}(\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}\ast^{\prime}s_{\delta(i_{0}),\delta(i_{0}+2)}^{k+1+e})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\id_{[{b_{0}}]}\ast\mathcal{P}^{\widetilde{k}-b_{0}-1+\delta(i_{0})}_{\alpha_{i_{0}},\alpha_{i_{0}+1}}\mathcal{H}^{n-b_{0}-1})s_{b_{0}}(\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}}}|_{[{b_{0}}]}\ast^{\prime}s\ast^{\prime}s_{\delta(i_{0}),\delta(i_{0}+2)}^{k+1+e})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\id_{[{b_{0}}]}\ast\mathcal{P}^{\widetilde{k}-b_{0}-1+\delta(i_{0})}_{\alpha_{i_{0}},\alpha_{i_{0}+1}}\mathcal{H}^{n-b_{0}-1})(\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}|_{[{b_{0}}]}\ast s\ast^{\prime}s_{\delta(i_{0}),\delta(i_{0}+2)}^{k+1+e})s_{\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}(c_{i_{0}}^{\prime})-x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\id_{[{b_{0}}]}\ast s\ast^{\prime}s_{\delta(i_{0}),\delta(i_{0}+2)}^{k+1+e})(\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}|_{[{b_{0}}]}\ast\mathcal{P}^{x}_{\alpha_{i_{0}},\alpha_{i_{0}+1}}\mathcal{H}^{x+\alpha_{i_{0}}+\alpha_{i_{0}+1}})s_{\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}(c_{i_{0}}^{\prime})-x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\underline{z}}(-1)^{\sum_{i<j}z_{i}(z_{j}+1)+a\sum z_{i}}

\displaystyle\cdot

\displaystyle(\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}|_{[{b_{0}}]}\ast^{\prime}sB_{\underline{z}}^{0}\ast^{\prime}\underbrace{s_{\delta(i_{0}),\delta(i_{0}+2)}^{k+1+e}s_{\alpha_{i_{0}},\alpha_{i_{0}}+\alpha_{i_{0}+1}}^{\alpha_{i_{0}}+\alpha_{i_{0}+1}}}_{s_{\delta(i_{0}),\delta(i_{0}+1)}^{k+1+e}})

\displaystyle\otimes

\displaystyle(\Sz^{c_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{\underline{i}^{\prime}},\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}}|_{[{b_{0}}]}\ast sB_{\underline{z}}^{1}\delta_{0}^{\alpha_{i_{0}}-1}\ast^{\prime}\underbrace{s_{\delta(i_{0}),\delta(i_{0}+2)}^{k+1+e}s_{0,\alpha_{i_{0}}}^{\alpha_{i_{0}}+\alpha_{i_{0}+1}}}_{s_{\delta(i_{0}+1),\delta(i_{0}+2)}^{k+1+e}})

using Lemma
5.49
, 2.
Thus equation (
104
) follows from cases 1. and 2. of (
102
) in Lemma
B.10
.∎

Appendix C
Decalage

This appendix contains a rather technical discussion of the compatibility of
\dec^{*}
(total decalage), in the Abelian case, with
\otimes
,
\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}
, and some (weak)
simplicial enrichments used throughout the text.
It should be consulted only if needed.

Recall the explicit formulæ for
\dec^{*}
and
\dec_{*}
in the Abelian case (Proposition
5.39
).

C.1
Symmetry

C.1.

Let
\sigma:\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}
be the morphism switching the factors. On the level of double complexes
\sigma^{*}
has the effect of switching the indices. Let
X
be a double complex with isomorphism

\alpha:\sigma^{*}X\cong X

We do not have an isomorphism
\dec\sigma\cong\dec
but certainly an isomorphism of
\Ab
-enriched pro-functors

\widetilde{\sigma}:\dec\,\sigma\cong C\,\dec\,\sigma\,\iota\cong C\dec\iota\cong\dec.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
X
be a symmetric double complex.
The composition of
\dec_{*}\alpha
with the mate of the morphism
L(\widetilde{\sigma})

\dec_{*}X\cong\dec_{*}\sigma^{*}X\cong\dec_{*}X

is given under the identification
r
(i.e. the identification described by Proposition
5.39
) as the morphism

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i+j=n}A_{i,j}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i+j=n}A_{i,j}

\displaystyle a_{i,j}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(-1)^{ij}\alpha(a_{i,j})

Proof.

It suffies to show that

\textstyle{D_{i}\boxtimes D_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{\can}}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{ij}}
\textstyle{\sigma^{*}(D_{j}\boxtimes D_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{\can}}
\textstyle{\dec^{*}(D_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L(\widetilde{\sigma})}
\textstyle{\dec^{*}\sigma^{*}(D_{n})}

commutes. Like in the proof of Proposition
5.42
, 2. it suffices to see that in degree
n
, we have

(-1)^{ij}c\dd_{r}s_{\can}=cL(\widetilde{\sigma})\dd_{r}s_{\can}.

Since
\dd_{r}s_{\can}=\id
, the right hand side maps
[\{0,\dots,i\}]\otimes[\{0,\dots,j\}]
to

[\{0,\dots,i+j+1\}]

and then applies the isomorphism
[i]\ast[j]\cong[j]\ast[i]
in
\FinSet
switching the factors. The left hand side morphism
maps it to

(-1)^{ij}[\{0,\dots,i+j+1\}]
, which is the same (cf. Lemma
5.6
for a description of the action).
∎

Lemma C.3
.

1.

The mate

\dec^{*}\dec_{*}\to\dec_{12,3,*}\dec_{1,23}^{*}

induced by (
L
applied to) the obvious isomorphism
\dec\,\dec_{12,3}\cong\dec\,\dec_{1,23}
is given by morphisms

\alpha_{n,m}:\prod_{i+j^{\prime}=n+m+1}A_{i,j^{\prime}}\oplus\prod_{i+j^{\prime}=n+m}A_{i,j^{\prime}}\to\prod_{i+j=n}\left(A_{i,j+m+1}\oplus A_{i,j+m}\right)

product of the identities

A_{i,j+m+1}\oplus A_{i,j+m}\to A_{i,j+m+1}\oplus A_{i,j+m}

over
i+j=n
.

2.

The mate

\dec^{*}\dec_{*}\to\dec_{2,13,*}\dec_{1,23}^{*}

induced by (
L
applied to) the obvious isomorphism
C\,\dec\,\dec_{2,13}\,\iota\cong C\,\dec\,\dec_{1,23}\,\iota
is given by morphisms

\alpha_{n,m}:\prod_{i+j^{\prime}=n+m+1}A_{i,j^{\prime}}\oplus\prod_{i+j^{\prime}=n+m}A_{i,j^{\prime}}\to\prod_{i+j=m}\left(A_{i,n+j+1}\oplus A_{i,n+j}\right)

product of

A_{i,j+n+1}\oplus A_{i,j+n}\to A_{i,n+j+1}\oplus A_{i,n+j}

\left(\begin{matrix}(-1)^{i(n+1)}&0\\
(-1)^{(i+1)n}\mathrm{d}_{l}&(-1)^{in}\end{matrix}\right)

over
i+j=m
.

3.

The mate

\dec^{*}\dec_{*}\to\dec_{13,24,*}\dec_{12,34}^{*}

induced by (
L
applied to) the obvious isomorphism
C\,\dec\,\dec_{13,24}\,\iota\cong C\,\dec\,\dec_{12,34}\,\iota
is given by morphisms

\displaystyle\alpha_{n,m}:\prod_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}=n+m+1}A_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\oplus\prod_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}=n+m}A_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\to\prod_{i+j=n}\prod_{k+l=m}\left(A_{i+k+1,j+l+1}\oplus A_{i+k+1,j+l}\oplus A_{i+k,j+l+1}\oplus A_{i+k,j+l}\right)

product of

\left(A_{i+k+1,j+l}\oplus A_{i+k,j+l+1}\right)\oplus A_{i+k,j+l}\to A_{i+k+1,j+l+1}\oplus A_{i+k+1,j+l}\oplus A_{i+k,j+l+1}\oplus A_{i+k,j+l}

\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&0\\
(-1)^{j(k+1)}&0&0\\
0&(-1)^{(j+1)k}&0\\
0&0&(-1)^{jk}\end{matrix}\right)

(with the left two factors from the first product) over
i+j=n,k+l=m

Proof.

The morphisms are in each case induced by a collection for
n+m=x

\alpha_{n,m}:\prod_{i+j^{\prime}=n+m}A_{i,j^{\prime}}\to\prod_{i+j=n}\left(A_{i,j+m+1}\oplus A_{i,j+m}\right)

maps
A_{i,j^{\prime}}
to the factor
A_{i,j+m}
on the right with
j^{\prime}:=j+m
.

\alpha_{n,m}:\prod_{i+j^{\prime}=n+m}A_{i,j^{\prime}}\to\prod_{i+j=m}\left(A_{i,n+j+1}\oplus A_{i,n+j}\right)

maps
A_{i,j^{\prime}}
to the factor
A_{i,j+n}
on the right with
j^{\prime}:=j+n
with sign
(-1)^{in}
.

\alpha_{n,m}:\prod_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}=n+m}A_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\to\prod_{i+j=n,k+l=m}\left(A_{i+k+1,j+l+1}\oplus A_{i+k,j+l+1}\oplus A_{i+k+1,j+l}\oplus A_{i+k,j+l}\right)

maps
A_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
to the factor
A_{i+k,j+l}
on the right with
i^{\prime}=i+k
and
j^{\prime}:=j+l
with sign
(-1)^{jk}
.

In each case, we get on

\prod_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}=n+m+1}A_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\oplus\prod_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}=n+m}A_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}

the map

where

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\mathrm{d}_{l}&0\\

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{matrix}(-1)^{i(n+1)}&0\\

\displaystyle\footnotesize\left(\begin{matrix}&&1&\\

C.2
\widetilde{\dec}^{*}
is simplicially enriched

C.4.

The functor
\dec^{*}:\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C})\to\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C}))
extends naturally to an
unbounded
functor (given by the same formula as in Proposition
5.39
, 2.)

\widetilde{\dec}^{*}:\Ch(\mathcal{C})\to\Ch(\Ch(\mathcal{C}))

and is left adjoint to
\tot^{\prod}
. For this functor, we have (by inspecting Lemma
C.3
, 2.) a canonical isomorphism

\widetilde{\dec}^{*}(A\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}B)\cong A\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\widetilde{\dec}^{*}B

where
A\in\Ch_{b}(\mathcal{C})
or
A\in\Ch_{b}(\Ab)
. On the right hand side the tensor is w.r.t. the second variable.
In particular

\widetilde{\dec}^{*}:\Ch(\mathcal{C})\to\Ch(\Ch(\mathcal{C}))

is simplicially enriched (w.r.t. the simplicial enrichment
\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}
) and so is the restriction
67
67

67

but
not
the restriction
\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C})\to\Ch_{\geq 0}(\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C}))
!
:

\widetilde{\dec}_{\geq 0}^{*}:\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C})\to\Ch(\Ch_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{C})).

Proposition C.5
.

On
\Hom
-complexes the enrichment of
\widetilde{\dec}^{*}
is explicitly given by:

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(X,Y)_{q}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\underline{\Hom}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}(\widetilde{\dec}^{*}X,\widetilde{\dec}^{*}Y)_{q}

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\left(\begin{matrix}f&\\
(-1)^{i+1}\partial f&f\end{matrix}\right)&\text{$q$ even}\\
\left(\begin{matrix}(-1)^{i+1}f&\\
\partial f&(-1)^{i}f\end{matrix}\right)&\text{$q$ odd}\end{cases}

where
\partial
is the differential in the
\Hom
-complex.

Proof.

In Lemma
C.3
, 2., working with unbounded complexes (in one direction) we have the morphism

\dec^{*}\dec_{*}\to\dec_{2,13,*}\dec_{1,23}^{*}

which is explicitly given by

\alpha_{n,m}:\prod_{i+j^{\prime}=n+m+1}A_{i,j^{\prime}}\oplus\prod_{i+j^{\prime}=n+m}A_{i,j^{\prime}}\to\prod_{i+j=m}\left(A_{i,n+j+1}\oplus A_{i,n+j}\right)

whose value at the pair at
i,j
is the value at the pair at
i,j^{\prime}=j+n
transformed by

\left(\begin{matrix}(-1)^{i(n+1)}&0\\
(-1)^{(i+1)n}\mathrm{d}_{l}&(-1)^{in}\end{matrix}\right)

It extends to unbounded complexes in the
j
-direction and has an inverse there,
given by

\left(\begin{matrix}(-1)^{i(n+1)}&0\\
(-1)^{(i+1)(n+1)}\mathrm{d}_{l}&(-1)^{in}\end{matrix}\right)

and letting
j=j^{\prime}-n
.

Now let

\kappa:\Delta_{q}^{\circ}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}B\to C

be a morphism determined up to degeneracies by
\kappa(\{0,\dots,q\}\otimes b_{i})=\widetilde{\kappa}(b_{i})
.
We have
\kappa\dd_{l}(\{0,\dots,q\}\otimes b_{i})=\partial(\widetilde{\kappa})(b_{i})
where
\partial
is the differential in the
\underline{\Hom}
-complex.

\dec^{*}\kappa
is the diagonal

\prod_{i+j^{\prime}=n+m+1}(\Delta_{q}^{\circ})_{i}\otimes B_{j^{\prime}}\oplus\prod_{i+j^{\prime}=n+m}(\Delta_{q}^{\circ})_{i}\otimes B_{j^{\prime}}\to C_{n+m+1}\oplus C_{n+m}

and the composition

\prod_{i+j=m}(\Delta_{q}^{\circ})_{i}\otimes(B_{n+j+1}\oplus B_{n+j})\to\prod_{i+j^{\prime}=n+m+1}(\Delta_{q}^{\circ})_{i}\otimes B_{j^{\prime}}\oplus\prod_{i+j^{\prime}=n+m}(\Delta_{q}^{\circ})_{i}\otimes B_{j^{\prime}}\to C_{n+m+1}\oplus C_{n+m}

maps
\{0,\dots,q\}\otimes(b_{m+j+1},b_{m+j})
for
i=q
to

\left(\begin{matrix}(-1)^{q(n+1)}\widetilde{\kappa}&0\\
(-1)^{(q+1)(n+1)}\partial(\widetilde{\kappa})&(-1)^{qn}\widetilde{\kappa}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}b_{m+j+1}\\
b_{m+j}\end{matrix}\right)

This gives the formula stated in the Proposition.
∎

Notice that this enrichment does not work properly in the bounded case. However, see (
C.6
) for a workaround.

C.3
Compatibility with
\otimes

\dec^{*}
is canonically monoidal for
\otimes
because the latter is computed point-wise interpreting source and destination as (bi)simplicial diagrams.
We denote the corresponding functor as

\dec^{*}:(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)\to(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)

(the notation
\dec^{*}_{\otimes}
being reserved for its composition with the Alexander-Whitney map in the second variable).

C.4
Compatibility with
\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}

Recall the adjunction

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec^{*}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}}
\scriptstyle{\dec_{*}}

where
\dec_{*}\cong\tot
interpreting the objects as complexes, and double complexes, respectively (cf. Proposition
5.39
, 1.).
It actually induces an adjunction between cooperads

\textstyle{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec^{*}_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\dec_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}},*}}

as follows, where the monoidal product
\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}
on the right hand side is given by

\dec_{13,24,*}-\boxtimes-

which can also be seen with the interpretation
\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}=(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}})^{\Delta^{\op}}
as the usual
\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}
-product

A\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}B=\dec_{*}A\boxtimes B

where
\boxtimes
is the exterior product
(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}})^{\Delta^{\op}}\times(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}})^{\Delta^{\op}}\to(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}})^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}
applying (the internal)

\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}
point-wise.

The functor
\dec_{*}
is actually monoidal by means of the isomorphism:

\dec_{*}(A\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}B)=\dec_{*}\dec_{13,24,*}A\boxtimes B\ \cong\ \dec_{*}\dec_{12,34,*}A\boxtimes B=(\dec_{*}A)\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}(\dec_{*}B)

where the middle isomorphism is induced by the isomorphism

\dec\dec_{13,24}\cong C\dec\dec_{13,24}\iota\cong C\dec\dec_{12,34}\iota\cong\dec\dec_{12,34}

of
\Ab
-enriched pro-functors. It follows from Lemma
C.2
, that explicitly, for a double complexes
A
,
B
:

\displaystyle\tot(A\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}B)=\bigoplus_{i,j,k,l}A_{i,j}\otimes B_{k,l}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle(\tot A)\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}(\tot B)=\bigoplus_{i,j,k,l}A_{i,j}\otimes B_{k,l}

\displaystyle a_{i,j}\otimes b_{k,l}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(-1)^{jk}a_{i,j}\otimes b_{k,l}

The left adjoint
\dec^{*}
is automatically lax monoidal (hence a functor of cooperads) via the mate

\dec^{*}(A\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}B)=\dec^{*}\dec_{*}A\boxtimes B\ \to\ \dec_{13,24,*}\dec_{12,34}^{*}A\boxtimes B=\dec^{*}A\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\dec^{*}B

and induces an adjunction between the categories of coalgebras. An explicit formula of the mate is given in Lemma
C.3
, 3.
We denote the corresponding functors of cooperads (and of coalgebras) by
\dec_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}^{*}
, and
\dec_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}},*}
, respectively.

Finally, in the corresponding adjunction

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}}_{\mathrm{/1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec^{*}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}}_{\mathrm{/1}}}
\scriptstyle{\dec_{*}}

between augmented objects, both functors commute with the forgetful functors forgetting the augmentation. For
\dec^{*}
this is clear, and for
\dec_{*}
it follows because the latter is computed by a connected finite limit (Proposition
4.89
).
Hence we will use them on augmented objects without further distinction.

C.5
More adjoints

We also have the formula

\dec_{*}=\int^{n}\mathbb{Z}[\Delta_{n}]\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X_{n,\bullet}

which comes from the tautological formula (Yoneda):

X=\int^{n}\Delta_{n}\boxtimes X_{n,\bullet}

and the fact that
\dec_{*}
obviously commutes with colimits (as one can see from the explicit formula
\dec_{*}\cong\tot
, cf. Proposition
5.39
).
This implies that
\dec_{*}
has a further right adjoint given by

(\dec^{?}X)_{[n],\bullet}=\mathcal{HOM}(\mathbb{Z}[\Delta_{n}],X)_{\bullet},

or also (modulo degeneracies)

(\dec^{?}X)_{n,\bullet}=\mathcal{HOM}(D_{n},X)_{\bullet},

which is similar to
\dec^{*}
except in low degrees:

\textstyle{\ddots}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{4}\oplus X_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{3}\oplus X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{3}\oplus X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{2}\oplus X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{0}}

Lemma C.9
.

There is a natural transformation

\pr_{2}^{*}\Rightarrow\dec^{?}

(adjoint of
\dec_{*}\pr_{2}^{*}\cong\id
whose inverse is induced by
\dec^{*}\Rightarrow\pr_{2}^{*}
. )
which is a row-wise quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

This can be seen directly by inspection or by the argument of Lemma
4.49
using that
\Delta_{1}\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}X\to X
is a quasi-isomorphism and hence by associativity
\mathcal{HOM}^{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}
maps the
homotopy equivalence (w.r.t.
\otimes
and hence also w.r.t.
\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}
)
\mathbb{Z}[\Delta_{n}]\to\mathbb{Z}[\Delta_{0}]
to a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

We have a functor of cooperads

\pr^{*}_{2,\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}:(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}\to((\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee})^{(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}}\cong(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}

which comes from the functoriality of the Day-convolution w.r.t. the projection
(\Delta,\ast)^{\op}\to\text{\footnotesize$\mathcal{O}$}^{\op}
. Unraveling the definition it boils down to the obvious morphism

\pr_{2}^{*}(X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Y)\to\pr_{2}^{*}X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\pr_{2}^{*}Y

noticing that, in the complexes viewpoint,
\pr_{2}^{*}X
places
X
in horizontal degree 0 and is zero other-wise. Since this is an isomorphism, we also have a morphism
between the corresponding operads:

\pr^{*}_{2,\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}:(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})\to(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}).

On the other hand, in
C.4
, we have enhanced
\dec_{*}
to a morphism of cooperads:

\dec_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}},*}:(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}\to(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}

Being monoidal, it is actually also a morphism of the corresponding operads, and induces a right adjoint

\dec_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}^{?}:(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})\to(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})

Lemma C.10
.

The natural transformation from Lemma
C.9
is actually a natural transformation between functors of operads:

\pr^{*}_{2,\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}\Rightarrow\dec_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}^{?}

Proof.

This boils down to the commutativity of

\textstyle{\pr_{2}^{*}(X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pr_{2}^{*}X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\pr_{2}^{*}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dec^{?}(X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dec^{?}X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\dec^{?}Y}

or equivalently to the commutativity of

\textstyle{\dec_{*}\pr_{2}^{*}(X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\dec_{*}(\pr_{2}^{*}X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\pr_{2}^{*}Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\sigma}}
\textstyle{X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\dec_{*}\pr_{2}^{*}X\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}\dec_{*}\pr_{2}^{*}Y}

which we leave to the reader to verify.
∎

C.6
Further compatibilities

Recall the enhancements of
\dec^{*}
to a functor of cooperads (which is monoidal, cf. also
4.29
)

\dec^{*}:(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}

and (cf.
C.4
)

\dec^{*}_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}:(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}

Lemma C.11
.

The following is commutative:

\textstyle{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec^{*}_{\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{EZ}}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\mathbin{\tilde{\otimes}})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathfrak{EZ},\mathfrak{EZ})}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dec^{*}}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\op}\times\Delta^{\op}},\otimes)^{\vee}}

Remark C.12
.

The corresponding square with the Alexander-Whitney-maps is not commutative!

Proof.

Need to show the commutativity of

\textstyle{\dec^{*}\dec_{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Ez}
\textstyle{\dec_{13,24,*}\dec_{12,34}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\Ez,\Ez)}
\textstyle{\dec^{*}\delta^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\delta_{13,24}^{*}\dec^{*}_{12,34}}

which is adjoint to

\textstyle{\dec_{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{unit}\circ\Ez}
\textstyle{\dec_{*}\dec_{13,24,*}\dec_{12,34}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\Ez,\Ez)}
\textstyle{\dec_{*}\dec^{*}\delta^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dec_{*}\delta_{13,24}^{*}\dec^{*}_{12,34}}

which is in standard form (
2.14
)
and thus it suffices to see that

\textstyle{\dec^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mlq c^{\op}\mrq,\mlq c^{\op}\mrq)\dec^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\dec^{*}\mlq c^{op}\mrq}
\textstyle{\delta_{13,24}^{*}\dec_{13,24}^{*}\dec^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{\dec^{*}\delta^{*}\dec^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\delta_{13,24}^{*}\dec_{12,34}^{*}\dec^{*}}

commutes.
Using the commutativity of
C
with
\dec^{*}
(Lemma
5.17
), this boils down to the (trivial) commutativity of the following diagram of functors

\FinSet^{2}\to\FinSet
:
